Self-as-an-End · The Second Critique

SAE: The Second Critique
SAE 第二批判

Han Qin (秦汉) · Independent Researcher · 2026
DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.23250387 · Full PDF on Zenodo · CC BY 4.0

Opening

By what does a law bind the one who made it?

This framework has said "cannot-not" many times. Some of those sayings claim too much, as if there were no conditions and no options. Some say the cannot-not is derived from the sole axiom. Some load several meanings onto a single word. This book lays them out and examines them: which kind each is, by what it binds, how far, where its cost lies, and how it lives alongside the cannot-nots of others.

It is our own words that are examined. The heaviest cuts fall on the earliest of them, the ones we said with the most force.

The text under examination is SAE's; what is to be seen is how SAE examines itself: which of its sentences hold, and on what they hold; and where those that do not hold fall back to.

This book does not legislate for the reader. The reader will find no "ought" here addressed to him; what he will find is where each law stands. Each of the book's own laws stands under interrogation too. The First Critique said in its Opening that a philosophy, once it stands, "stands under the interrogation of others." So does this one.

Table of Contents

Rules of the House

This book has set itself a few laws of writing, recorded here. Only when the law is in the open does interrogation have a handle. The first six continue those of the First Critique; the other nine are this book's own additions.

One. Two voices. External objects — Kant, other philosophies — are treated in an objective voice; the examination of this framework's own words is in the first person; commitments and retractions are signed "we."

Two. No backstage. The text does not explain its own style, announce its chapters, or narrate its writing process. Sources go in the footnotes; each term is explained on the spot where it first appears.

Three. No self-praise. The book issues itself no certificates of virtue; it only states structures, states costs, and keeps its commitments.

Four. Metaphor carries no definitions. Every term is defined first in strict wording; metaphor may only annotate what has already been defined.

Five. Chapters. Each chapter of the text stands as a chapter in its own right; "this chapter" refers to the present chapter, "this book" to the whole, and cross-references use chapter titles or chapter numbers.

Six. Registration and quotation. Every commitment is registered with its content, status, source of warrant, opponent, and branch; every quotation from the corpus is checked against the cited text, word for word; revision candidates and readings of the corpus each have their own ledger — the revision queue, the reading registry, the annex ledger — set out in Chapter Five, Section 4.

Seven. Two kinds of sentence. This book's sentences are of two kinds. Recording sentences record as written, audit, and register the text under examination. Law sentences state the laws of this branch that we keep in the name of "we." A law sentence is law only for those who keep it; it is not laid on the reader. This rule, too, is a recording sentence.

Eight. Definitions, not standards. A definition says what a word means here; it is not a yardstick for measuring people. Sentences such as "that is simply how high the bar is" this book does not say.

Nine. Rule out; never rule in. From outside, one can rule that a person is not, at this moment, operating at 15DD; one cannot rule that he is. A ruling-out needs something solid: some necessary condition of this particular operation, together with its failure to be met, independently verified. Where only general traces of risk are seen, what one has is a direction report. Both can be withdrawn. There is no absorbing state: a ruling is not a settlement, and there is no state from which, once ruled, there is no way back.

Ten. Diagnosis reads structure, not the heart. A third-person diagnosis reads only the shapes of acts, accounts, channels and precautions; first-person claiming belongs to the person himself, and is not written for him from outside.

Eleven. Levels record operations only. A level records one operation, by moment and by domain; it does not record a person. This book does not name anyone by level, in praise or in blame.

Twelve. Wording. The words "acknowledge" and "recognize," with their nouns, appear only inside quotations. In their place stand two words. Concession (认) is what an act aimed at another's will hands over in its very performance; adoption (承) is setting non dubito as a premise of one's own law. Non dubito is the default that asks for no proof. Besides quotations, the titles of cited works and explicit discussion of a source's own terms keep the source's wording.

Thirteen. Two oughts. The lateral ought — an ought one person issues to another — this book does not discuss. The inside-out ought is the cannot-not.

Fourteen. From outside in. What the text under examination says it has handed over, and where it says it has sent it, is recorded as written and audited; it is not adopted as this book's debt. This book does not fill in its entries, does not bring them into line, and does not choose among them on its behalf.

Fifteen. Quotation marks. Words in quotation marks with a source noted are the cited text's own words; words in quotation marks without a source are hypothetical utterances or claims under test, not quotations. This book's own terms are not put in quotation marks. Direct quotations from Kant are this book's translation (Appendix IV); passages of Kant that reach us through the SAE corpus keep the cited paper's wording, and their notes say so.

If the reader catches this book breaking these laws, that is precisely the use these laws were made for.

Chapter One · Introduction

1 · What We Are Asking

The question Kant set for the Critique of Practical Reason was whether pure reason can be practical on its own — whether, borrowing no empirical motive, it can determine the will by itself alone. His answer was not a deduction. The consciousness of the moral law he called "the fact of reason" (Faktum der Vernunft), and not an empirical fact; the objective reality of this law, he said plainly, no deduction can prove; it stands firm on its own.[^c1-kant1] His first Critique had laid down a priori conditions for cognition; here, on the practical side, Kant starts from this fact and from it establishes freedom.

SAE's Second Critique inherits Kant's move — turning a capacity around to examine its own validity — and not his conclusions. What it examines is not pure practical reason but the law a person gives himself. Its question is therefore parallel to Kant's in form, and lands somewhere else in content:

A law a person gives himself has no outside guarantor, issues no orders to anyone else, and can be revised by its maker at any time: by what does its "cannot-not" bind the lawgiver himself, how far does that binding reach, and what is its relation to the cannot-nots that others each have?

By what, how far, in relation to others: entitlement, boundary, universality. The three words come from this framework's Action Theory. That series spent nine papers on how a person acts on a cannot-not, and when it was done it said plainly that it does not prove whether these cannot-nots ought to be so: a cannot-not, "with its standing, its boundaries and its relation to universality, is the business of the Second Critique."[^c1-act0] Action Theory handed itself over for examination, and that is what this book takes up.

Inside this question lies a difficulty Kant saw: if the one who binds and the one who is bound are the same person, how is the binding anything more than an intention that can be withdrawn at any moment?[^c1-kant2] In SAE the difficulty is sharper, because the lawgiver's door to revising his law always stands open.[^c1-v2] So the first opponent this question meets — by what does the law bind? — is not the skeptic. It is the lawgiver's own power to revise.

The gap here has to be divided first. Revising is a change with the books kept: one can say what one held before and why one now changes it; the stretch the old law governed still counts as governed by it — which does not mean that stretch cannot be judged again later — and the new law runs from the moment of the change. Publicly renouncing a law that was wrong is also revising. Dropping is quietly setting a law down when that is convenient: no books are kept, and the old law simply stops counting. A person who holds fast to his own law may be showing autonomy, or obsession; and besides these two there is a third shape, which for now we call fickleness — stopping whenever stopping is convenient, changing the law without keeping the books on the change. How the three are told apart can only be made clear later. Here one division is made first: revising is not dropping.

Then there is a question that falls back on our own heads. This critique is, in its turn, one of the framework's self-given laws: by what right would it be exempt from the same examination? The answer is the one the First Critique gave at its outset: no exemption. In this book that answer has to be made good in three places.

The first place. This framework has said: "Cannot not legislate only for oneself."[^c1-a16lemma] Yet that very sentence is said to other people. The paper that first said it, One's Own Law, already ran into this, and its answer about the sentence's immunity was: "Not a logically absolute immunity, but a pragmatic one: whoever takes it and legislates for others immediately knows they are overstepping."[^c1-a16immune] It places itself the same way: "This is not the morality chapter of SAE. It is SAE's anti-ethics chapter." And: "It is a bulkhead, not a door." We take this answer over as it is, and push it no further. What a bulkhead cannot hold back, the paper's own close shows: "everyone should be their own philosopher-king."[^c1-a16self]

The second place. This book does not legislate for the reader. It tells no one what to do; it only sets a law out in view: where it stands, what it rests on, where its cost lies. Every "cannot-not" in this book is either something we say to ourselves or hangs on a condition: if someone stands here, then for him … This way of working has a cost, stated up front: this book cannot, on its own authority, tell anyone how to act. A reader who hopes to carry off from here an ethics for legislating on others' behalf will not find one. This too is only a bulkhead. What we can do is make every sentence able to bear one question: is this issuing an ought to someone?

The third place. There is an earlier paper in this framework that called itself a critique: One's Own Law, whose full title is One's Own Law: The SAE Critique of Ethics and Morality, written before the First Critique. This book reads it as a forerunner, and also as something under examination: a forerunner is not spared examination because it calls itself a critique, and this book is not spared because it comes later. Where it holds, we take it as it stands — the imperative understood "as direction rather than rigid rule"; "Evil is not a positional identity; it is the direction of regression and suppression"; asked what SAE's ethics is, "the answer is one word: none."[^c1-a16keep] The next section takes apart some of its other sentences.

In examining, we look from outside. Some papers in the framework write that a given question "goes to the Second Critique." That is the paper's own arrangement for itself. We examine what it wrote; we do not finish its sentences for it, and we do not take its arrangement as work this book is bound to take on. The text under examination has its own voice, and the examiner does not speak in its place.

One more thing must be made clear at the start: the same word "ought" may say two different things. One is my using my law to make demands of you — the lateral ought. SAE does not discuss it: a person's law going beyond his own boundary to make demands of others is exactly what One's Own Law calls "ethics," and sets out to dismantle. The other is a person's being unable, within his own law, to do otherwise — the inside-out ought, which is the cannot-not. This book examines the second: the cannot-nots stated across the framework, which kind each is, by what it binds, and how far.

2 · Dismantling Three Things Mistaken for Necessities

Around the practical cannot-not, this framework made a set of necessity-claims: it is not a choice, there is no option, there is no condition; it follows from the sole axiom; it is a force that pushes a person on. We once said exactly this. Now take this set of "necessities," one by one, to the edge of the knife, and see which of them are necessities at all. Everything taken apart here is our own.

First: the practical cannot-not is not free of conditions, and not without options

We wrote sentences like these: "When a subject reaches 15DD, they 'cannot not oppose the suppression of any subject's remainder.' This is not a choice, not nobility — it is a mode of being." "When your DD level arrives there, you have no option of 'not opposing.'" This law is "unconditional (no precondition can cause it to lapse)."[^c1-a16hard][^c1-dd] 15DD is one level in this framework's sequence of levels: the level at which a person takes not only himself but others as ends. The remainder is the part a single demarcation fails to exhaust. Reason Cannot Not put it more heavily still: this cannot-not is not a hypothetical of the kind "if you want to be a rational subject, you must maintain rationalizing operation"; a subject who stops is "swallowed by the 14DD instrumental structure and reduced to a tool"; "To exit is to be degraded"; "reason's legislative motion itself cannot stop."[^c1-reason1]

These sentences do not survive one simple question: that "no option of 'not opposing'" — was it proved, or asserted? One's Own Law also writes: "Alignment is moral; deviation is immoral."[^c1-a16dev] In its own pages, deviation is possible.

Imagine a person who stands in the position of taking both himself and others as ends, and who then leaves it in one matter: for the sake of his own review this year, he regards the other team as people it would be all right to keep in the dark, changes the number, and says nothing. Is there a contradiction in this picture? None. Action Theory writes this scene precisely. First it sets a limit: "whether he changed the number or spoke up does not settle it on its own" — he might be afraid, or held down. Then it says: "If he really does leave in this way, this series does not judge him; in this one matter, this cannot-not is no longer there for him, and that is all; this series still speaks to him. Leaving goes matter by matter: having left in this one matter does not mean he is from then on no longer a person who treats people as ends." Only one thing cannot be done: "saying 'I am a person who treats people as ends' while also saying that it is all right to keep the other team in the dark. That cannot be done, not because anyone forbids it, but because those two statements cannot sit together in one person."[^c1-act1leave]

So here the claim must be lowered a grade. What comes down is the claim of no condition and no option. What remains is a cannot-not that hangs on conditions, and it has two layers: "The shared layer is mostly about how people are regarded, and cannot be cancelled while one stands in the position of taking oneself and others as ends; a person can leave this position in a given matter, but cannot stand in it and cancel it at once. The case layer is mostly about what to do this time; it is the person's own judgment, can be questioned, can be revised, and may be obstructed in the doing."[^c1-d5] For one who stands in that position, some things cannot be cancelled; the position can be left, matter by matter; and the cost of leaving goes on the account of the one who leaves. What is spoken of here is the binding force of his law upon him; what he did to the other team is not settled by this account.

That cost must be stated exactly. To say "reduced to a tool," to say "To exit is to be degraded," is to state the cost as a loss of status, and to overstate it. The First Critique has already said how large this cost is: "a subject who halts this operation no longer realizes its being-an-end through this operation — that is all; this cancels neither its normative standing as an end, nor licenses anyone to treat it merely as a means" — and noted, in its own words, that "the strict argument for this layer belongs to the practical critique," which is to say, to this book.[^c1-fc1] Paper 4 says it more directly: "Within the domain of human objects this paper undertakes, impaired capacity, coerced change, or the absence of others' recognition does not constitute a ground for revoking standing as an end." That sentence rests on a postulate Paper 4 openly undertakes: a human being is an end in itself.[^c1-p4guard] A postulate is an assumption that someone openly undertakes and that can be refused (Chapter Four, postulate region). One who does not accept this postulate can still say "To exit is to be degraded"; that is another branch — a different law, with its conditions and its costs — which Chapter Five examines again. Seen from this postulate, what the one who leaves loses is the realization of that law for him in this one matter; his standing as an end is not cancelled by this one leaving.

At the same place a force must also come down. We wrote that the remainder "cannot not have an external outlet (avoiding internal collapse); the only legitimate outlet is another 15DD subject"; that interrogation is "the engine of 16DD"; that when two people's separate purposes meet, the thing they do together is "the course of action forced into being once two separate purposes meet."[^c1-engine] Each of these sentences hides a motor: the remainder pushes a person along, the outlet forces him toward others, the meeting forces out a joint course of action. On the cognitive side, the First Critique has already taken this motor apart: "The remainder is only a gap … by itself it pushes nothing."[^c1-fc2] A remainder shows where the debt lies, and with that, where a direction issues from; no one may rewrite that at will. Whether to walk, and which of several places to take first, rests with the one who walks.[^c1-fc13] As for the thing two people do together: by Action Theory's own division it is "what to do this time," the judgment of the two — "Reasons do not turn into action by themselves"[^c1-d7] — and to call it "forced into being" is to turn a judgment into a force. The two may well feel they cannot do otherwise; that is still their judgment.

The First Critique also left one such place of its own. It called the practical cannot-not "the inner drive of SAE," and stated that this layer goes to the Second Critique.[^c1-fc3] On the cognitive side it had withdrawn every claim of the same kind — the compulsion vocabulary "takes the lowered sense" — but this one it left as it was. We do not change a word of it; we only say how it is to be read: "drive" here reads as a condition — the condition for going on, not a push from behind. The First Critique has a sentence of its own that supports this reading: "able to stop, and yet unceasing — that is autonomy; unable to stop, and unceasing — that is only a machine turning."[^c1-fc14] Removing the motor does not remove the walker: when a person walks on by his own law, the strength is his. The rule against exemption applies to earlier texts as well.

With this taken down, a tension is on the table. One's Own Law says "This is not a choice"; Action Theory speaks of the cannot-not "as a person's own choice." The first has been taken down. Where the "choice" in the second falls — on seeing, or on setting what is seen as a premise of one's own law — is the first case this book has to examine, and it is left for Chapter Five.

Second: the law is not deduced from the sole axiom

With the unconditional taken down, a more solemn claim remains: that the practical law is derived from the sole axiom. Moral Law Paper 1, explaining its use of the word "theorem," writes: "SAE has only one axiom—negativa. All content in this series is deduced from this single axiom's specific unfolding in multi-Self situations." The same section says the four theorems "can themselves be proven—deduced from one's own law and the multi-Self situation." Rights Theory, too, speaks of Negativa as that "from which autonomy and the theorems of the moral law all follow as lemmas and theorems."[^c1-deduce]

In this framework, Negativa names the act of demarcation: parting "this" from "not-this." The First Critique has already withdrawn the claim that everything is derived from it. Imagine a minimal system containing only negation: it flips any proposition into its opposite, and flips it back. Negation exists in it, cannot be denied, and can be applied again and again; yet it generates no remainder, no reflexivity, no forward-growing sequence — "Negativa does not entail all things."[^c1-fc4] Methodology 0 (second edition) puts the withdrawal in so many words: "The sole axiom of SAE, across all its series: Negativa. Everything else is a postulate or theorem within its series."[^c1-m0]

What, then, are Moral Law's four? Moral Law gives them this status: "They are constitutive conditions of 15DD operation." Moral Law has taken this sentence both ways. On one side, it says the four are a description of structure, which "can be discovered, articulated, inspected"; on the other, it says that one who violates them "is, in that moment, not operating at 15DD" — every counterexample is placed outside 15DD, so the sentence cannot be tested, and in use it works like a definition.[^c1-ml24] Nor are the four alike. The first, to "recognize the specific other as End," says the same thing as the Methodology Overview's definition of 15DD; it is analytically true of 15DD, and to state it is to restate the definition, which cannot be offered as evidence. The other three carry more than that definition does. Read as definition, they mean Moral Law has enlarged the definition of 15DD, and it owes an account of where the enlargement comes from; read as substantive claims, each needs its own warrant. Either reading comes to the same outcome: they are not derived from Negativa. Here the one word "theorem" is carrying two things. The First Critique met the same thing: that time, what "theorem" carried was a set of replaceable postulates; this time, what it carries includes at least one definition. So when this book cites the four, it says of each what it is — part of a definition, or an assumption that series undertakes on its own account — and no longer calls them theorems of Negativa.

Third: last, taking apart a family of sayings we have long run together — "cannot-not"

This dismantling looks only at the inside-out kind. Every inside-out "ought" in this framework's published texts is a cannot-not, so all of them belong here; the lateral ought, which SAE does not discuss, does not. The question is which kind each of these cannot-nots is.

In this framework's published texts, sayings of this inside-out kind come in at least nine forms. Some are called cannot-not, some go by near-synonyms of it (the Chinese has 不得不, 不能不, 无法不); some are called duty; some, direction:

How it is put The sentences
No condition, no optionOne's Own Law: "This is not a choice, not nobility — it is a mode of being"; "you have no option of 'not opposing'"; "unconditional (no precondition can cause it to lapse)"
Ontological; expressly refuses the hypotheticalReason Cannot Not: not "if you want to be a rational subject, you must maintain rationalizing operation" (14DD hypothetical); "To exit is to be degraded"
Constitutive condition; exit possibleMoral Law: "Once a Self operates at 15DD, it cannot-not be so; not being so is not operating at 15DD."
Hung on a position; can be left matter by matterAction Theory: "For someone standing in this position, some things cannot be cancelled"; by its own account, "The Conflict Treatise has used a statement of this shape" — the Conflict Treatise: "So long as I go on handling, from the first-person position, a conflict that has passed the gates, and go on holding that my own law may not be written for me by the conflict, then … are conditions this handling position cannot itself cancel"; "I may stop this operation"
Inward-facing dutyRights Theory: "SAE's one duty faces inward"; Meaning Theory: "A person has only the duty to keep his own law; there is no lateral duty"
Structural cannot-not; a direction that emerges of its own accordMethodology 00: "The 'must' in 'must recognize' is structural 'cannot not,' not ethical-imperative 'should'"; To Forget the Self: "not a moral command but the direction that naturally emerges after completing observation and illumination"
Survives scrutiny; outside opposition cannot put it outLearning series: "persists after sustained 13DD scrutiny"; "not only unextinguished but further confirmed under external opposition"
The other's purpose enters my constraintDimensional Sentence-Form Theory: "The other's purpose is A, therefore I cannot not do B"; "I for purpose A, the other for purpose B, we cannot not do C"
Double negationMethodology Overview: "Categorical imperatives necessarily take the form of double negation — 'cannot not X.'"; "Non dubito is cannot-not-chisel"

[^c1-family]

The nine differ in more than wording. Some hang on no condition, some on a definition, some on a position. Some allow no exit; with some, to exit is simply to be outside that level; some can be left matter by matter; some can be stopped. Some say outright that they are duties; some say outright that they are not commands. Some count only if they survive scrutiny; in some, another's purpose has entered my constraint; some can be said only in a double negative. When one family of sayings carries nine things at once, force can be borrowed from one to another: what cannot be cancelled within a position is passed off as a necessity no one can escape; or what holds analytically of a position is made to serve as a constraint with weight for anyone at all. Whether it has in fact been borrowed depends on how each passage actually argues.

The method is the one the First Critique used on "conservation": part them one by one, return each to its place, and keep whatever holds. The first two — no condition and no option, and exit as degradation — have been taken down above; whether the others hold, and where they stand, is examined later, one kind at a time. Only once they are parted can their relations be asked about clearly. Are the one hung on a definition and the one hung on a position two ways of writing one thing, or two things? Are the Conflict Treatise's "may stop" and Action Theory's "can leave" the same? Is the inward duty the same constraint as the cannot-not of a position, under another name?

One point is worth stating now. Both the one hung on a definition and the one hung on a position have a layer that holds analytically of that position. Action Theory says of its own cannot-not that it "resembles the analytic proposition that whoever wills the end wills the means, and the difference lies in the antecedent"; Reason Cannot Not says it is precisely the cost of exiting that "transforms clause I from 'A=A' analytic redundancy into the absolute existential tension" — and that cost has been withdrawn above.[^c1-analytic] The weight does not lie in the analytic layer; it lies in whether a person steps into that position. That is the adoption spoken of below.

After the dismantling

Three things taken down, and the practical side's story of "necessity everywhere" has largely collapsed. That the cannot-not has no condition and no option — lowered: it hangs on a position, can be left matter by matter, and the cost of leaving goes on the leaver's own account; it is not a loss of status. That the law is deduced from the sole axiom — withdrawn: Moral Law's four are not theorems of Negativa, and what each is is stated item by item; the rest are postulates each series undertakes for itself, together with what is derived from those postulates. The "cannot-not" family comes in at least nine forms — taken apart: each returned to its place, to be examined one kind at a time.

What remains is this critique's question: a cannot-not that hangs on a position, can be left, and has no outside warrant — by what does it bind the one who stands in that position? The shape of the answer can be given now. Entitlement comes from adoption: a person takes up "others are ends too" and sets it as a premise of his own law — without testing whether the other deserves it, simply in non dubito — and this step can be refused. The boundary is marked by cost. Cost is the part that a cut leaves on the lawgiver's own account and that has not been taken in: it stands on his own account; it is not a consequence, and it is not compared with anyone else's; it is not a fine this book levies on anyone, still less something that buys a license to harm others. Universality comes from the overlap between laws — something that is the same in two people's separate laws — and from traces that can be read from outside: others cannot see a person's law, but they can read where he has locked shut others' ways of correcting or leaving, or, after being interrogated, has closed the door on his own review. The first, once verified, supports a ruling-out; the second is a direction report. Cost is treated in detail in Chapter Two; overlap and ruling-out in Chapter Six. The whole book's normative grammar fits in one sentence: no coercion — only the cost, stated. The sentence has a prototype in the First Critique: speaking of being interrogated, it takes it in a lowered sense: "not enforced, but priced — violation has its cost."[^c1-fc5]

3 · With Kant: The Divide Is Not "Inner"

An easy formula says: Kant's moral law is universal, SAE's law is inner. The formula is inexact. Kant himself held that ethical legislation cannot come from outside;[^c1-kant3] "inner" is not where the divide lies. The real divide lies at two places, and at a third the two sides meet.

The first is the step to universal law. At its close, One's Own Law criticizes Kant: "Kant's autonomy originally meant exactly this — legislating for oneself. But after legislating, he universalized it … Autonomy reverted to heteronomy."[^c1-a16end] The First Critique later put it more exactly: in Kant, "the form of universal law is not a test appended to self-legislation but a constitutive condition of autonomy" — what fails the test does not issue from autonomy at all. What SAE changes is this step: "withdrawing that test's exclusive validity is a redefinition of autonomy, not a mere loosening of content: self-given laws of different content ('do not construct' is also a law) are not judged wrong, but registered as different branches, each with its conditions and its costs."[^c1-fc6]

The second is duties of right. Kant ties right to the authorization to coerce.[^c1-kant4] SAE takes on no duties of right: Rights Theory deletes the old rule "Every right corresponds to a duty," and what remains is a single sentence — "SAE's one duty faces inward."[^c1-rights1]

The third is duties of virtue toward others, and here the two sides half meet. Kant makes the happiness of others an end that is at the same time a duty;[^c1-kant7] Rights Theory's formula is: "the constraint faces inward, and inward the law already includes the other."[^c1-rights2] The meeting goes this far and no further: the constraint issues from one's own legislation, its content includes others, and others cannot coerce me. Kant's duty holds for every rational being, and his duty of respect also gives every person a rightful claim on others' respect. SAE takes on neither. There is only one duty, facing one's own law; the content of that law already includes others, but others cannot on that ground demand anything of me.

Action Theory set out, point by point, where it leaves Kant; three of those points matter most.[^c1-act0kant] On the source: "In this series, one's own law governs only oneself"; "To say that one's own law is Kant's autonomy would be a mistake." On sentence form, its cannot-not "is not a hypothetical imperative"; "Nor is it a categorical imperative." On judgment: "This series gives neither: it gives judgment no rule, and it does not check maxims by a test of universalization."

Two more places are better called contrasts than divides.

In his second Critique, Kant takes the consciousness of the moral law as a fact of reason, and seeks no deduction for it. At the same place, this book posits no fact. "A human being is an end in itself" is, in Paper 4, a postulate openly undertaken; it can be refused, and whoever refuses it founds another branch. What follows from it is that "Within an already specified domain of objects, no appraisal of capacity or of recognition issues a certificate of standing as an end," and Paper 4 takes care to say that this "states what this paper itself does; it is not a claim about what attitude anyone ought to hold."[^c1-p4] Yet in this framework's texts, "the other is an end" is said in several ways. Value Theory says "the other is an end, and was so before I recognized it" — a fact. The First Critique says "The human being is an end in itself" is a postulate. The Methodology Overview defines 15DD by "Not only I am an end" — there it is a definition. Action Theory, again, speaks of the cannot-not "as a person's own choice"; Methodology 00 calls it "structural 'cannot not'"; the Introspection series calls it "the direction that naturally emerges after completing observation and illumination."[^c1-four] Kant's side has two words as well: the Groundwork, at the step from me to others, notes that the proposition is "here put forward as a postulate" (our translation), and says the grounds will be found in the last section; the Critique of Practical Reason calls the consciousness of the moral law a fact.[^c1-kant5] The objects and the argumentative work of these two places cannot be taken as the same on the strength of the words alone. Fact, postulate, definition, choice, structural cannot-not, direction: are these answers to one question, or do they speak separately of the object, the ground, and the undertaking? The First Critique left one case behind, with the words: "the first full hearing of that critique, and its case file, lie here."[^c1-fc11] That case sits at the 16DD station (the First Critique's On Genesis divides this road into stations by DD), and it examined Methodology 00's argument from "cannot be taken in" to "ought to acknowledge." In taking it over, this book changed its shape into two questions. If the other's being an end is a fact, can that fact be a person's reason for entering 15DD? If it is a postulate, who undertakes it? Both questions, and the original case, are in Chapter Five.

For the seam between ought and is, Kant set up a postulate of practical reason: the complete fit of the will with the moral law is holiness, which a finite human being attains at no moment, so an endless progress of approach is needed, and for its sake the immortality of the soul is postulated.[^c1-kant6] The First Critique stopped here and stated the asymmetry: "we keep the remainder precisely by refusing to register any approach as closure."[^c1-fc7] On the practical side, this book says: no one can accord with 15DD at every moment and in every matter — that is reality, not something derived from any postulate. It has an opponent: the one who claims to fit 15DD at all times. This opponent has the same form as the completeness-theorist on the cognitive side; neither can confirm his success from within. The First Critique set "the remainder cannot be eliminated" as a postulate;[^c1-m0rem] this is its counterpart on the practical side, each with its own warrant, the one not derived from the other. Postulates of practical reason, in Kant's sense, we do not set up.

4 · The Zero Point on the Practical Side

The First Critique measured every candidate foundation with one ruler: can an intelligible and performatively consistent opposing position be formed? Measured so, one remained: "There is no performatively consistent opposing position; Negativa is therefore an axiom — so far, the only one."[^c1-fc8] Anyone who denies "there is Negativa" has, at the moment of denying, already drawn a demarcation, and is already using Negativa. Methodology 0 (second edition) fixes it as the sole axiom of all the series, and writes: "The list is open: when a new candidate arrives, it passes the same test."[^c1-m0open] What this axiom binds is the act of denial, not action.

On the practical side, this book needs one more thing. It passes the same test, but passes it only within one class of acts. Here it is:

The in-domain zero point: every act that takes another's will as its object — domination, coercion, deception, persuasion — concedes, in the act, the other's "Negativa": it concedes that he can say no; and within acts of this class, this concession cannot be denied in a performatively consistent way.

The two halves of the name say exactly these two things. Zero point: no intelligible and performatively consistent opposing position can be found. In-domain: this holds only within this one class of acts. The concession meant here is not agreeing with him, nor granting him any standing; it is that the act has already counted in his ability to say no — a no that can be spoken, that others understand, and that they can take up, not mere struggling. What is conceded is this capacity; whether he is an end has, in this book, another source.

On the practical side there are two candidates, and they must be looked at separately.

The first: denying that one has any self-given law at all. Is this denial performatively consistent? It is. A person who lives by others' rules, or takes things a step at a time, and says "I have no law of my own," has not, in saying it, laid down a law for himself — unless he makes that very sentence a rule he keeps. The denial holds, and a person can live this way; so "everyone has a self-given law" cannot be a zero point, and whoever denies it stands on another branch. (When the First Critique says "'do not construct' is also a law," that is another matter: a law whose content is not to construct is still a self-given law.)

The second: denying that another can say no, and treating him as a thing. Here the case is different. The slaveholder does not take the slave as an end, yet he has to guard against his running away. Against livestock, however high the fence and however strong the lock, what is guarded against is only that animal. Against a slave, he needs fugitive-slave laws, travel passes, and other slaveholders who likewise do not take this slave as an end. These are not higher fences; they are aimed at other people. The fugitive-slave law governs whoever might take him in; the pass is for the eyes of whoever meets him on the road; what the institution forbids is precisely the response of a third person. For the slave's "no" can be called out to a third person, and the third person understands that it is a no, and can take it up. Everything the slaveholder guards against faces one side of that capacity: being able to think, to speak, to seek others out, to join forces with them. His books are therefore kept in two sets: one lists a thing, the other a person to be guarded against; the second set will not balance, and it is his own acts that testify. The capacity his words deny, his acts concede all along — which is what the definition above says. (Chapter Three walks this ladder of precautions rung by rung; fugitive-slave laws, travel passes and the like are examples this book supplies.)

The in-domain zero point differs from the sole axiom, and where it differs must be stated exactly.

It does not depend on standpoint: whatever branch you stand on, whatever set of assumptions you hold, it holds. But it governs only one class of acts — acts that take another's will as their object. It can of course be written "if you do an act of this class, then …"; but this "if" is not "if you accept some assumption." The antecedent is what you are doing, not what you have accepted, and the act is classed by what it does — threatening, deceiving, persuading — not by what the doer calls it. Changing branch or changing assumptions changes nothing here. The axiom's antecedent is also an act, namely the act of denying it, so no opponent stands outside it; the antecedent of the in-domain zero point is a different class of act, and whoever does no act of that class stands outside it — without being its opponent.

For newly arriving candidates the First Critique set up a front door in three steps: first, whether an intelligible and performatively consistent opposing position can be formed; then, open up the candidate's contradiction and "see where the load-bearing point falls"; last, entry into the register.[^c1-fc12] Apply the second step. Opened up, the contradiction here bears its load not on some replaceable assumption but on the kind of act, so this is not a zero point hung on an assumption; and what it speaks of is the other's "no," not the denier's own demarcation, so it is not merely that axiom in another garment either. Among the outcomes the second step originally sorts candidates into, there is no such case; this book adds it as a new disposition, and it enters the register in Chapter Four.

What this book examines is the cannot-not of a person who stands in the position of "Not only I am an end" (Methodology Overview, V3.1), and in that position there are always others — present ones, or future ones. Every act in that position aimed at others' wills falls within the range of the in-domain zero point; but this book examines more than these. Avoiding harm to someone not present is also acting from this position, yet it does not operate on his will. A person who never lifts a hand, never opens his mouth, and directs nothing at anyone does not fall within the zero point's range — he is not its opponent, only outside it. The criterion of exclusion is whether the act is aimed at another's will, not whether anyone is nearby: a solitary person who writes a book for people to come, meaning to persuade them, is still within the zero point's range.[^c1-v14b]

It is not a second axiom. It is a tool this book uses for diagnosis. The First Critique has a precedent: for the operator — the one who performs and answers for judgments — it set out a theorem displayed by performance, which is "a registry item warranted by display, not a second axiom."[^c1-fc9] The precedent holds only at this level. That theorem has an intelligible opponent, the thoroughgoing mechanist, whom the First Critique names; the in-domain zero point, within its domain, has none. Nor does it vouch for any particular content. To say that someone has falsely claimed something — produced a certificate he did not earn — one must still read what he actually did and what he guarded against, and whether that reading is right can be rechecked; whether an act counts as aimed at another's will can also be disputed and rechecked; and to use the zero point to vouch for a particular judgment would be a form of false claiming. It does not change the First Critique's conclusion: "One thing crosses all four dimensions — among candidates examined so far, exactly one."[^c1-fc10] What crosses everything is still that one alone.

With this, the verdict of the first case has its core half in hand: Concession is unavoidable; adoption can be refused. Concession is handed over by the act, in acts that take another's will as their object, and cannot be denied in a performatively consistent way; what is conceded is his capacity to say no, not his standing. This agrees with the limit Paper 4 draws: Paper 4 writes that pointing out that a person is already judging "does not render his claim performatively self-defeating" — performative consistency does not reach as far as standing.[^c1-p4lim] Adoption is setting "others are ends too" as a premise of one's own law; this step remains a choice, and can be refused. One's Own Law long ago had a sentence close to this: "you did not create this law; you arrived at it, and you bear it yourself."[^c1-a16reach] Whoever denies in words that others can say no, while his acts keep dealing with that very capacity, keeps two sets of books; whoever concedes without adopting keeps one set — that is another branch; whoever has adopted has, from then on, a cannot-not in his law. The step from concession to adoption is one no zero point can take for anyone. Readers of Value Theory should mind one word. Value Theory says that the work of 15DD is to "acknowledge this fact" (the Chinese page's word is 认, the word this book keeps for concession), and the fact in question is that the other is an end;[^c1-v14c] in this book's division, that falls on the side of adoption.

What remains is this critique's proper work: to lay out and examine, one by one, the cannot-nots this framework has stated in its various places — which kind each is, by what it binds, how far, and how it lives alongside the cannot-nots of others. The zero point that crosses everything is one only. The in-domain zero point passes the test only within one class of acts, and is a tool of diagnosis. The rest are postulates that someone undertakes, and what is derived from them, each with its premises and grounds, and each to be examined on its own. The lateral ought is not discussed; the inside-out ought is the cannot-not; the text under examination is read from outside, and no one speaks in its place. This critique legislates for no one. What it examines is this: by what does a law bind the one who made it? The work of critique begins here.

Chapter Two · On Cost

1 · What an Act Leaves on One's Own Books

On Jurisdiction, in the First Critique, asks of a demarcation how far it reaches and what it lets slip. Its answer is a ledger: every cut deviates, and the deviation belongs to neither side of the line; it "belongs to this cut".[^c2-fc-zone1] This chapter asks the same question on the practical side. A person has done something by his own law. What has that deed left on his own books?

Our answer, stated up front: it leaves cost, and cost is remainder — the part this cut did not take in. Cost is one's own cost. Accepting a power has a cost. People often overlook it, not because it is small but because it makes no sound.

Why cost is remainder. This sentence has to carry the way the whole book speaks, so it is argued first.

To do something by one's own law is to make a cut in a situation: this, not those; for this, not for those. A situation always holds more than one cut can take in. Action Theory sorts what is left after a judgment into three kinds: what has been judged and may be done or not; what was considered but could not yet be judged; and what bears on the thing to be done this time, yet falls outside what is handled this time — "Only the third kind is the remainder of this judgment; it stays, counted neither as settled nor as something that may be cleared away."[^c2-why-d4] In D3 it records a further kind of leftover: when two lines drawn on one matter collide, "the one not followed is not cancelled by that".[^c2-why-d3] D3 does not call this a remainder. This book enters it as one, on the ground that the First Critique lists the double-hit among the modes in which remainder enters the ledger (Section 3). Cost is the face these two kinds of leftover show on the lawgiver's own books: something his law holds that this cut did not take in — some of it never handled, some of it weighed and yielded.

An example. To get an urgent thing done, a man breaks an appointment with a friend. The friend's disappointment is real; as something that happens on the friend's side, it is a consequence, and how heavy it is the friend alone can say. His own books record something else. His law contains the rule that a promise made is a promise kept, and this time that rule was not taken in. He weighed it and yielded it; in Section 3 this case is a double-hit. What was weighed and could not be done is not the same kind of leftover as what never entered this judgment at all; when both fall within his own law, both are his cost. Take the same broken appointment where his law held no such rule: the friend's disappointment is still a consequence, but it is not his cost. Cost follows the law, so it stands only on the lawgiver's own books. Two people each have their own law, so there is no common ledger in which to compare whose cost is heavier.

A single entry can be settled; the overall ledger of remainder never empties. An apology, amends, another appointment can answer this entry, and on the friend's side the matter may well be over. But every answer is a new cut, with things of its own it cannot take in. The First Critique put it this way: "The act of eliminating the remainder is precisely an act that produces remainder."[^c2-why-rem] This is exactly what Section 3 says under the intake-deficit: "the modes can shift; the ledger does not empty". As for each cut: every cut has something it cannot take in, so no cut can claim to have left nothing behind; whoever says so will find a case waiting for him in Chapter Three, the false claim of costlessness. What a given cut has left in his own law is said matter by matter.

This settles how the book speaks. If cost were only a forecast of consequences — do this, and here is what will land on you — stating it could turn into pressure from outside. If cost were a price, stating it would mean measuring it against a common measure. Value Theory's Prequel has ruled that remainder submits to no such measuring: "every pricing presupposes a common measure and a market"; "To be without price is not to be the maximum of price; it is to be outside the domain of pricing."[^c2-noprice] Since cost is remainder, the same ruling falls on it: no common measure can measure it, and no market can put a figure on it. So to state a cost is only to point something out to a person: here is where, in your own law, this cut did not take something in. Whether that is right, only he can check, against his own law — and he can check wrongly, the misplacement-deficit being one case of it; but no one outside can do the checking for him. Whether to bear it, too, is his alone to decide. What happened, and whether the law he has stated and the things he has done contradict each other, both sides can check; a check that finds a contradiction still gives no one the entitlement to claim the cost on his behalf. So to coerce no one and only state the cost is not a softer kind of coercion.

Remainder is neither a punishment nor a medal. It is a gap, and also a handle. The First Critique says the remainder "gives the next round its conditions, and exerts no compulsion", and again: "the remainder accumulates to where chiseling is possible; the next stroke has its conditions and its handle; whether the stroke falls lies with the performer, stoppability on record."[^c2-why-cond] Action Theory says it just as exactly: "A remainder gives possibility, not a push."[^c2-why-push] Where the next cut falls often depends on what the last one did not take in.

This answer has a ready source upstream. Speaking of different branches, the First Critique registers them "each with its conditions and its costs"; and in On Jurisdiction it writes a sentence with the weight of a definition: "branches are defined by laws, costs are settled against ends".[^c2-fc-cost] Costs are settled against ends: what a law pays out for its end goes on the books of the one who made that law, not on anyone else's.

2 · Six Uses of the Word "Cost"

We start not from a definition but from usage. In the framework's published writing, the word "cost" (代价) has at least six uses.

Use The original sentences
A · A form of remainderPaper 5: "has as its logical remainder the ceding of adjudicative authority to external bodies. This is not a framework failure but the cost of completeness"; Power Theory P3, on the bystander: "Silence is not without cost: the expenditure of maintaining silence is precisely the remainder at work"
B · What is paid in actionValue Theory P3: "standing is not cost. What one pays for a value may be enormous: a person who truly holds the care of his family dear pays immensely, and that is a cost in action, unrelated to this judgment"
C · What others or the world addValue Theory P3: "what sustains that compliance is no claimed content of his but the cost of departing from it"; Action Theory P6: "what he can do is not add a cost to it"; Economics P2: exit costs "can be high enough to be practically equivalent to lock-in", and, on retaliation risk, "After the 15DD party signals exit, the 14DD party can punish"; Moral Law P7: costliness is "the 14DD topological signature visible to external observers (including later researchers) — not the subject's internal cost calculation"
D · The cost of a positionValue Theory Paper 0: "The cost is that it cannot, as the substantive value reading can, derive a duty directly from a value"; Action Theory Paper 0: "the price is that no procedure can replace judgment and guarantee that it is not wrong"; Paper 4: "The cost is a burden of proof and a transmission ledger, not politeness"
E · Cost at the construct layerPower Theory P6, on back-binding: "The ρ layer of reflexive-binding is the locked form of A's remainder"; "its structural-layer cost is A's adjustable freedom cast into maintenance tasks (exhaustible, it can be pressed through a threshold)"
F · DegradationReason Cannot Not: the subject who stops "is swallowed by the 14DD instrumental structure and reduced to a tool"; "To exit is to be degraded"

[^c2-cost-table]

Of the six, this book reads A, B and D as adjacent to the sense of remainder on the practical side (this book's reading). B is at the level of action, D at the level of theory, and A names what the two levels share: a cut falls, some part is not taken in, and that part is booked to the one who cut. B's "pays" and D's "burden of proof and a transmission ledger" both, in the original, name something taken on; to read them as the side that was not taken in is this book's reading. Paper 5 speaks of "the cost of completeness"; Paper 4 says that whoever refuses a postulate loses "the entitlement to have this paper's premise vouch for his own conclusions". This book reads both in the same sense: choose this side, and that side is left outside. That is the one sense in which this chapter uses "cost". A loss added from outside is booked as consequence; whether it has also left something undone in the person's own law is booked separately, and is not excluded wholesale because its origin is outside.

The other three each have their own place.

C is not one's own cost. The cost of departing, the cost of exit, the risk of retaliation are responses from other people's laws or from the world; they land on a person, but his cut did not leave them. Value Theory sees this point exactly: where compliance is sustained only by the cost of departing, "No attribution has done constitutive work in him", and those requirements "run throughout the relation as external requirement".[^c2-v34] What others add is consequence, which gets a column of its own in the next section; it is not thereby the cost meant here — whether it has also left something undone in your own law is booked separately (see above). Moral Law P7's "costliness" also belongs in this column. What it measures is loss of life, freedom, property, reputation, and the text notes in its own words that this is a signature for external observers, "not the subject's internal cost calculation". It uses the loss as one layer of a three-layer filter, and says plainly: "The three are jointly required; passing one or two is not enough." The overstep is not in using loss as a filter; it is in what comes after the filtering: affirming from outside, positively, that a given person had a "15DD moment". Chapter Three files this under the false claim of a human type, as positive naming.[^c2-cost-evidence]

One example has to be set apart: sacrifice. Action Theory writes that "sacrifice is a cost, not a medal"; its upstream, Moral Law P7, says in its own words "Sacrifice is cost, not virtue", and calls sacrifice "the collision product of the 14DD environment — the remainder of 14DD, not the remainder of 15DD".[^c2-sacrifice] Taken at its word, the loss produced by the collision with the environment belongs to C. We accept, as it stands, that it is not to be written up as a medal. Whatever item in his own law he weighed and yielded is still his cost, by Section 1. The two are booked separately.

E is the same word doing a different job. Back-binding is Power Theory's term: a power-holder who sets out to lock others in is himself bound by the work of keeping them locked. ρ is Power Theory's sign for remainder. Power Theory cuts back-binding into two layers: the ρ layer is remainder, "inextinguishable, it cannot be compressed to zero"; the construct layer is adjustable freedom, "exhaustible". In Power Theory P6 the word "cost" attaches only to the construct layer.[^c2-backbind] The two usages do not conflict. Power Theory's construct-layer cost is the share that can be mechanically used up; this chapter's cost is the share a cut did not take in, and it answers to Power Theory's ρ layer — "reflexive-binding is a form of A's remainder, not its ashes". One word, two places, two jobs; when quoting, keep them apart. What is matched here is the kind: the share a cut did not take in. Which item in a power-holder's own law is left undone by his being bound to the lock is still identified matter by matter, as in Section 1; it is not filled in directly from the fact that the structure constrains him.

F writes the cost of leaving as a loss of identity. The Introduction has already taken this apart: what the one who stops loses is that law's realization in him in this one matter, not who he is.

3 · The Anatomy of Cost: Four Ways of Entering the Ledger

The First Critique says that a demarcation's deviation "enters the ledger in four basic modes": the intake-deficit, the misplacement-deficit, the double-hit, out-of-field.[^c2-fc-four] On the practical side each of the four takes a shape of its own. This book borrows the four names to sort the practical side; it does not carry them over unchanged, cell for cell.

The intake-deficit: action never takes in the whole of the law. A law says more than any run of actions ever does. No one can accord with 15DD at every moment and in every matter — this is a fact of life, not something derived from any postulate. The sentence has its opponent: whoever claims to accord with 15DD at all times. Writing about reputation, Moral Law sets down the other face of the same thing: "Perfection, indeed, would be more suspicious than imperfection"; "Imperfection within this operation is normal and expected"; it even lists "periods in which the Self is operating in 14DD modes" among the ordinary limitations that do no damage to the subject's identity.[^c2-ml3-27] The completeness-theorist on the side of cognition cannot confirm from inside that his intake is complete; neither can the person on the practical side who claims to keep to his law at all times.

The First Critique has one more sentence on this mode that matters. The four modes do not stand equal: "Misplacement can be corrected … the double-hit can be resolved … out-of-field can be relocated"; only the intake-deficit cannot be removed by treatment of its own kind: "the modes can shift; the ledger does not empty".[^c2-fc-ledger] So too on the practical side. One can correct a misplacement, move to another field, do the next thing; the seam between law and deed does not close because one got it right once.

The misplacement-deficit: taking the foreign for one's own, or one's own for the foreign. The First Critique wrote down what this mode looks like "upon the self": "taking one's own doing for foreign, or the foreign for one's own".[^c2-fc-self] Taking the foreign for one's own is what Value Theory P3 writes as self-colonization: an external warrant taken as constitution already completed. Its verdict can only look back — "Nor can the judgment be contemporaneous. … What remains, then, is retracing" — the same shape as the First Critique's "the verdict of misplacement is retrospective".[^c2-v37] The other direction runs the reverse way, and the example is this book's: a man believes he is only following the rules, and sees only afterwards that it was his own law at work all along. Either way the misplacement costs the same: through that stretch of his days he did not see clearly for whom he was paying.

The double-hit: two demands that cannot both be met in one place. In the First Critique the double-hit is where "this cut's criterion strikes both sides on one and the same thing"; the cause is a condition the criterion left out, and fixing the condition resolves it.[^c2-fc-four] The double-hit on the practical side is not like this. Value Theory P5 places conflict in joint realizability, and "joint realizability is a property of demands for action": one afternoon, two things that both ought to be done, and only one of them can be.[^c2-v62] One and the same act is a success under one demand and a failure under the other — that is what striking both sides looks like on the practical side. The difference is that adding a condition, or adding a rule, does not remove it: "the gap has merely moved to the next line of the list".[^c2-v70] Here a person yields one item. To yield is not to give that item up: "Yielding belongs to the ordering of action, exit to constitution, and the two are not on the same side."[^c2-v64] What becomes of the yielded item afterwards, Value Theory splits into four questions — does the demand still hold; is the window still open; can that row still surface new candidates; has its place within his own law changed — "four different things here, each with its own subject".[^c2-v65] The cost of a double-hit lies in these four questions, and cannot be merged into one vague sense of owing.

Out-of-field: where one's own law does not reach. A law makes its cuts within its field; outside the field it issues no verdict: "out-of-field is not error but no-verdict".[^c2-fc-out] On the practical side, out-of-field leaves two roads. One is to change the field, and that means revising one's own law — Value Theory P2 says a content stands throughout "at a position revisable by the bearer in the first person", where it can be "questioned, altered, or given up";[^c2-v20] the other is to record it as it is: on this matter, my law issues no verdict. The second road is the example given here. The cost of out-of-field is that stretch of no-verdict: there, a person has no law of his own to go by.

Cost and consequence: two columns. The First Critique keeps the remainder apart from the reverse side: the remainder belongs to the cut; the reverse side belongs to the other side of the line. The practical side keeps cost and consequence apart in the same way. Cost is what this cut owes on one's own books; consequence is how the world and other people respond to the deed.

The framework's own writing already supports this split. Value Theory P5: "The adjudication, the thing he chose, his carrying it out, and what followed in the world, are four things. They have their own times, their own subjects, and their own settlements."[^c2-v68] Meaning Theory, keeping a person's own ledgers, takes in only three kinds of event — claiming, revision and exit — and states: "Not every result in the world caused by that event automatically becomes associated material of the entry"; the two ledgers "settle no responsibility".[^c2-y2] Action Theory puts it most directly. Of the thing a man failed to do: "Nor does it erase the consequences: the extra quarter the other team spent on the problem is real."[^c2-d24]

So for the man we met earlier who changed the number, the cost of leaving goes on his own books. The extra quarter the team spent is consequence: his entry of cost does not settle it, and his having paid the cost does not write it off.

One more comparison needs to be made clear. Value Theory P1 lists five things a local adjudication of action rests on: "the concrete relation, boundary, irreversible harm, retractability, and the keeping of the remainder".[^c2-v1-5] These five are grounds of adjudication, not entries on the books, and only two of them touch the two columns. Irreversible harm is what Value Theory P5 calls "a judgment about consequence"; of retractability it says "the subject there is the action".[^c2-v5-5] The other three fall in neither column: relation and boundary are the situation a person faces when he judges, and "the keeping of the remainder" has already been broken down, in P5, into the four questions above. The five are not forced into two columns here. Retractability asks whether this cut can be taken back. What cannot be taken back is, first, the action and its consequences, each to its own column; the item in his own law that thereby went undone, this book reads as cost (this book's reading).

The subject is moving. On Jurisdiction, in the First Critique, says: "the subject is moving" — after each stroke, "where the chiseler stands is no longer where it stood before the stroke".[^c2-fc-move] So cost often falls on one's later self: a word said today is paid for by tomorrow's self. The First Critique records a kind of false claiming called the false claim of the present station: "yesterday's construct impersonating today's performer".[^c2-fc-now] The practical side has its mirror image: today's I signs for a cost on behalf of tomorrow's I, as though the person of tomorrow would not change. A promise made today that reaches into tomorrow is not automatically a false claim; treating it as a permanent authorization that never needs asking again is the case Chapter Three examines.

4 · The Cost of Five Acts

For each of the five acts below, two things are set down: what shape the cost takes, and why it is often overlooked.

Accepting a power. Power Theory has written down the cost on the side of refusal: "refusal costs you the position of dignity promised by the narrative".[^c2-p1-5] By this chapter's division, that is something the narrative takes back, and it belongs to C. For the cost on the side of acceptance, this book has found no ready-made sentence. It runs like this. A person lets a power take a place in the ordering of his own actions, and from then on some things in that place are arranged by that power. Whether the arrangement shuts out his own judgment, and whether it leaves room for change and for exit, has to be seen in the actual relation. By the definition in Section 1, the cost lies here: if his own law contains "these few things I arrange myself", this time that was not taken in. What he gained is another matter. On Accountability rejects outright the reading that makes the two one transaction: "A recognition-based right is not got in exchange for recognizing a power"; to call accountability "the price of holding the right" — "as a universal rule it does not stand".[^c2-f43] The cost of accepting a power is often overlooked precisely because it so often arrives together with a benefit. It looks like a purchase; but the two accounts cannot be set off against each other.

Exercising a power. Power Theory calls the power that sets out to lock the other in chiseling power, and the power that lifts the other up, toward the positional difference's own dissolution, cultivating power. P6 is plain about it: a chiseling power-holder "is blocked by the physics of ρ ≠ ∅ (the compression cannot reach bottom)", and what overflows is "the system's residual heat that A cannot press down, and A is compelled to supply force permanently to suppress it. This is the mechanical cost of the severing structure, not A's calculation."[^c2-p6-1] This cost has two layers. In one, the power-holder's own remainder is bound into the work of maintenance and cannot be pressed to zero; in the other, his adjustable freedom is taken up inch by inch, and can be used up. It is often overlooked because it is slow, and because it does not necessarily fall on the apex: "the apex of a severing structure can end well while the structure's account is never cleared".[^c2-p6-1b] Not every exercise of power carries this cost. Power Theory P1 says that modes of exercise that do not perform locking "do not generate back-binding cost"; P6 adds: "Cultivation also pays … low cost is not zero cost."[^c2-p1-8] Both statements are recorded as written.

Pursuing a value. To take up a value is to make a cut: this stretch of time, this much strength, goes to this row and not to the others. Value Theory says outright that uptake is not an obligation: "A bearer with a legitimate bridge before him may decline to take it up, and stands no worse in value for that."[^c2-v59] So the cost of pursuit does not fall on standing. It falls on the stretch of the other rows that is not being walked right now. It is often overlooked because pursuit seems to bring only gains.

Giving up a value. Value Theory draws fine lines here. Walking back from a higher source is "without loss in value"; yielding an item "does not change the standing of what was yielded"; only one case must be cut apart from these — the conditions of application have appeared, and the person actively voids a source: "this is not a neutral walking-back", and even then what it alters is "the accessibility of that row, not the presence of that row".[^c2-v-give] The cost of giving up therefore falls on accessibility, on windows, on the side of action; not on standing. It is often misstated from both ends: either giving up costs nothing, or giving up is losing oneself.

Stopping, and not stopping. The First Critique sent two items, and both are settled here. One is the cost of the one who stops. The Introduction has already set it down in the First Critique's own words: the one who stops "no longer realizes its being-an-end through this operation — that is all".[^c2-fc-stop] The other is "whether one ought to advance". The First Critique writes: "advanceability is a capacity, not a duty"; "whether one ought to advance is a matter of the subject's self-given law, and its strict warrant belongs to the practical critique".[^c2-fc-push] This book's answer: advancing and not advancing both have costs, and both are stated; whether one ought to is not ruled on here but in each person's own law. Of the one who does not advance, the First Critique has already written that he "is registered as a branch, as ever". Here one sentence is added: the stretch he does not advance also goes on his own books, just as the roads not taken by the one who advances go on the advancer's books. The cost of stopping is often overstated; the cost of not stopping is often talked away.

The ladder of precautions, read for its cost. In Chapter Three a ladder is used to read a person who denies that others can say no: the pattern of his precautions shows what he in fact presupposes. Here we first read the cost on the ladder. Someone who means to manage others as things must keep a whole apparatus of maintenance running: fences to mend, passes to check, fugitive slave laws to enforce, neighbors who must all join in refusing shelter — the controlling is never done. The fugitive slave laws and the passes are this book's examples. Power Theory P6 writes that "reflexive-binding does not move and does not spread thin", and that "Apparatus-mediated division of labor cannot dilute T6; it can only replicate T6" — T6 being Power Theory's back-binding theorem.[^c2-p6-4] Read by these two sentences, the fugitive slave laws and the passes take nothing off the slaveholder's share. Everyone who checks a pass with his own hands, who turns the man away with his own hands, performs a lock of his own, and it goes on his own books; the slaveholder's share is not a fraction smaller. Which invoking or managing nodes bear back-binding is identified item by item according to Power Theory; a bystander who has merely received a demand is not booked for a full share of back-binding. The labor of maintenance can be exhausted, and that is Power Theory's construct layer; what lands on this chapter's books is the share of his remainder that cannot be pressed to zero.

One stake must be driven in here: how high or low a cost is has nothing to do with whether a false claim holds. Power Theory has said as much: "What discriminates the triggering of T6 is neither the presence of a maintaining action nor the size of its cost, but the object of the maintenance"; "built on cost, the equivalence would soften into a quantitative correlation".[^c2-p6-3] Cheap oppression is still oppression. This chapter only reads cost; the testimony is in Chapter Three.

5 · Stating the Cost Is Not Coercion

This book does not discuss the lateral ought. What, then, can I do with someone else's cost?

There are three things I can do. First, read. What can be read from outside is acts and channels: whether he has shut his own review, whether other people's paths to correct and to exit are open. What is read is the direction in which these traces change — not a verdict, and not his level. Second, act by my own law: not cooperate, exit, oppose. Action Theory writes of such a person: "wherever he could avoid working with this colleague, he did not work with him, and wherever he could avoid dealing with him, he did not deal with him"; and it states that "exit is no longer keeping in touch, not a judgment that the other is unworthy".[^c2-d41] One's Own Law says: "Opposition is your own remainder unfolding, your own 'cannot not,' not legislation upon them."[^c2-a16-44] Third, when he says no to me, I add no consequence to him for it. Action Theory writes of a man who holds the power of evaluation: "what he can do is not add a cost to it: if the new hire says no, he truly accepts the no, and does not give him any less afterward because of it."[^c2-p6-add] The "cost" there is, by this chapter's division, consequence. What is kept here is a refusal to turn the other's no into a pretext for retaliation; exit, and stopping extra cooperation, are not forbidden by it. How the harm he has done to others is handled is not taken up here; see Chapter Six, Section 4.

There are also three things I cannot do. I cannot read constitution: from outside, no one sees how a person's law is made in him. I cannot read level: this book does not sort people by 15DD; it records operation only by moment and by domain — a discipline of diagnosis (Chapter Three, Section 7), not an assertion about whether some kind of person exists in the world. I cannot turn my reading into his cost. Value Theory says that a community "may confer recognition and may withdraw it", but that this kind of thing "does not constitute value content"; what is removed "is social position and support, not this bearer's own value content".[^c2-v22] What is withdrawn belongs in the consequence column. His entry of cost is still on his own books, and it is his to state.

From this, an act has its address, in four columns: whose law; which position; which matter, which moment; which branch. Once the address is written out, overstepping has a definition on the practical side as well. The First Critique says overstepping is "carrying a criterion out of its field and issuing verdicts as usual";[^c2-fc-over] the practical side follows suit: carrying a law out of its address and making demands as usual.

Last, five constraints, which the whole book keeps.

  • Cost is not punishment. Power Theory says back-binding "is a mechanical cost, not retribution"; Moral Law P7 places outside 15DD "[t]he act of 'making the bad person pay'".[^c2-not-punish]
  • Cost confers no entitlement. Value Theory P3: "standing is not cost." However much you pay, that does not make a content yours.
  • Cost is not consequence. Section 3 has already set them in two columns.
  • Cost is written up neither as a medal nor as degradation. Against the first, Action Theory and Moral Law have already set the rule; the second, the Introduction has already taken apart.
  • Cost is not compared across persons. There is no ruler that stands outside both laws at once and can convert two costs into one quantity.[^c2-v75] Within one person's own single judgment, comparing risk, timing and feasibility is not limited by this. Action Theory says outright that drawing a line "does not presuppose a common master scale across persons and across sources; this does not rule out comparing risk, timing and feasibility within one matter".[^c2-d7]
