Chapter Three · On Illusion

1 · The Unified Form: Displaying More Than It Holds

The First Critique's On Illusion gathered the many ailments on the side of cognition into a single form: "a construct shows a credential it never earned." The test is a reconciliation of accounts: "if any one credential of status that a claim shows in use is not among the entries granted it by test, review, and index, the false claim is constituted — the set shown exceeds the set held".[^c3-fc-ill1] And "the verdict is retrospective and asks no motive": what is diagnosed is the form in which something appears, not the intent of whoever presents it.[^c3-fc-ill2]

On the practical side the form stays the same and only the subject changes: a law, or a cannot-not, displays a credential of validity it never earned. The columns of the ledger become practical columns as well:

  • Modality — how it binds: without condition, hung on a definition, or hung on a position;
  • Source — where it comes from: derived from the axiom, a postulate, or a definition;
  • Jurisdiction — whom it binds: the one who made it, or others;
  • Station — which moment it speaks for: today's self, or yesterday's, or tomorrow's;
  • Object — whether it governs an operation or a person;
  • Warrant — on what grounds: derived, undertaken, or with no recorded source;
  • Cost — where the cost falls, and whether it has been stated.

The First Critique set a rule of discipline at its door, and it is kept here: "what is judged is the false claim; no person is convicted. A subject captured by an illusion is not thereby out of the game".[^c3-fc-ill3] The cases below are drawn only from this framework's own published writings. Kant's universal legislation is, in this book, a branch, not a case.

2 · The Case File

False claim of the unconditional (modality). One's Own Law writes: "This is not a choice, not nobility — it is a mode of being"; "you have no option of 'not opposing'"; the law is "unconditional (no precondition can cause it to lapse)". Reason Cannot Not casts the same law in the ontological form and refuses the hypothetical.[^c3-case-uncond] What is displayed is "no condition." What is held is a cannot-not hung on a position, and the sentence carries its own antecedent: "When your DD level arrives there". The Introduction has already lowered it one grade. One point of fairness must be added: One's Own Law keeps jurisdiction and modality apart — "its binding force falls only on the subject who holds it".[^c3-a16-32] The lesion is in the modality column, not in the jurisdiction column.

False claim of deduction (source). The first paper of the Moral Law series calls the four theorems "the specific unfolding of SAE's sole axiom—negativa (非)—in multi-Self situations"; Rights Theory Paper 0 says it three times on a single page, each time differently, once as "self-legislation and the four theorems all follow from it as lemmas and theorems, not as axioms".[^c3-case-deduce] Two places go further, each setting up an axiom of its own. The War Treatise says that what it argues from "is SAE's axiom itself — subjectivity cannot be eliminated, cultivation is the form in which subjectivity grows, cultivation cannot not continue." Economics P6: "SAE's core axiom is 'the remainder must develop.'"[^c3-case-axiom] What is displayed is "derived from the axiom"; what is held is a set of assumptions and definitions that each series undertakes for itself. The second edition of Methodology 0 has withdrawn "Everything else is theorem" and replaced it with "Everything else is postulates and theorems within each series".[^c3-m0-retract] All these places were written before that withdrawal. They have not been changed; this book records them as written and does not change them on their behalf.

False claim of the lateral (jurisdiction). A law crosses its maker's own boundary and makes demands on others. The framework already has a diagnosis of this shape, in Paper 5: "the universalization of a single cannot-not, imposing an individualized action-necessity as a law everyone should obey"; and when such a person meets someone different, all that is left to say is "you should be like me".[^c3-case-horiz] The easiest way to commit this case is to use overlap as the premise of an inference: you and I both take people as ends, therefore you too should do such-and-such. The sentence is this book's own; the corpus contains no such sentence. Its fault is to take a coincidence guaranteed by a definition and use it as grounds for giving orders to others. Wherever the corpus says "ought" in a lateral voice, this book records it as written and reads it as going beyond what this framework discusses. The close of One's Own Law is one such place: "everyone should be their own philosopher-king." The Introduction has already said that this is exactly the kind of thing its own bulkhead failed to hold back.

One point of usage needs a note. In the same passage Paper 5 says such people "erroneously elevate their personal ethics into universal moral commands" — its "ethics" and "moral" run exactly opposite to their use in One's Own Law.[^c3-p5-ethics] Quotations keep each paper's own sense; they are not brought into line.

False claim of a human type (object). This is treating 15DD as a kind of person and counting levels by heads. Moral Law P0 writes "As the 14DD-to-15DD ratio rises"; P6 writes "12DD constitutes the majority + 14DD in the middle + 15DD as a minority".[^c3-case-type] Here the lesion has to be located exactly. The very next item in the same list in P6 reads "Intra-individual mixing — the same individual may operate at different DD levels in different contexts — not a fixed type"; and Economics P3, on which it draws, writes "This is not a classification of people into three fixed types", reading by behavioral tendency and statistical distribution.[^c3-case-type2] So the lesion is not that the original lacks a qualification. It is that the proportion sentence can shed the qualification its own paper supplies. P6 §0.1 itself uses it once on its own — "Real communities are not pure 15DD — 12DD constitutes the majority + 14DD in the middle + 15DD as a minority" — with no qualification beside it. The fix is to write the index of operation into the sentence, not to delete the sentence. Another sentence of Economics P3 goes further: "Subjects with a 15DD tendency … treating the other as means is no longer a cognitively available operation." This makes an operation into something a person of that tendency is unable to do, and it collides with the sentence in Chapter Two that no one can accord with 15DD at every moment.

The same column has a second form: positive naming. Moral Law P7 takes the event as its unit. It infers back to a single 15DD operation from evidence filtered and graded by three joint criteria — "The three are jointly required; passing one or two is not enough" — and does not rank people by the size of their sacrifice. Yet once the filtering is done it still pronounces, from outside and in the positive, on particular figures' "15DD moment"; and P6 says "Positive examples may name historical figures".[^c3-case-name] This book does not use such outside material to certify how anyone's first person is constituted; what it opposes is the step from these proxies to positive certification. Positive naming, like negative naming, settles from outside the level of someone's operation. The discipline of Section 7 of this chapter applies equally to both.

The framework also contains the proper form of this case. Economics P3 wrote: "not because they 'are' 12DD, but because their behavioral output is indistinguishable from 12DD at the market level."[^c3-case-proxy] Behavioral output is taken as a proxy for observation, the proxy is marked as not being the definition, and it is marked as fallible. Used this way, a proxy makes no false claim.

Degradation (cost). Reason Cannot Not: the subject "is swallowed by the 14DD instrumental structure and reduced to a tool"; "To exit is to be degraded". Paper 2 has a sentence of the same family: "without integrity, the subject becomes a tool".[^c3-case-degrade] What is displayed is a loss of standing. What is held is at most that one operation, in one matter, went unrealized. The Introduction and Chapter Two have already taken this apart. The second edition of Paper 4 itself wrote "that this reading can be drawn from the original does not mean that all of the original's inferences about 'the subject becomes a tool' or 'the subject no longer exists' have already been gathered under it."[^c3-p4-25] This book enters these sentences, as written, among the revision candidates. Whether they are revised is for each paper to decide.

False claim of a final verdict (station and warrant). A single reading is taken as the settled verdict on a person. One's Own Law wrote: "Seeing it and still not acknowledging it is not a vision problem — it is lying." That diagnosis must first know what, "at your level," one "should be able to see" — which means it must first settle which level the other is at.[^c3-case-final] Moral Law P3 sets this form out most completely. It says reputation has staying power: it is "somewhat persistent", because "the prior operations have established what kind of operator the Self is"; it says "Motivation is observable, but observation requires time. 日久见人心 — over time, the human heart becomes visible"; and it says "Sustainable strategic faking of 15DD reputation can only converge to actually becoming 15DD."[^c3-case-ml3] Taken together, the three sentences give reputation a drift toward a settled verdict: motive is read, the kind of operator is fixed, and convergence is guaranteed. The same paper also contains a clause that runs the other way. It provides for "the event-level reset of recognition state", and "when the Self returns to 15DD operation the community can re-recognize". So reputation is not an absorbing state there; the false claim falls in two places, the reading of motive and the guarantee of convergence.[^c3-ml3-32]

The framework has long contained its opposite. The Conflict Treatise states that "no test is given anywhere in this treatise for determining which layer the other is running on"; and also, "What is suspended is this path, not a final judgment on this person."[^c3-case-conflict] This book's ruling-out follows that lead. A ruling-out can be withdrawn and is no absorbing state. It requires independent verification that some necessary condition was not met; where only traces of risk are seen, the result is a direction report (Section 7).

False claim of the present station (station). The First Critique's original definition: "an intake of one station issues verdicts, without re-examination, upon today's"; and "judging another's today by yesterday's construct of him" is one of its cases.[^c3-fc-now] On the practical side, this case is yesterday's reputation used, without re-examination, as today's judgment. Chapter Two described its mirror image: today's self signs a cost on behalf of tomorrow's self and treats the signature as a permanent authorization that needs no further asking. A mirror image is not the same case; it is recorded alongside here.

False claim of a drive (modality). Reason Cannot Not: "remainder cannot not have an external outlet (avoiding internal collapse); the only legitimate outlet is another 15DD subject"; and when two independent purposes meet, "C does not belong to A nor to B; it is the action forced into being by the meeting of two independent purposes."[^c3-case-drive] What is displayed is a force that pushes people along; what is held is conditions and judgment. On the side of cognition, the First Critique said long ago: "The remainder is only a gap … by itself it pushes nothing". The Introduction has already taken this apart. Action Theory P8, in the same section where it repeats this sentence, also wrote its own correction: the thing two people do together "is something both of them avow and both are able to do".[^c3-d47]

False claim of a standard (source). Action Theory writes: "The demand is that high, because the premise is that others are ends. No one can do it at every moment, but at the moment one stands here, the demand is that high."[^c3-case-std] Just before this, the same passage says that "cultivation is a direction, not something that can be done at every moment". The meaning is sound. The trouble is that the word "demand" is easily read as a yardstick for measuring people. This book reads it as the strength of a definition (Chapter Five, Section 4; reading registry, item sixteen) and does not find a false claim on the strength of one word. The Moral Law said it itself: "The four are not standards that the Self strives to reach." One's Own Law also refuses the word — "Standards imply publicness" — yet the same paper, in its section "The Subject-Recognition Radius," writes "The standard of judgment has only two variables".[^c3-case-std2] This book's practice is to use not a standard but a definition. 15DD is a definition, not a ruler others hold up to measure me.

False claim of costlessness (cost). When an action or a postulate claims to have left nothing behind, it denies the remainder on the practical side. One's Own Law, on resonance: "When they do not, each continues to vibrate; neither loses anything."[^c3-a16-44] Whether this sentence is such a case depends on what "loses" refers to. If it means standing, then each vibrating on its own does indeed lose nothing. If it means that this once they did not come together, then that is a cost, only a silent one. Power Theory, elsewhere, has already corrected sayings of this kind: "Silence is not without cost: the expenditure of maintaining silence is precisely the remainder at work."[^c3-p3-silence] This case grows out of Chapter Two. Every cut leaves something it cannot take in, so no cut can claim to have left nothing; a single entry can be settled, but the overall ledger of remainder never empties.

False claim of unsourced validity (warrant). The Conflict Treatise says its four constraints "issue no normative force". Yet the same section says that the decision to stop, remove, arbitrate or suspend "is itself still not to be placed in principle beyond examination of reasons. What continues there is an effect further upstream"; and in drawing together it says so once more: "What continues is the reviewability of the decision to stop, an effect further upstream".[^c3-case-upstream] Along the whole chain on which this framework hands over its "ought," this is the only place this book has found that claims binding force without stating where the force comes from. What is displayed is binding force; what is held has no entry on the ledger. This book only records it and does not supply a source on its behalf.

False claim of convergence (warrant). The direction of history is called necessary. Moral Law P6: "14DD growing toward 15DD is ontologically necessary"; "Structurally necessary — not possibility, but structural necessity". Economics P4: "Shape is the necessity of the ρ-conservation principle. Speed is the result of human choice." The title of Economics P5 is The Structural Inevitability of 15DD Emergence in Free Markets.[^c3-case-conv] Economics P4 ties its warrant to the conservation of the remainder, whereas the First Critique wrote: "conservation is no motor. A remainder persisting on the ledger pushes no one to chisel at it; whether to chisel remains, always, the subject's affair."[^c3-fc-cons] This is the most direct instance of displaying more than is held. Action Theory later lowered the claim to a judgment of the system: "This system's judgment is that the phase transition is coming nearer step by step, and that how fast it goes is chosen by people"; and it added that "particular periods can go backward".[^c3-case-conv2] This book examines only the form of this case. A claim about the direction of history must account for three things separately: its empirical premises, the reasoning from premises to conclusion, and which stretch of actual history it applies to. Offered as an empirical hypothesis, it states what is to be observed and what counts as counter-evidence. Offered as a derivation, it has its premises and connections examined one by one. Economics P5 lists conditional premises and proxy predictions, and this book records them as written; listing them is not yet establishing them. To put the voice of necessity above these three things without accounting for any of them is a false claim. How history actually goes, this book does not assess.

Good-and-evil predicates (source): a candidate case. One's Own Law writes: "There is no finally adjudicated evil person, but there are diagnosable operations of evil. Evil is not a positional identity; it is the direction of regression and suppression." The Conflict Treatise writes: "Lacking the reciprocal condition does not itself constitute evil. … Actively cancelling another subject's position, or compressing their conditions of exit — that is what enters the diagnosis of evil operations." The abstract of Moral Law P0 states that "the present work does not apply good/evil predicates to colonization".[^c3-case-evil] In each of these places "evil" is tied to direction and operation, not to persons. That is the same form as this book's ruling-out, which is a direction. So whether this is a false claim depends on whether the word "evil" is used as a direction or as a person's position. Recording only the direction of an operation makes no false claim. Only when the word is used as a person's lifelong identity does it fall, separately, under this diagnosis. The case is listed here as a candidate and is not decided.

3 · Two Cases of the Same Form as False Claiming: Colonization and Violation

Two further cases share the form of false claiming without being species of it. They come from two series, each with its own definition, and this book writes them according to those original definitions.

Colonization. Value Theory P3: "Colonization is this structural state: a content stands, in the bearer, registered as constitution already completed, while that constitution was not made in the bearer's own law, and what occupies that position is an external end." It speaks of where the constitution was made, not of where the content came from: "Provenance as a measure therefore fails entirely".[^c3-v27] Its crux is one sentence: "It does not succeed in constituting. It succeeds only in being taken as constitution." This is exactly the shape of displaying more than is held. Yet Value Theory states in so many words: "Neither is reducible to the other. … this proposition registers only the relation between them, and does not define colonization as a species under misclaim." Colonization has two things more than false claiming: the reading is already occupied, and the channel of reckoning is already sealed. "A misclaim can occur while the channel of reckoning is open, and can be corrected there; colonization is the state in which that channel has been sealed."[^c3-v43]

Value Theory also draws a boundary: naked coercion falls outside this genus. "An open system of coercion can compress a person's candidate field enormously, … and it never claims that this is his own value"; such a form "falls to P2's locking".[^c3-v27-2]

Violation. Rights Theory P5 lays down that "the core cannot be seized": the core is "the remainder ρ encapsulated by the subject under its own law". Violation does not seize the core; it is "ghostwriting the first person from an external position" — "To decide for a subject which dimensions are its own, to write its first-person encapsulation in its stead". The clearest instance it gives is indoctrination: "an educator who instils too much — the motive is transmission, and the instructed even feels the benefit — has still committed violation and not limitation".[^c3-p5r]

Each of the two is laid down in its own series, and each shares the form of false claiming. Where an external position passes itself off as someone else's first person, the two can intersect. Whether something is colonization, and whether it is violation, is judged in each case by the original definition, and neither is inferred from the other. One candidate at the intersection is indoctrination (the second rung in the next section). Rights Theory P5 rules it a violation: the educator "has still committed violation and not limitation".[^c3-r5-indoc] Whether it is colonization in Value Theory's sense is judged separately, by V27. Neither series says the two are two sides of one thing, and this book does not set that up either. Value Theory's self-colonization — "the source remains outside, while the running need not be" — needs no external position, while violation has to ghostwrite from an external position.[^c3-v37-self] Paper 4 supplies corroboration from another side: "Having reduced another to a state unable to refuse, express or appeal does not by itself show that he lacked standing as an end when the act occurred, nor by itself render that act justified."[^c3-p4-261]

4 · The Ladder of Precautions

This section reads one person's testimony: how the actions of someone who denies that others can say no testify in his place. The examples below — fugitive slave laws and passes, the ban on literacy, the lie about the showers, the curved chutes of the slaughterhouse — are this book's own. The slaveholder example is also used by Power Theory P1, with a reading different from the one here (see below).

The slaveholder does not take the slave as an end, yet he has to guard against the slave running away and rising up; he has no need to guard against his cattle rising up. To get the Jews into the gas chambers, the Nazis told them these were showers. The pattern of precautions shows what the denier himself presupposes the other to be.

  1. Fencing in. What is guarded against is an uncontrollable remainder; livestock too are guarded against in this way.
  2. The ban on literacy, and indoctrination. What is dealt with is the other's self, whether one already there or one about to take shape. This rung has to be split. The ban on literacy is an open prohibition; by Value Theory's division it belongs to locking (V27, second point), not to colonization. Indoctrination, Rights Theory rules a violation. The first paper of the Learning series calls the forcible writing-in, during the window period, of scripts the regulator wants "colonization"; that is the Learning series' own usage and does not automatically coincide with V27. Either may work against a self already there, or may hold down its formation in advance.[^c3-rung2]
  3. Fugitive slave laws and passes. His "no" can be shouted to a third person, and it can be caught; what the institution forbids is precisely the third person's response. The fugitive slave law governs whoever would take him in; the pass is there to be shown to whoever meets him on the road. Animals too give alarm calls, and their fellows answer; what this rung reads is not whether there is a signal, but that the "no" can be heard by a third person as a no, taken up, and that the hearer can stand on his side. Power Theory writes this position as the bystander's "counter registration": refusing to respond, withdrawing protection.[^c3-p3-counter]
  4. Guarding against uprising. The oppressed can know one another as fellows and can join forces.
  5. Luring to death. Right up to the killing, his understanding must still be bypassed.

The reading is this. Each rung shows which response his actions draw on: understanding, speaking, finding someone, joining with others. The top rung shows that right up to the killing, the act still has to go around the other's understanding. This is a rung-by-rung reading, not a ruler for measuring completeness; what the other can actually do is checked separately, against the facts. What he denies in words is exactly what his actions are dealing with all along. So he keeps two sets of books: one lists a thing, the other a person to be guarded against. The second never squares with the first, and the witness to the false claim is himself. Power Theory writes this second set of books as "the debt of verification": "As long as B remains a subject … A can never confirm once and for all that B has become a tool. … A must verify again and again."[^c3-p6-check]

First, a word for the other side. Slaughterhouses use curved chutes to calm the animals. Is that not a precaution too? What the chute manages is sensory cues. The lie about the showers works on understanding, and works only on a being that can believe a sentence. The reading stands. Killing, too, is largely covered: deception, ambush, concealment, striking while the victim sleeps are all ways of dealing with the other's capacity to say no. Rights Theory Paper 0 makes the same point from the other side: sleep is "a temporary interruption of the bandwidth of exercise and of operation, not an exit".[^c3-r0-sleep] What still lies outside this ladder is only an act that draws on no one's response of will.

The strongest opponent is in the framework's own writings. Power Theory P1 writes of the slaveholder that for the party whose subject-position has been fully stripped, "the operations performed on it are not power" — as if there really were a state in which the denier keeps a single set of books, and it lists a thing.[^c3-p1-6] This book reads the passage this way. If it really comes to that, the precautions shrink to the single rung of fencing in; the act no longer aims at his will, so it lies outside the range of the in-domain zero point and does not collide with that zero point. Power Theory does not say that this point cannot be reached. It says that once it is reached, what remains is no longer power, "only the processing of an object", while B remains a subject ontologically. P1 states in the same section: "Any reading of §VI as SAE permits some humans to be actually reducible to objects is a mis-reading." P6 is speaking of power: "Severing power precisely cannot go that far. It needs B to retain enough subjecthood to execute, adapt, respond, carry, internalize. What it wants is B's usable subjecthood".[^c3-p6-2] This book parts from P1 over the whip. P1 reads the slaveholder's whip as "an 11DD–12DD domestication-operation". But if the whip is used to make a person understand an order and carry it out, it is aimed at the other's understanding and choice; that is coercion, and it lies inside the domain. The disagreement is recorded as it stands. The same section of P1 has one more sentence: whipping a slave to death "constitutes, in the fields of the other not-yet-fully-objectified slaves … the strongest registration pressure on A". Even the killing is addressed to other people's wills.

Hence the branch the First Critique recorded in On Genesis — "the branch that gathers all value into the self has been historically inhabited" — has to be split three ways in this book.[^c3-fc-solo] In the first kind, the act aims at no one's will. Theoretical solipsism lays a hand on no one, says nothing, points at no one; and the step at which only the processing of things is left (previous paragraph), if it is really reached, also belongs here. Both lie outside the range of the in-domain zero point, and the stamp does not reach them; theoretical solipsism is performatively consistent and remains a branch as usual. The other two kinds both lie in practice, and both act upon other people's wills. One denies that the other can say no while its actions deal with that very capacity all along. It is intelligible, and people have historically lived in it, but it is performatively inconsistent: those who live in it keep two sets of books. The other concedes that the other can say no, and simply does not take him as an end. It is performatively consistent, an inhabited branch, and the stamp does not reach it. What this book can say about it is where it stands. If it locks in someone who can say no, Power Theory can read off what it pays: the one who exercises power is himself bound first by that locking, which Power Theory calls back-binding (Chapter Two, Section 4). This is a structural reading made after Power Theory. Which item his own law has failed to take in is said matter by matter, and is not filled in for him from outside. When it locks no one in, Power Theory states that no back-binding arises, and all this book can say is the remainder that every cut leaves as usual. Beyond that, there are the traces it leaves outside (Chapter Six). This three-way split is this book's reading, not a division found in On Genesis. The stamp of performative inconsistency is not a verdict of invalidity. The First Critique long ago refused Kant's verdict of invalidity: "we do not rule his claim invalid, but register it as another branch, labeled with its condition". The precedent the stamp matches is what the First Critique said of the axiom's opponents: "people who spoke the denial have really existed; what cannot stand is the position, not the person".[^c3-fc-stamp] What cannot stand is only his one denial. His law, and he himself, are not voided by it, and no one is given license to dispose of him. The stamp rests on a description of his own actions.

The same check must be turned back on the framework's own earlier versions. Reason Cannot Not says in Clause I that one cannot exit and that to exit is to be degraded. In Clause IV it then requires "both subjects to expose themselves to the other's interrogation", and speaks of "two interrogable, mutually-calibrating subjects".[^c3-rcn-34] On one side, stopping is not allowed; on the other, it lives by interrogation and adjustment. It also says that "regression is also a form of development", so that "cannot stop" can be falsified by nothing whatever.[^c3-rcn-32] The framework has already turned old sentences of this kind back several times. The First Critique wrote: "We once wrote this machine as an inescapable forced cycle". The second edition of Methodology 0 withdrew "therefore negativa cannot not operate". The second edition of Paper 4 states that inferences of the "the subject becomes a tool" kind have not yet all been gathered under the new reading.[^c3-self-correct] At the same time the First Critique warned that "a diagnosis of an old version applied, without re-examination, to the new" is also a misdiagnosis.[^c3-fc-mis] So before turning back an old version, first check whether its newer version has already changed it. Action Theory has already lowered these sentences; this book examines only the ones that have not yet been changed.

5 · Why Illusion Is Natural

The First Critique says illusion is "no accident of cognition — where there are constructs, there are its conditions", and it registers the conditions apart, each with its own warrant.[^c3-fc-nat] On the practical side there are four conditions.

First, in two layers. The structural layer can be derived. A cannot-not always hangs on a position, yet in the way it is said, the antecedent can go unwritten: the sentence "this cannot be cancelled" does not on its own carry "as one stands here", just as a construct's appearing carries no index. This layer follows directly from the position-conditioned cannot-not. The experiential layer is recorded only as a hypothesis: a person may feel a cannot-not strongly without attending, at the same moment, to its position-condition. The first layer speaks of sentences, the second of feelings, and the first does not yield the second.

Second, a phenomenological hypothesis, recorded and not derived. The First Critique says: "the deeper a construct's sedimentation, the more readily it enters in the voice of 'it was always so'". Laws are no different. When the Moral Law writes that the four are "the structure that the Self naturally is, when operating at 15DD," that is the voice.

Third, a tendency on the subject's side, likewise only recorded: a law that works for oneself is easily said to work for anyone. The First Critique said something of the same form: "ambition is no disease; exempted ambition is."[^c3-fc-amb] The urge to universalize is no disease; exempted universalizing is.

Fourth, the deferral of cost, also only recorded. Cost often comes later, and falls on one's later self; at the moment of action it appears as if it cost nothing.

The four differ in status and point in one direction. From this the First Critique set up a law of risk, and the practical side sets it up just as it was, without dropping a word of its premises or its limit: under the conditions that the position-condition is not automatically preserved along with the cannot-not, and that new invocations are not reviewed in step, the more widely a cannot-not is invoked, the more points there are at which it oversteps its position-condition — and the risk of illusion rises with them; it "does not guarantee that actual illusions multiply."[^c3-fc-risk]

6 · Two Practical Antinomies

A. Cannot be cancelled; can be left.

Thesis: while one stands in a position, some things cannot be cancelled. Action Theory: "The shared layer is mostly about how people are regarded, and cannot be cancelled while one stands in the position of taking oneself and others as ends". Antithesis: the position can be left. Action Theory: "if a person leaves this position in a given matter, this cannot-not is no longer there for him in that matter".[^c3-ant-a]

Both are Action Theory's own formal claims, and the resolution is in its own text too: "What it states is that two statements are incompatible, not a psychological inability to bring oneself to act." Action Theory Paper 0 writes: "How 'cannot be cancelled' and 'can leave' hold at once in the shared layer, this series explained in Paper 1, §4 by two statements that cannot sit together; a formal account of it can be given separately."[^c3-ant-a2] This book adds only the reading from the doctrine of illusion: the antinomy arises when the index of position drops out, which is the first condition in the previous section. "Cannot be cancelled" says that within the position the two statements cannot both hold. "Can be left" is said of the position, one matter at a time. Each sentence is true at its own address. The First Critique's "able to stop, and yet unceasing — that is autonomy" is a ready-made seam for this antinomy. The formal account is in Appendix III.

One small account still has to be entered. This framework states the cost of exit in four ways. Reason Cannot Not says degradation; the First Critique says "that is all"; Action Theory says "and that is all"; the Moral Law says that after leaving 15DD operation the Self "still has the capacity to legislate".[^c3-ant-a3] The four cannot all be right. Introduction, Section 2, First, has already stated this cost as the last three do, and noted that the statement stands on Paper 4's postulate; Chapter Two follows it.

B. Autonomy and obsession.

Thesis: not ceasing is the mark of autonomy. This is no straw man. Reason Cannot Not wrote: "True autonomy is the persistent legislative motion: the subject gives itself law moment by moment; the moment legislation stops, autonomy ceases to operate."[^c3-ant-b1] Antithesis: not ceasing can also be obsession. The First Critique wrote: "what is able to stop yet unceasing may also be obsession, and parting autonomy from obsession is the practical critique's work."

The second half of the sentence from Reason Cannot Not states a necessary condition: "the moment legislation stops, autonomy ceases to operate." The first half joins its terms with "is": "True autonomy is the persistent legislative motion." With the definite article in "the persistent legislative motion," that "is" invites an identity reading, and an identity runs both ways; so this half can be read as giving a sufficient condition. The antinomy sits on that "is": as long as one does not cease, it counts as autonomy. Not ceasing is necessary, but it is not enough. The candidate criterion is whether, when one is interrogated, the channel of revision and review is open. Obsession is not ceasing while the channel has closed itself. At the other end lies fickleness: stopping whenever it suits, changing the law without keeping the books on the change. So this book gives autonomy three marks: able to stop, yet unceasing; the channel open under interrogation; revising the law and keeping the books on it.

This criterion has to be aligned with one the framework already has. The Learning series gave "cannot-not" a structural definition, and one of its touchstones is "External opposition cannot extinguish it". Seen from outside, that is hard to tell apart from obsession. But the same passage sets a limit: the external opposition the touchstone means "is social blockage … not factual refutation"; and "If factual refutation appears and you still refuse to adjust, that is not 14DD presence but 13DD failure".[^c3-ant-b2] The line falls exactly here. Social blockage cannot extinguish it, but factual refutation it has to take up: the channel is open. Value Theory has a sentence that matters even more: "A person may hold something as what he will keep to the end, unaltered, and this does not conflict with revisability: revisability says the position is open, not that it must be used."[^c3-v21] So keeping something unaltered to the end is not obsession. Only a closed position is.

The three marks are the definition of "autonomy" that this book gives within its own branch, and the definition has an opponent: the branch that counts a law's being unrevisable as loyalty. One's Own Law contains the nearest sentence: "You do not need to stop and ask 'is this direction still correct?' The direction has not changed."[^c3-a16-34] This book does not falsely claim its own definition as the universal definition of autonomy.

Kant's antinomy of virtue and happiness is not set up here: nothing in this framework's material forces it.

7 · The Discipline of Diagnosis

To diagnose others is also to make a claim. So this book lays down five rules of discipline for its own diagnoses.

Name columns, not persons. The First Critique: "A competent diagnosis writes — which credential was shown, which column of the ledger holds no such entry, and where the sources are … what it does not write is — what the shower intended, and what he deserves."[^c3-fc-disc1]

Read structure, not hearts. A third-person diagnosis reads only actions, accounts, channels and patterns of precaution, never motive or disposition. This discipline has several grounds in the framework. The First Critique says "The criterion never asked for motive"; Value Theory says that the criterion of colonization "judges structural effect, not the enactor's initial intent", and that audit "reads only the opening and closing of positions; it does not judge what the bearer really avows"; Power Theory says that means-position is "not A's intent or psychology".[^c3-disc-struct] Its opposite is also in the framework: Moral Law P3's "Motivation is observable … over time, the human heart becomes visible." Section 2 of this chapter has already entered that strong reading under the false claim of a final verdict. Claiming in the first person belongs to the person himself and is not written in from outside. Value Theory states: "First-person avowal can be neither conferred by another nor revoked by another."[^c3-v21b]

Levels record operations only, by moment and by domain. An ideal type is not evidence about a concrete person, and this book does not name persons by level. The Moral Law itself supplies two grounds: "The unit of study is the event, not the person; 15DD names a behavioral layer, not a personal identity"; and its community is "an idealized case in which all members are 15DD legislative subjects". Value Theory also wrote: "What can be said is the direction of change … What cannot be said is where it has got to".[^c3-disc-level] This book does not advance a substantive claim such as "there are no 15DD people"; it lays down only this discipline.

Rulings-out and direction reports are kept apart; both can be withdrawn; there is no absorbing state. The Conflict Treatise: "None may pass for another: cues are not criteria, and traces are not verdicts."[^c3-conflict-26] A ruling-out needs something real: some necessary condition of this one operation, together with the fact that it was not met, has been independently verified. Where only general traces of risk are seen, the result is a direction report (Chapter Six, Section 6). A ruling-out can be withdrawn because a diagnosis can be wrong. Moral Law P3 itself keeps this way open: "when the Self returns to 15DD operation the community can re-recognize".[^c3-ml3-32]

A diagnosis is also a claim. The First Critique gave misdiagnosis a typology too, the exact mirror of false claiming: an unreviewed accusation carrying itself as a settled case; the diagnosis of one case stretched into a judgment on the whole framework; a diagnosis of an old version applied, without re-examination, to the new. Value Theory gave the clearest shape. Of an audit that claims to see what a person really avows, it says: "if he accepts it, the conclusion holds; if he denies it, the denial is read as further evidence for it."[^c3-disc-self] A diagnosis of that kind is itself a false claim.

Every diagnosis set up in this chapter also stands under interrogation.
