Chapter Four · On the Postulates

1 · Format: What a Claim Must Spell Out

The two preceding chapters were diagnoses: what a cut leaves on the cutter's own books, and where a credential displays more than it holds. Diagnosis needs a ledger to check against: for every cannot-not this framework has stated, what it stands on. That ledger is set up here.

The First Critique set up a ledger of the same kind in its chapter On the Postulates, and said plainly what it is for: "The format is the counting-house honoring its word: registration proves none of them; registration makes each stand at the position it has earned."[^c4-fc-fmt] To register a claim is to write it into the register and state where it stands. Registration does not testify for the claim.

Each entry states four things:

  • content — what it claims;
  • status — axiom, postulate, definition, or derived from other entries;
  • source of warrant — derivable, undertaken by someone, or merely a hypothesis; the First Critique calls the undertaken kind "registered";
  • opponents and branches — whether an intelligible opposing position can be imagined, and whether the alternative branch is inhabited.

Two columns are added. One is dependencies: which other entries the entry's warrant draws on. Only with this column can one work out, when a postulate is replaced, which entries lose their footing along with it. The First Critique also wrote down the column's limit: "the dependency list is a registered claim, not an omniscient readout."[^c4-fc-dep] To lose footing is not to become false. It is to be left unsettled for now, to be reconciled again under the new posits. A postulate's entry also states its propagation: if it is replaced, which judgments lose their footing with it.

The other is domicile: in which series of this framework, and in which section, the entry was laid down, and what it calls itself there. The register of Methodology 0 (second edition) already has this column, headed "Home / place of statement"; Paper 4 writes to the same format: "The zero point is shared; the methodology series' postulates are cited by domicile; this series' postulates are domiciled in this series."[^c4-m0-hj] This book records domiciles and gives no entry one. It is a critique: it examines the entries and records them, and holds none of them itself. Nor does a domicile exempt an entry from examination. Whatever series it is written into, it goes through the four columns all the same.

This book does not divide its register into regions quite as the First Critique does, and says so first. The First Critique's register has five regions: the axiom region, the postulate region, the branch-postulate region, the limit-name region (for names such as the thing-in-itself and Hundun, chaos — names given after the fact to what intake leaves unexhausted, adding no content), and the derivation-and-identification region.[^c4-fc-zones] Three of these are used here — the axiom region, the postulate region, and the derived region (that is, the derivation-and-identification region) — and three are new:

  • Definition region. The First Critique's register has no such region; the register of Methodology 0 (second edition) has a Definition row.
  • Branch region. The First Critique's branch-postulate region registers branch postulates that can be undertaken and replaced, such as the law of contradiction and the law of excluded middle. The branch region here registers alternative branches that are already inhabited. The names are close; what they register is not the same.
  • Region of laws of this branch. The First Critique uses the name "a law of this branch" in one place only, to give the signature doctrine its status — the doctrine that every philosophical judgment has an actual bearer.[^c4-fc-sig] Making it a region is this book's own step. A law of this branch is a law that those who stand on a branch keep in the name of "we"; it is not a law for everyone.

No limit-name region is set up here. The fourth limit-name, domiciled by Paper 4, is recorded as written at the end of Section 2.

Definitions need one more word. A definition is the easiest place for checking to lapse: it claims nothing, so there seems to be nothing to oppose. That is not so. Methodology 0 (second edition) registers opponents to its definition of the source of Negativa, and writes: "The dispute lies in the definition itself."[^c4-m0-def] So each definition here also states two things: the alternative definitions, and the cost of choosing each. Each is also marked as not evidence. A definition says what a word refers to here, not who falls under it. To observe from outside, one needs a separate proxy, and must state that the proxy is fallible — which is how Economics P3 used behavioral output as its proxy, in Chapter Three's case of the false claim of a human type. The costs given below for the alternative definitions are this book's analysis.

2 · The Register

Axiom region: nothing added.

There is only one zero point: 非不能非, the denial of Negativa cannot avoid enacting it. Methodology 0 (second edition) writes: "The axiom has no home series. Its warrant hangs on the act of denial itself"; and: "The axiom forbids one thing only: denying that there is Negativa. It does not forbid the negation of a negation." It also "does not warrant a motor": whether there is a next cut is not decided by the axiom.[^c4-m0-ax] So what it constrains is the single act of denying; it tells no one what to do. The in-domain zero point of the Introduction is not in this region; its place is Section 4 of this chapter.

Definition region · Borrowed. The entries below are borrowed from the framework's series; each notes its domicile.

15DD, non dubito. The Methodology Overview V3.1: "15DD Non dubito: 'Not only I am an end.' Acknowledging that the other is also an end in itself. But 15DD is unidirectional — I acknowledge you, but you have not chiseled me back." The revision note of the same edition: "15DD is one subject's not doubting that the other is an end in itself: a cut that this subject makes, not a norm holding horizontally between subjects. This edition settles the name as non dubito; 16DD remains mutual non dubito."[^c4-v31] The "acknowledging" in the quotation speaks of the posture of not doubting that the other is an end, and in this book's division it falls on the side of adoption: adoption is taking non dubito as a premise of one's own law (Introduction, Section 4). When 15DD is operating and the act in question is aimed at another person's will, there is a concession as well — the concession that he can say no. In what 15DD does that is aimed at no one's will, such as avoiding harm to someone who is not present, there is non dubito and no such concession. Whether non dubito is a passing appearance or has already been set as a premise of one's own law — only the latter is called adoption — is recorded elsewhere (Chapter Five, Section 2).

The definition has a consequence. While operating at 15DD, a person does not doubt that the other is an end; when two people both operate so, they coincide on this point. The coincidence is given by the definition. It is analytically true and carries no new information, so it cannot be used to issue commands to anyone else (Chapter Three, the case of the false claim of the lateral).

There are two alternative definitions. One makes it a capacity: Paper 5 writes the "the other is an end" dimension as "the capacity to empathize with and acknowledge others' cannot-not," which can be higher or lower.[^c4-p5-axis] The cost of choosing it is that 15DD becomes something that can be graded; and if a measurement of capacity is then used to decide who counts, grounds for the measurement and for inclusion must be supplied separately. Paper 5 itself records the tension: "the same matter given two routes, one requiring no qualification and one requiring a capacity."[^c4-p5-ten] The other alternative is the old names: "ethics," "one-sided acknowledgment," "sociality." The Methodology Overview V3.1 has already merged them into one, on the ground that "one layer carried three names," and because the name "ethics" will be read as a norm holding laterally between persons (see the entry on ethics below).

Not evidence: the definition does not tell you who is at 15DD.

End in itself. One name, two offices. Paper 4: "'End in itself' carries two discussions in this series, and this paper separates them by address." The standing sense — "The non-meritocratic normative standing of every person in the domain" — is domiciled in Paper 4, §2.3. The realization sense — "How far individual, relational and institutional conditions actually support subjecthood" — is domiciled in Paper 2, §6.3.[^c4-p4-25] Paper 4 states the cost of merging them into one sense: "Taken as one object of judgment, the two collide." Paper 4 also limits itself. That Paper 2's text can be read apart in this way does not mean that Paper 2's older sentences have all been brought into line with the split: "This paper does not let a split of names supply either side's missing argument."[^c4-p4-25b]

Not evidence: the two names do not tell you who has standing, or how far anyone's subjecthood has been realized.

Colonization. Two definitions stand side by side. Moral Law P9: "Colonization is the structural operation of replacing another's own law with one's own purposes." Value Theory P3, V27: "Colonization is this structural state: a content stands, in the bearer, registered as constitution already completed, while that constitution was not made in the bearer's own law, and what occupies that position is an external end." V27 adds expressly: "It is not a class of action but a state."[^c4-col2] One puts colonization in the genus of acts, the other in the genus of states. Action Theory P2 and P6 say that this system defines colonization "in a single sentence," and cite only Moral Law P9;[^c4-col-one] that is recorded as written, and read as imprecise. Using only one of the two has a cost either way. With the act definition alone, there is nowhere to put the case that has no actor and only an occupied position — the self-colonization of Value Theory, where "the source remains outside, while the running need not be." With the state definition alone, the act by which an institution puts its own purposes in place of someone else's law is not what the definition is about, and can only be described backwards, from the state it produces. Each is used at its own address: Chapter Three's diagnoses use the state definition; to say what an institution is doing, one uses the act definition.

Not evidence: neither definition tells you where colonization has taken hold. To say that it has, at some place, one must read the structure there, and the reading is fallible (Chapter Three, Section 7).

Ethics. Not used as a name for 15DD; the Methodology Overview V3.1 has withdrawn it. Rights Theory Paper 0 uses "ethics" for the side that is deleted: "a horizontal duty is the pushing-outward of an inner law into a demand upon others, structurally an overreach." One's Own Law says: "If anyone asks what SAE's ethics is, the answer is one word: none."[^c4-ethics] Both speak of the lateral side, in agreement with V3.1. Uses elsewhere are recorded in their own sense: Paper 4, writing of the relational layer, says "Where each person actually draws the line is an ethical decision"; Paper 5's "personal ethics" uses the word the opposite way from One's Own Law (recorded in Chapter Three). The Methodology Overview V3.1 also writes: "Subsequent papers that refer to 15DD as 'ethics,' 'one-sided acknowledgment,' or 'sociality' should be brought into line." That is the Overview's self-description. This book reads accordingly, and does not press the papers to change their texts. Alternative: go on calling 15DD "ethics." Cost: the name will be read as a norm holding laterally between persons — the reason V3.1 withdrew it. Not evidence: this entry governs the name only.

One's own law. Value Theory P1, V8: "One's own law (自己的法) is the structural form by which a bearer legislates for itself, unfolds, and revises. It is a form, the structure by which the bearer sets and changes, not some one piece of concrete content."[^c4-v8] There are two alternative definitions. One is Kant's autonomy, in which the form of universal law is a constitutive condition of autonomy. Action Theory Paper 0 says plainly: "To say that one's own law is Kant's autonomy would be a mistake." The Kantian branch is registered in the branch region. The other reads one's own law as whatever one wants at the moment. The cost of the first: the form of universal law becomes a constitutive condition of every law of one's own; if Kant's actual branch of duties of right is taken along with it, it cannot stand together with postulate 2, no lateral ought (branch region, the Kant row); whether the form of universal law taken alone entails the negation of postulate 2 would have to be shown separately. The cost of the second is the one Value Theory P1 names where it sets up V8's distinctions: the three concepts must be pried apart, "or the unifying line will at once slide into subjective arbitrariness."[^c4-v8b]

Not evidence: the definition does not tell you what is in anyone's law.

Position-conditioned cannot-not. Action Theory P1, D5: "A cannot-not always hangs on conditions, and has two layers. The shared layer is mostly about how people are regarded, and cannot be cancelled while one stands in the position of taking oneself and others as ends; a person can leave this position in a given matter, but cannot stand in it and cancel it at once." The shape is borrowed from the Conflict Treatise, and Action Theory records that "this series applies the shape to action in general, and answers for that step on its own account."[^c4-d5] The shape — two layers: the shared layer cannot be cancelled while one stands in the position; the case layer is the person's own judgment, and can be revised — is entered in the definition region. Applying the shape to action in general is a posit Action Theory undertakes on its own account, and is entered in the postulate region as the first row of postulate 5; that one can leave, matter by matter, is entered in its second row. D5's own sentence states the shape together with leaving. Here they are entered apart, because the half about leaving has opponents of its own. The alternative statements are all in the table of the Introduction, Section 2, Third: the unconditional form, the ontological form, the constitutive-condition form. Choosing the unconditional form costs exactly Chapter Three's case of the false claim of the unconditional. Choosing the ontological form costs this: leaving can only be read as degradation, a reading the Introduction has already dismantled. Choosing the constitutive-condition form costs this: every counterexample is assigned to what lies outside 15DD, and the claim can no longer be tested (Introduction, Section 2, Second).

Not evidence: the shape does not tell you who is standing in the position now.

Definition region · This book's tool-words. These words are this book's own. The three preceding chapters explained them one by one; they are gathered here:

Word Meaning in this book See
CostThe part a cut leaves on the cutter's own books, not taken in; not a consequence; never compared across personsChapter Two
The four-column addressThe address of an action: whose law; which position; which matter, which moment; which branchChapter Two, Section 5
False claimingA law or a cannot-not displaying a credential of validity it has not earnedChapter Three, Section 1
The in-domain zero pointEvery act whose object is another's will concedes, in the act, that the other can say no; within such acts, this concession cannot be denied in a performatively consistent wayIntroduction, Section 4; this chapter, Section 4
ConcessionThe concession made by the act, in acts of this kind; what is conceded is a capacity, not a standing; it is not the result of a testIntroduction, Section 4
AdoptionTaking non dubito as a premise of one's own law; it passes through no test, so refusing adoption is not a negation reached after a test eitherIntroduction, Section 4
Two sets of booksThe books kept by one who denies that others can say no while his acts are busy handling that very capacity: one lists things, the other lists persons to be guarded against. Whoever concedes without adopting keeps one set of booksIntroduction, Section 4; Chapter Three, Section 4
The ladder of precautionsReading how a denier guards against people, to see what his acts have concededChapter Three, Section 4
The stamp of performative inconsistencyStamped on a position that denies others can say no while its acts keep handling that capacity: intelligible, inhabited, yet out of joint with its own acts; not a verdict of invalidityChapter Three, Section 4
Ruling-outWhen a necessary condition of this operation, together with its not being met, has been independently verified, one rules that the person is not operating at 15DD at this moment; where only traces of risk are seen, it is a direction report; revocable; no absorbing stateChapter Three, Section 7; Chapter Six, Section 6

These are this book's tool-words; their alternatives and costs are in the chapters cited, and they too are not evidence. One last word: performative inconsistency does not occur in the corpus, and the First Critique's criterion always says "performatively consistent." The stamp is this book's own term.

The words "acknowledge" and "recognize," with their nouns, appear in this book only inside quotations. They are how the English pages of the corpus render 承认, the word the Chinese text of this book likewise keeps to quotations; the same Chinese word comes out as "acknowledge" on some pages and "recognition" on others, so the English word a quotation happens to carry decides nothing. Inside quotations, the word is read by its source. In Action Theory Paper 0 ("the antecedent is a recognition, not a willing"), in Value Theory P1, V14 and in P6, and in the Methodology Overview V3.1, §1.7, wherever it speaks of standing as an end or of the posture of non dubito, it falls on the side of adoption; where an act aimed at another's will is involved, there is a concession as well. We do not split every such passage in advance into two acts occurring at once. In Paper 6, "acknowledgment" presupposes an inspection process, and is read in that sense. In Paper 4, the word belongs to the side of relational achievement — "what recognition completes is realization, not standing" — and is likewise read in its own sense. In Paper 5 the word names a capacity at the relational layer, and does not belong to the case in Chapter Five.[^c4-ack] This division governs only this book's own vocabulary; it does not define terms for Paper 5's theory.

Postulate region: seven entries.

Status first. In their own series, most of these seven are not called postulates. Value Theory, Meaning Theory, Action Theory and Rights Theory Paper 0 never use the word for anything of their own; their entries call themselves "propositions." Three places say their entries are derived. Rights Theory Paper 0 says that "self-legislation and the four theorems all follow from it as lemmas and theorems, not as axioms"; Moral Law P1 says that "the four can themselves be proven… not assumed as premises"; Value Theory P1, V1 says that non-ranking is "the necessity of conceptual entailment."[^c4-self]

This book still registers them as postulates, for three reasons. First, Negativa does not entail them; Methodology 0 (second edition) has already revised its old wording to: "Everything else is a postulate or theorem within its series."[^c4-m0-rest] Second, for each of them an intelligible opposing position can be imagined that holds its ground. That is exactly the ruler by which the First Critique tells axiom from postulate: "can an intelligible and performatively consistent opposing position be formed? — where 'intelligible' does not mean agreed with, but that such an opponent could really have existed in human history."[^c4-fc-crit] Third, they are independent of one another (Section 3), and only this reason separates them from the derived region. Section 3 gives candidate configurations; the item-by-item verification is in Appendix III, on the annex ledger; until it is settled, their independence stays at the level of candidates. Any entry shown to follow from the others together with the definitions is re-registered as derived or as a definition; an entry for which no configuration can yet be found is marked pending, and is not re-registered. To register them as postulates is not to demote them. A postulate is a posit someone openly undertakes; it can be refused, and whoever refuses it lives on another branch. In the master table of the register, the warrant column reads "undertaken," with the undertaker entered, for now, as the series in which the domicile lies; that the entries call themselves propositions is recorded as written. Where a paper's self-description differs from this book's registration, this book does not change it for them.

① Not doubting that the other is an end. Entered in two rows, with different domiciles.

The standing row: a human being is an end in itself.

  • Content: Paper 4's Postulate One, "A human being is an end in itself." Its technical sense is "the non-meritocratic normative standing of every person within the domain of objects — not a reward for acquiring some capacity or receiving some recognition."
  • Status and warrant: a postulate, openly undertaken — "It is not derived from the operation of negation, and it does not pose as the result of any physical test."[^c4-p4-23]
  • Normative consequence: "Within an already specified domain of objects, no appraisal of capacity or of recognition issues a certificate of standing as an end." Paper 4 notes that this is Postulate One's "normative consequence in this paper, not a premiseless performative theorem." The domain of objects is "individual human beings now living."[^c4-p4-24]
  • Opponents and branches: "Whoever refuses Postulate One domiciles a branch of his own, and that branch may be inhabited. He does not thereby lose the capacity to think or to negate, and this paper does not judge him a bad person." There is more than one intelligible opponent. The First Critique lists three: "those who define the human as instrument, as pure process, or who refuse the category 'end' altogether — each inhabiting its own geometry." Paper 4 names, besides, the branch that fixes standing by a capacity threshold.[^c4-opp1]
  • Two neighboring headings: Value Theory's "standing as an end" and Rights Theory Paper 0's "basic rights." This book does not presuppose that the two have the same domain or the same full content. The case on the Value Theory side is called colonization, the one on the Rights Theory side violation. Each keeps its own definition; they can intersect, and they are not two faces of one thing (Chapter Three, Section 3).
  • A difference in terms: Value Theory P1, V14 does not state it as a postulate — "this other's being an end is a fact prior to my recognition, already so." The same section has a modal blank as well: "modal blank says this paper does not claim it inevitable to every fourteenth-level running."[^c4-v14] What is left blank is the modality of occurrence of the corresponding other-directed running, not the factual assertion that the other is an end; the two are entered separately. Papers 4 and 6 undertake it as a postulate, Value Theory states it as a fact. The terms differ; they are recorded as written, and this book does not align them.
  • Propagation: Paper 4's own dependency transmission ledger states that if Postulate One is replaced or refused, what must be re-audited is "This paper's ground for 2.4 and the guard rail," and what is not thereby cancelled is "That judging occurs; acts of negation; the truth or falsity of physical research."[^c4-p4-tbl] In this book, the step of Chapter Five's verdict that speaks of standing loses its footing with it. The in-domain zero point and the concession are not among what is affected; they cite no postulate.

The posture row: non dubito.

  • Content: Paper 6's Premise V, "non dubito toward all subjects as ends" — "'Non dubito' is a default posture: I do not need you to prove you are an end; I default to assuming you are." It is not a conclusion reached after a test: "'Non dubito' is not 'acknowledgment' — 'acknowledgment' presupposes an inspection process."
  • Status and warrant: "Standing: a postulate, borne rather than derived. It can be refused; the branch that refuses it is habitable, and this paper does not strike that branch off the register."
  • Proviso: "that default is not withdrawn on account of that subject's conduct"; "There is no finally condemned evil person, but there are diagnosable evil operations — this paper's proviso therefore judges operations, not persons."
  • Two addresses: Paper 6 separates the framework's non dubito from the subject's, "with different offices, and neither can stand in for the other." This book cites the subject's address: "The posture an individual actually holds."
  • Normative force: Paper 6 does not rule on it. "Normative force is not adjudicated in this paper. This paper does not argue that people ought to hold non dubito… The step from 'cannot be taken in' to 'ought to be acknowledged' is handed to the critique of practice."[^c4-p6v] The First Critique hands over the same step: the validity from "cannot be taken in" to "ought to acknowledge" "must still be obtained separately by the practical critique."[^c4-fc-hand] The item is settled in Chapter Five.
  • Wording: Paper 6 lays down that "'Non dubito' is not 'acknowledgment'," yet the closing sentence of the same entry says "ought to be acknowledged." Recorded as written.
  • Propagation: replace it, and the conditions Paper 6 sets with non dubito as their premise lose their footing, as does adoption in the region of laws of this branch below.

Between the two rows. Paper 4 states that its normative consequence "states what this paper itself does; it is not a claim about what attitude anyone ought to hold"; and "from 'standing does not vary with any test' to 'I must not demand that you prove yourself before I treat you as an end' there is a further step about how persons are to be treated, and this paper does not build it. That undertaking has its own address."[^c4-p4-gap] This book's reading: that address is the posture row, Paper 6's. The gap between the two rows is recorded and not bridged. Whether the gap is real, Section 3 tests with a configuration.

② No lateral ought.

  • Content: there is no duty that one person owes to another; the only duty faces inward.
  • Domicile: Rights Theory Paper 0, M1: "SAE's one duty faces inward — the termination lemma, that one cannot-not legislate only for oneself, the subject's answerability to its own law"; "So SAE has no horizontal duty, no duty a subject owes to others." Value Theory P1, V16 says the same: "no lateral duty holds as before: the root of recognizing the other is wholly within the bearer's own law, not a debt owed the other." Meaning Theory P3, Y25 cites V16, and says that "its ought half belongs to the Moral Law."[^c4-nohz]
  • Self-description: Rights Theory Paper 0 says it is derived from the axiom (see above); Value Theory and Meaning Theory call it a proposition.
  • Opponents and branches: Rights Theory Paper 0 names its opponent: "Western rights theory has a proposition close to an axiom: correlativity. Every right corresponds to a duty." Kant's right and coercion is another branch (branch region).
  • Reading: this book does not discuss the lateral ought; the inside-out ought just is the cannot-not. The "ought half" that Meaning Theory hands to the Moral Law is written, in the Moral Law's own words, as a cannot-not. The "ought" that Moral Law Paper 0 does without is the one "requiring the subject to obey some external rule or internal virtue" — it uses neither the external kind nor the internal — and what it says instead is that "the structure cannot-not be such." Action Theory does not write even "I ought": what is routed to it "is repaid, without exception, in cannot-not and in what is open either way; the series does not write ought."[^c4-ought]
  • Propagation: replace it, and Chapter Three's case of the false claim of the lateral loses its footing; Rights Theory's sentence "The constraint on others is carried not by duty but by the violation-reading and its consequences" must also be re-audited.[^c4-r0-f21]

③ First-person non-substitutability.

  • Content: no outside party can settle, on a person's behalf, what he truly avows. There can be readings from outside, and a reading can be right or wrong; none is a final verdict, and none closes off his own claiming and revision. (The other half — that the outside cannot write it for him — holds analytically, given the definition of one's own law, and stands in the derived region.)
  • Domicile: Value Theory P1, V8: "the first-person's non-substitutability, for whoever it be, value-content cannot be written for it from outside"; Value Theory P2, V18: "Appearance is the candidate position; first-person avowal is the sole constitutive position"; V21: "First-person avowal can be neither conferred by another nor revoked by another."[^c4-fp]
  • Self-description: a proposition.
  • Boundary: Value Theory states that this is a boundary, not a shortfall in what the outside can yet do: "What is externally auditable is whether the candidate position has been occupied and whether the bearer's room for reconstruction and revision is still there; what is not auditable is any adjudication, on the bearer's behalf, of what it truly avows."[^c4-v20]
  • Opponents: Value Theory has drawn them: a regulator who would "claim to see what you really avow"; a person "who says that this was never your own."[^c4-v37] On the side of doctrine, there is a use that, in the name of false consciousness, takes an outside reading of a person as settled; it has been inhabited in history.[^c4-engels]
  • Nearest neighbor: Action Theory P1, D6: "Judgment cannot be done by rules in anyone's place." It governs rules judging in a person's place, not others writing for him. The nearest it comes is its sentence on the feeling of whether something goes smoothly: "still less can it be used to judge others."[^c4-d6]
  • Propagation: replace it, and an outside reading could be final, settling for a person what he truly avows. The rule in Chapter Three's discipline of diagnosis that reads structure and not the inner life loses its ground, and diagnosing colonization no longer needs to read structure. The entry that it cannot be written for him from outside (derived region) is unaffected: it rests on the definition of one's own law.

④ The source is not external.

  • Content: the entitlement by which a content counts as a person's own value is not given by anything outside his own sources.
  • Domicile: the wording is Value Theory P1, V8's: "The self-giving of one's own value is that its source comes-from-not-external, not that it is declared by the subject at will." Three things confer no such entitlement, and they are laid down in two places. Declaration and time, in Value Theory P3, V36: "Declaration produces none: I say this is my value, therefore it is — this step does not hold. Time produces none: I have held it long enough, so it must count as mine by now — this step does not hold either." P4, V46 says it again: "Habituation produces no ownership, long holding produces no avowal, and long interruption revokes none." The external warrant, in P3, V27: "That this content counts as the bearer's value has its standing given by something outside the bearer's own sources: an authoritative pronouncement, an institutional arrangement, a reading issued by another's circuit of ends."[^c4-src]
  • The two places must be kept apart. V36 states that declaration and time "do not fall under this judgment": they are not colonization — "They are merely failed claimings." The external warrant is a branch of colonization.
  • Self-description: a proposition.
  • Opponents: the subjectivism in which declaring makes it so, which is exactly what V8's criterion was set up to block; the view that long holding confers it; the view that authority or institutions confer it.
  • Dependencies: the definition of one's own law.
  • Propagation: replace it, and V36's judgment and the external-warrant branch of colonization lose their footing together.

⑤ The position condition, in two rows.

First row: the shape of the position condition, applied to action in general.

  • Content: every cannot-not in action in general is read in the shape of the position condition: two layers; while one stands in the position, it cannot be cancelled.
  • Status and warrant: a posit Action Theory undertakes on its own account. Setting out D5, Action Theory records that the shape is borrowed from the Conflict Treatise, and that in applying it to action in general "this series … answers for that step on its own account."
  • Opponents: the constitutive-condition form (the Moral Law, current); the unconditional form (Kant). The framework's own older ontological and unconditional statements have been replaced on its own books (see below, on old versions not registered as branches); they are not live opponents.
  • Cost: take it, and a cannot-not holds as uncancellable only inside the position; to someone not standing there it has nothing to say. The cost Chapter Five speaks of falls here.
  • Dependencies: the definition of the position-conditioned cannot-not.

Second row: one can leave, matter by matter.

  • Content: for one who stands in the position of treating people as ends, some things cannot be cancelled; but a person can leave this position in a given matter, and leaving it in one matter does not mean that the person no longer treats people as ends from then on.
  • Domicile: Action Theory P1, §4: "Leaving goes matter by matter: having left in this one matter does not mean he is from then on no longer a person who treats people as ends." "If he really does leave in this way, this series does not judge him; in this one matter, this cannot-not is no longer there for him, and that is all; this series still speaks to him."[^c4-a1-4]
  • Self-description: a proposition, part of D5; for D5's shape, the series says it "answers for that step on its own account."
  • Provenance: on the side of cognition, the First Critique said that a being that chisels and then stops involves no contradiction: "It can stop."[^c4-fc-stop] That is said at another layer; it is recorded here as provenance only, not as domicile.
  • Opponents: the framework's own older statements — Reason Cannot Not's "To exit is to be degraded," One's Own Law's "you have no option of 'not opposing'" — have been replaced on its own books (see the branch region). The outside opponent is the view that moral demands on persons are "inescapable." Action Theory Paper 0 itself records that its "cannot be cancelled" is "close on the surface to the inescapable that Williams criticizes; the difference is that one can leave."[^c4-a0-10]
  • Debt: how "cannot be cancelled" and "can leave" hold at once — Action Theory Paper 0 grants that "a formal account of it can be given separately." This book gives it in Appendix III.
  • Dependencies: the definition of the position-conditioned cannot-not.
  • Propagation: replace it, and the resolution of Chapter Three's first antinomy, and the passage of Chapter Two on stopping and not stopping, lose their footing.

⑥ The gap between law and conduct does not close to zero.

  • Content: no one can accord with 15DD at every moment and in every matter.
  • Status and warrant: a postulate. It is laid down as a matter of fact, not derived from the cognitive side's "the remainder cannot be eliminated"; the two have the same shape, and each has its own warrant (Introduction, Section 3).
  • Domicile: blank. The nearest the corpus comes is three places, each stating one side. First, Moral Law P3, §2.7: "The 15DD legislative subject is not required to be perfect. Reputation does not certify perfection." Second, the latter half of Action Theory P1, D5: "to judge differently from others at this layer, or not to manage it this time, is not to have left the position." Third, D4: the remainder of a judgment "stays, counted neither as settled nor as something that may be cleared away."[^c4-near6] None of the three is the whole of this entry.
  • Opponents: whoever claims to accord with 15DD at all times. Moral Law P3 doubts him too — "Perfection, indeed, would be more suspicious than imperfection" — though its reason for doubt is that such a self "would suggest performance and forgery." This book's reading is that he has the same shape as the completeness-theorist on the cognitive side, and the First Critique's stamp on the completeness-theorist is: "intelligible, with a condition-stamp: he can never confirm success from within."[^c4-opp6] Claims in religious and contemplative traditions to have reached completion or sainthood fall on this side as well, each with its own original qualifications, recorded as written.
  • Propagation: replace it — suppose someone really could accord with 15DD at all times — and the rule in Chapter Three's discipline of diagnosis that levels record operations only loses one piece of supporting evidence: for such a person, recording by the moment and recording by the person would come to the same thing. The rule has other grounds (Chapter Three, Section 7) and is not withdrawn on this account. The case of the false claim of a final verdict reads motive and warrant, and does not hang on this entry. That ruling-out is revocable rests on the possibility that a diagnosis is wrong, and does not hang on this entry either.

⑦ Standing as an end is not graded.

  • Content: at the level of standing as an end, comparisons such as higher, lower and equal have no definition.
  • Domicile: the wording is in Paper 4, §4.3: "This paper admits no grading of standing as an end, and no position on the spectrum constitutes a threshold conferring it (2.4)"; and in Value Theory P1, V3: "The complete subject and the subject-to-be, as bearers of end-value, are of equal standing" (the subject-to-be, in Value Theory, is a bearer who can develop true subjecthood and whose gate is still forming; Chapter Five, Section 2 takes this up in detail).[^c4-rank]
  • Nearest neighbor: what Value Theory P1, V1 declines to rank is value-sources, not standing — "value-sources are structural, differing by structural level, yet they admit no ranking by better or worse." V1 makes "unranked" exact: "the comparison higher, lower, equal simply has no definition at the level of value-standing"; and says of itself: "The necessity here is the necessity of conceptual entailment, not another kind."[^c4-v1]
  • Opponents: V1 itself names one, the misreading "that differing by level means rising by level," and so "the bearer mounting rung by rung"; doctrines that grade persons by virtue, merit or birth have all been inhabited in history.
  • Propagation: replace it, and standing as an end has highs and lows. The standing beat of Chapter Five's first case — Paper 4's "no appraisal of capacity or of recognition issues a certificate of standing as an end" — must be re-audited: once there are grades, there is a scale on which to issue certificates. Chapter Two's rule that cost is not compared across persons, and Chapter Three's rule that persons are not named by level, do not hang directly on this entry. The first rests on cost following each person's own law, the second on levels recording operations only; grading standing would not by itself yield a ruler for comparing costs, or a credential for assigning persons to levels.

The branch region.

This region registers alternative branches that are already inhabited. They are not errors; they are other roads, each with its conditions and its costs. The First Critique says: "a branch can be inhabited without its inhabitant being its builder".[^c4-fc-res] Paper 4 adds a rule, and this region keeps it too: the paper "does not let registering a branch substitute for argument, nor announce by that registration that argument has ceased."[^c4-p4-br]

  • Kant's universal legislation. The First Critique: "In Kant, the form of universal law is not a test appended to self-legislation but a constitutive condition of autonomy: there is only one test, though many maxim-contents may pass it." To withdraw that test's exclusive validity "is a redefinition of autonomy, not a mere loosening of content".[^c4-fc-kant]
  • Right and coercion. In the Doctrine of Right of the Metaphysics of Morals, Kant ties Right to the authorization to coerce (6:231). This branch takes others as ends and, along with that, takes on lateral duties of Right and external coercion. This book does not discuss the lateral ought; the branch is registered as it stands.[^c4-kant-r]
  • The law of not-constructing. For some subjects the self-given law is "precisely not to construct — losing day by day, doing without doing, no chiseling."[^c4-fc-nb]
  • Theoretical solipsism. It lifts no hand, opens no mouth, points at no one. It is performatively consistent and lies outside the scope of the in-domain zero point (Chapter Three, Section 4).
  • Domination that denies the capacity to say no. Intelligible, and historically inhabited. It bears the stamp of performative inconsistency: those who live in it keep two sets of books (Chapter Three, Section 4). The general statement above (not errors, but other roads) does not apply to this row. What cannot stand is its one denial.
  • Candid domination. It concedes that the other can say no; it simply does not take him as an end. Performatively consistent; no stamp. Condition: he takes only himself as an end. Cost: for one who locks others in, Power Theory's reading is that he is himself bound by that locking — back-binding — which at the ρ layer cannot be pressed to zero (Chapter Two, Section 4). That is a structural reading; which item it leaves in his own law is said case by case. For one who locks no one in, Power Theory states that no back-binding arises.
  • Refusers of Postulate One. "Whoever refuses Postulate One domiciles a branch of his own, and that branch may be inhabited."

The standing row has two further kinds of opponent, and they must be written apart.

One kind fixes standing by capacity. Two neighboring positions are in fact at issue here, and they are not the same. What Paper 4 contends with is "a position that fixes standing by a capacity threshold". The potentiality-theory that Value Theory speaks of has its first sense in V3: taking end-value on the strength of a future potentiality. "A subject-to-be does not take end-value by a future potentiality. This must be nailed down, for it is most easily read as a potentiality-theory." V14 speaks of another layer: if displayed capacity were made the way in, "the infant would not pass this judgment, and the potentiality-theory would come back from here".[^c4-pot]

The other kind holds that standing must be granted by others. The corpus has no ready name for this branch; the nearest passages all turn it away as a misreading. Value Theory, V14: "The misreading of recognition as creating end-standing is blocked by the third layer: the other is an end prior to recognition, recognition a response, not a creation." Paper 4, §4.4: "Recognition does not create subjecthood; recognition completes subjecthood."[^c4-grant] One more passage needs a note. In the same section Paper 4 writes: "'The same subject' is a result of external recognition, not an ontological fact." That sentence is about identity, not standing, and does not belong to this branch.

Old versions are not registered as branches. The framework once said, in One's Own Law and Reason Cannot Not, that one "cannot exit". That claim is not registered as a branch. The later Action Theory and the First Critique both say that one can leave, and that one can stop. The First Critique's rule is that "on one's own ledger, when a postulate is pierced, the old version must die and be replaced by revision — replaceability does not exempt from death; it is precisely death's form." The genealogy stays on the books: "Nor is the old record written off: a claim that passed review under the old postulate-set remains a valid record of that station".[^c4-fc-dead] So these are not living branches. They also differ from this register on more than one entry, so they are not used as single-entry configurations either (Section 3). Whether their texts are revised is a matter for each paper.

The region of laws of this branch.

Each entry states five things: whose law it is; its antecedent; what cannot be cancelled; its ground; its cost. The four-column address is added.

Antecedents come in two forms. One is the purpose form: "if this is wanted, then …". The other is the adoption form: "whoever has adopted non dubito as a premise of his own law …". There is also a third kind of sentence, of the shape "once one operates at 15DD, one cannot-not do so". That is the constitutive form. It states a definition, so it belongs in the definition region and is not called a law.

The ground warrants only the link inside the conditional, not the antecedent. It does not say you should want that purpose, nor that you should adopt. Cost is written on both sides: the cost of keeping the law, and the cost of leaving it. To state only the cost of leaving and say nothing of the cost of keeping is the case Chapter Three calls the false claim of costlessness.

Adopting non dubito as a premise of one's own law (adoption, for short).

  • Whose law: that of whoever has adopted non dubito as a premise of his own law, kept in the name of "we".
  • Antecedent: the adoption form.
  • What cannot be cancelled: not requiring another to prove first that he is an end before treating him as one. In Paper 6's own words: "I do not need you to prove you are an end; I default to assuming you are." Nor is the default withdrawn because of a person's conduct. What may be judged is his operations.
  • Ground: the definition of non dubito. To default is to require no proof, and that is what links the conditional. The ground does not warrant the antecedent: whether to adopt remains a choice.
  • Cost: keeping it, one does not withdraw the default. Toward a man who is doing evil, one still treats him as an end and judges only his operations, and that cost falls on the keeper's own books. Leaving it: in that matter, this law is no longer there for him. If in that matter he still aims his acts at the other's will, he still concedes that the other can say no. He keeps two sets of books only if he also denies this.
  • Domicile: Paper 6 (v3), Premise V, at the address of the subject's non dubito. It is recorded apart from postulate 1 of the postulate region. There, what is recorded is a postulate; here, it is a law that a person keeps in the name of "we".
  • Four-column address: whose law — that of the one who lays it down; which position — 15DD; which matter, which moment — every matter in which he has adopted; which branch — the branch that has adopted non dubito.

Being interrogated must not stop (purpose form).

  • Law: if the quality of the account and an increase in gain are wanted, being interrogated must not stop.
  • Source: this sentence does not occur in the published First Critique. The two words of the antecedent are defined there: "the exact content of the gain is: some registered debt is answered; a new, distinguishable, reviewable construct is actually generated and booked; its quality is settled by subsequent review".[^c4-fc-gain]
  • What cannot be cancelled is the channel of outside review. One cannot put forward or reuse a claim on the strength of the quality of one's accounts while placing it, as a matter of principle, beyond outside review. One may leave a particular interrogation unanswered for now, or move to another channel; that is different from declaring that no relevant evidence may ever come in. What is required is a channel, not unlimited presence.
  • Ground: First Critique, On Illusion §3: "Interrogation is compulsory reconciliation: it forces the shower to lay open the holdings, forces the appearing to hand over its index." Why it must be being interrogated, and not self-questioning alone: self-questioning "cannot warrant the discovery of all one's own blind spots; the other's interrogation therefore supplies an independent check that self-questioning cannot supply for itself."[^c4-fc-q]
  • Cost: the First Critique has stated the cost of stopping: "when a construct is continually reused and expanded while long lacking review from outside itself, the conditions of that cumulative feedback are gathering." The cost of keeping it: the accounts must be laid open to others, revisions must be booked, and an old version that has been pierced must die.
  • Domicile: blank. No series has laid it down in this shape.
  • Four-column address: whose law — that of the one who lays it down; which position — not confined to any one level: wherever the antecedent holds, so does the law; which matter, which moment — every time the quality of the account is wanted; which branch — the branch that wants quality in its accounts.

The law has five nearest neighbors. They differ in modality and in object; they are recorded as written, and none is read as its domicile:

  1. Reason Cannot Not, Clause IV: "Reason cannot not be interrogated." That paper says the modal "cannot not" "pulls all four from Kant's sollst (ought) sentence-form to the ontological statement of 15DD-16DD". It is not a hypothetical. Object: reason. Level: cooperation at 16DD.[^c4-rcn4]
  2. The First Critique cites it in On Illusion §3, "taken here in its lowered sense: not enforced, but priced — violation has its cost." The modality drops one step. Object: the one who displays.[^c4-fc-low]
  3. Moral Law P1, the Fourth Theorem: "Cannot-not let one's own recognition structure be interrogated." The modality is a constitutive condition: "Once a Self operates at 15DD, it cannot-not be so; not being so is not operating at 15DD." The object is the "recognition structure": "The whole composed of the subject's radius of recognition, objects of recognition, and modes of recognition."[^c4-ml1-4]
  4. First Critique, On the Postulates §1: "should not is a hypothetical law: if the width of the four corners and the seat of judgment are to be kept, the base is not surrendered whole to rules." The hypothetical form is one the critique laid down for itself. Object: the base. It is a neighbor in form (both are hypothetical), not in content.[^c4-fc-hyp]
  5. Rights Theory P2, §III: "The encounter boundary cannot-not be questioned." Object: the boundary within a space of action that several subjects share. Reason: "boundary collisions cannot be sealed once and for all by a list drawn up in advance".[^c4-r2-3]

The phrase "cannot-not be interrogated" occurs many more times, in Moral Law P2 to P8, Rights Theory P1 and P6, and the War Treatise. These are not recorded one by one.

That this book, too, stands under interrogation belongs to the procedure of critique; it does not rest on this law. The First Critique opens: "A philosophy, once it stands, stands under the interrogation of others — its validity is not proclaimed by its own house, but settled with interrogation as a participant." Where On Genesis closes, it says "the name of critique is warranted not by a verdict on paper, but by a court that never adjourns."[^c4-fc-court]

This settles a question the First Critique left open. In On Illusion it handed over the grounds for being interrogated, and wrote: "Whether 'one ought to be interrogated' can be erected into a law upon this pathology's grounds — that settlement is not in this chapter; what this chapter hands over is the grounds, and the grounds' boundary."[^c4-fc-b6] This book's answer: the law can be erected. What is erected is a law of this branch, for those who want quality in their accounts. It is not a law for every subject.

The derived region.

  • Overlap. When two people both operate at 15DD, each holds non dubito toward the other as an end. This coincidence is given by the definition: it is analytically true and carries no new information. Dependencies: the definition of 15DD. The First Critique's On Genesis records three sources of overlap. One of them, "the shared premise of the acknowledgment layer — common to those who have walked to acknowledgment," can be read alongside this entry.[^c4-fc-ovl] What grows on top of the overlap is the business of Chapter Six.
  • The cannot-not of the shared layer. For one who stands in the position of treating himself and others as ends, certain things cannot be cancelled. Within the position, two statements cannot sit together; this is analytically true. Dependencies: the definition of the position-conditioned cannot-not. The half about leaving matter by matter depends, in addition, on the second row of postulate 5.
  • It cannot be written for him from outside. Whether a content is a person's own value can be settled only by his claiming it within his own law. This is analytically true under the definition of one's own law. Dependencies: the definition of one's own law (Value Theory P1, V8).

The normative subject and the source of ends.

The First Critique's On Jurisdiction left a task-sheet: "the normative subject that can legislate for itself — to the practical critique."[^c4-fc-b5] This book delivers two items on it here.

The normative subject: what is recorded here is an operation, not a person. "Normative subject" names the operation that carries out self-legislation moment by moment and domain by domain. It does not name a class of people (Chapter Three, Section 7).

The source of ends: not adjudicated. Paper 4 marks the bound of the question "why there is subjecthood to speak of at all" with a fourth limit-name, "the source of subjecthood". It states that the name is "a commitment of usage, not a discovery", and that "'Why I undertake that a human being is an end' and 'where subjecthood ultimately comes from' are two questions and are not packed into one name."[^c4-p4-27] The First Critique's On Jurisdiction listed three candidates: "the subject's self-legislation? the mutual recognition of ends? even the reflexive structure itself — does taking oneself as object already make oneself one's end? Listing is where it stops".[^c4-fc-cand] This book records them as candidates and vouches for none. Whichever one is taken up, it is a postulate that someone has to undertake.

One no-entry sign stays up: "to infer 'the subject is its own end' from 'the subject chiseled itself out'" is the eighth case of the First Critique's On Illusion, "the false claim of cross-field validity".[^c4-fc-8]

3 · Independence: Candidate Configurations Drawn from the Corpus

In the First Critique's format, a postulate is independent of the rest when there is a configuration that satisfies all the other entries and fails this one. The axiom is not among the conditions of any configuration.[^c4-fc-ind] This book has one region the First Critique lacks, the definition region, so a configuration must also keep the definitions. An arrangement that keeps the other postulates but breaks a definition cannot show that the entry does not derive from the definitions. A configuration need not be an inhabited branch; it only has to be an arrangement that makes sense. On the practical side, most candidates can be found in the framework's own texts and their nearest neighbors:

  • No lateral ought: not derivable from the rest. The promise form. Making a promise creates a binding owed to the other, and the other can claim it from you. The binding hangs on the position of having promised, and it can also be broken matter by matter, each breach booked. This arrangement can keep all the other entries; it only adds one lateral ought.
  • Action in general hangs on the position (postulate 5, first row): not derivable from the rest. Here the layer concerning persons is a position one can leave matter by matter, while the cannot-nots of all other action hang on no position and are unconditional.
  • One can leave, matter by matter (postulate 5, second row): not derivable from the rest. The vow form. The cannot-not hangs on the position and cannot be cancelled while one stands there, but the position may not be left matter by matter: leave it once and you have left the whole stance. Some in the traditions of the vow keep it this way.
  • Non dubito toward others as ends: not derivable from the rest. Theoretical solipsism keeps all the other entries, yet does not take others as ends.
  • First-person non-substitutability: not derivable from the rest. The regulator Value Theory sketched. He can treat you as an end, lay no lateral duty on you, and let you leave; he need only "claim to see what you really avow".
  • The source is not external: not derivable from the rest. The one who takes long holding as title: "I have held it long enough, so it must count as mine by now". He writes nothing for others and treats people as ends. He has simply treated time as the source.
  • The gap between law and conduct does not close to zero: not derivable from the rest. One who claims to accord with 15DD at every moment can keep all the other entries.
  • Standing as an end is not graded: not derivable from the rest. Ladder ranking: everyone is an end, but some are higher and some lower. This arrangement takes persons as ends, yet grades the ends.
  • Between the two rows of postulate 1. Keep the standing row and drop the posture row. One holds that everyone has standing as an end, yet still requires a stranger to show his credentials before dealing with him. This arrangement tests whether the "further step" Paper 4 speaks of is really missing. This book only tests; it does not build that bridge.

Most of the figures in these arrangements come from the corpus's own pages: the regulator, the one who takes long holding as title, and ladder ranking were all named by Value Theory. To read them as configurations that keep every other entry is this book's reading, and whether they keep each entry is checked in Appendix III. Reading ladder ranking as a configuration, and the three-way split of the solipsist branch, are also this book's readings. The promise form, the configuration for the first row of postulate 5, and the one between the two rows of postulate 1 are this book's proposals; the vow form, read as a configuration, is this book's reading. Kant is registered in the branch region. The framework's old versions also differ from this register on more than one entry, and none of them serves as a single-entry configuration.

One more grade must be kept apart. Within its domain, the in-domain zero point cannot be refused: whoever performs an act aimed at another's will while denying that concession has his own act testifying against him. All seven postulates can be refused. The arrangements above are candidates, and whether each one keeps all the other entries is checked entry by entry in Appendix III. The in-domain zero point enters no configuration; the postulates are separated one by one.

The prose examples are given here. The narrow formal renderings and the item-by-item verification go into Appendix III, The Formal Package of Practical Configurations, in the format of the First Critique's Appendix II. This is a debt on the annex ledger, entered in Chapter Five, Section 4; until it is cleared, the independence shown here stays at the level of candidates. Where verification shows that an entry can be derived from the rest together with the definitions, it is re-registered as derived or as a definition. Where a configuration has simply not been found yet, the entry is marked as awaiting verification and is not re-registered. For the law of this branch that being interrogated must not stop, a configuration will be supplied once it has a domicile.

4 · The Front Door: the In-Domain Zero Point Enters the Register

The Introduction has already set out the in-domain zero point. Every act that takes another's will as its object (domination, coercion, deception, persuasion) concedes, in the act, that the other can say no; within this class of acts, that concession cannot be performatively consistently denied. Chapter Three used it first as a diagnostic, reading only the denier's own books. Here it is entered in the register through the First Critique's front door. Diagnosis comes first and entry second, the same order as in the First Critique, where On Illusion comes before On the Postulates.

The front door is the examination the First Critique set for new candidates: whoever claims that some further entry, like the axiom, cannot be opposed must pass through it. It has three steps. The first is the performative test: "can an intelligible, performatively consistent opposing position be formed?" The second is the reduction test: "open the candidate's performative contradiction and see where the load-bearing point falls." The third is entry into the register.[^c4-fc-gate]

Step one. Within its domain, no intelligible and performatively consistent opposing position can be found. Coerce a man while saying he has no capacity to say no, and the coercion is aimed at exactly that capacity. A stone needs no coercing; you simply move it. There have been people in history who said such things, and many of them. The First Critique wrote long ago that "people who spoke the denial have really existed; what cannot stand is the position, not the person".[^c4-fc-ax] The strongest objection comes from Power Theory P1, which treats "a fully objectified slave" as a state that can be reached. Chapter Three has answered it. Were that point really reached, the only precaution left against him would be the one rung of fencing in; the act would no longer be aimed at his will, and it would already lie outside the domain. What Power Theory P6 says concerns power: "Severing power precisely cannot go that far." Go that far and it is no longer power, only the processing of things (P1 §VI).[^c4-p1-6] Those outside the domain are not opponents. One who never lifts a hand, never opens his mouth, and directs nothing at anyone stands outside it. Step one is passed.

Step two. The First Critique gives four outcomes, each with its disposition. If the load-bearing point is the denier's own cut of demarcation, it is "a rewritten form", only the axiom in another garment. If the load reaches demarcation only by way of some postulate, it is "conditional reduction: its zero-point standing hangs on that postulate". If the load "stands on its own ground, not through demarcation", it is "a new candidate", and if it holds, "the census of uniqueness is updated accordingly". If the load-bearing point is unclear, the disposition is suspension.[^c4-fc-gate2] Checked one by one:

  • Not a rewritten form. The axiom's contradiction runs: you deny, and so you demarcate. The contradiction here runs: you aim your act at his will, and so you have conceded that he can say no. This contradiction concerns the other's "no", not the denier's own cut.
  • Not conditional reduction. In conditional reduction the condition is a postulate. A postulate can be replaced, and when it is, the entries hanging on it lose their footing. Here the condition is a class of acts. An act can only be done or not done; it cannot be replaced. While one performs acts of this class, the concession remains whichever postulate is replaced. The hardest push is this: before an act can be judged to aim at another's will, must there first be a postulate that others have wills? No. A threat or a deception is a threat or a deception because it is aimed at someone who can understand and choose. That presupposition is carried by the denier's own act, whether or not it succeeds; no one undertook it and brought it in. Whether the other actually can, and whether the act succeeded, are judged separately, on the facts. One can be replaced and the other cannot, so the two also behave differently under propagation.
  • Not a new candidate. A new candidate's load "stands on its own ground" and hangs on no condition. As the First Critique writes it, if such a candidate holds, "the census of uniqueness is updated accordingly". That is read here as making it a candidate for a second axiom, whose warrant would have to cross every field (this book's reading). The in-domain zero point's load hangs on a class of acts. Whoever does not perform such acts is outside it without being its opponent, whereas the axiom has no outside: every denial falls within it. The in-domain zero point does not enter the census of axioms, and what crosses everything is still only that one axiom.
  • Not suspension. The load-bearing point is clear: it lies in the kind of act.

It is none of the four. This book adds a disposition: the load-bearing point falls on a class of acts. It does not run through a replaceable postulate, and it does not lie in the denier's own demarcation; the warrant holds only within that class of acts. Disposition: established as an in-domain zero point, with its domain written out; it enters neither the axiom region nor the postulate region. This disposition is a procedure that this book lays down on its own books (self-registered below). It is added to the First Critique's gate and does not alter that gate. How that gate disposes of similar candidates in future is left to it.

This also makes its place under propagation clear. The First Critique says the zero point stands outside the propagation graph whose nodes are postulates: "The performative axiom's warrant cites no postulate; replacing any postulate cannot touch it — no privilege, but the shape of its source of warrant."[^c4-fc-tr] The in-domain zero point likewise cites no postulate. Within its domain, replacing any postulate cannot touch the concession. Only its domain can be touched: whoever does not perform such acts is not inside it.

Step three. The First Critique requires that a candidate be labeled as one throughout the examination: "a candidate entering in the axiom's voice is the false claim of the zero point".[^c4-fc-gate3] Where the Introduction and Chapter Three used it, it served only as a diagnostic tool and read only the denier's own books, and it was said throughout not to be a second axiom. It never appeared in the axiom's voice. Here it enters the register. Status: in-domain zero point.

Only the core is exempt from re-examination at every application: within this class of acts, the concession cannot be performatively consistently denied. The core, and the division that makes this disposition, can still be brought back through the front door for examination. Every application is a judgment: whether this act counts as aimed at another's will, and what this form of precaution shows. Each can be disputed and reviewed, and none borrows the zero point's warrant; to borrow it would be the false claim of the zero point. How the claim that some things cannot be cancelled holds together with the claim that one can leave is given its formal statement in Appendix III.

The entries this chapter holds central are self-registered here. At its close, the First Critique's On the Postulates marked each of its own central entries with its status and dependencies. The same is done here:

  • The in-domain zero point. Status: in-domain zero point, the disposition added at the front door. Dependencies: cites no postulate. What it needs is a judgment of whether an act belongs to this class, made case by case and open to review.
  • The new disposition at the front door. Status: procedure, laid down by this book and added to the First Critique's gate without altering it. Dependencies: the performative test; this reading of the new candidate (step two of this section). Without that reading, the in-domain zero point would be a new candidate, and no disposition would need adding.
  • The distinction between concession and adoption. Status: this book's definition. Dependencies: the concession half rests on the in-domain zero point; the adoption half rests on the posture row of postulate 1.
  • Cost is remainder. Status: this book's definition, the use of the remainder's ineliminability on the practical side. Dependencies: the methodology series' "the remainder cannot be eliminated", as registered in the First Critique's On the Postulates; the residue of the kind in D3 is entered as remainder because the First Critique books the double-hit as remainder (Chapter Two, Section 3). Limits: only the face that falls within the legislator's own law; a single entry can be settled, but the overall ledger never empties; what a given cut has left in his law is said case by case; no comparison across persons.
  • The four-column address. Status: format. Dependencies: the four columns of the First Critique's On Jurisdiction.
  • Ruling-out. Status: procedure, laid down by this book. Dependencies: independent verification of some necessary condition together with its not being met (Rules of the House, Nine; Chapter Six, Section 6, lists where these conditions are drawn from); the ladder of precautions supplies material only. Limits: revocable, because a diagnosis can be wrong; where only traces of risk are seen, the result is a direction report.
  • The discipline of diagnosis. Status: procedure. Dependencies: First Critique, On Illusion §4; postulate 3.
  • The format of the region of laws of this branch. Status: procedure. Dependencies: the status-name the First Critique gave to the signature doctrine.

The inferences this book lays down for itself elsewhere in the volume are registered here as well, each with its status, dependencies and limits:

  • The ladder of precautions (Chapter Three, Section 4). Status: this book's reading. Dependencies: the in-domain zero point. Limits: the rungs are types of response that the act draws on, not a complete measure, and they are not ranked; what the other can actually do is checked separately, case by case.
  • The three marks of autonomy (Chapter Three, Section 6). Status: the definition of autonomy given by this book's branch. Dependencies: the distinction between revising and dropping (Introduction, Section 1); the law of this branch that being interrogated must not stop. Limits: it makes no false claim to be the universal definition of autonomy; the branch that takes the unrevisability of a law as fidelity lives elsewhere.
  • The three-way split of the solipsist branch (Chapter Three, Section 4). Status: this book's reading. Dependencies: the in-domain zero point; the stamp of performative inconsistency. Limits: On Genesis registered a single branch. The three-way split here is useful only for where the stamp can land, and it does not alter that branch's registration.
  • The configuration between the two rows (Section 3 of this chapter). Status: a configuration proposed by this book. Dependencies: the division of postulate 1 into a standing row and a posture row; its independence awaits verification in Appendix III. Limits: it tests only whether the further step Paper 4 speaks of is really missing, and it does not build that bridge.
  • What lies beyond the overlap is borne by external constructs (Chapter Six, Section 4). Status: an architectural claim this book assembles from parts. Dependencies: the four grounds listed in Chapter Six, Section 4. Limits: it proves neither that the constructs are legitimate nor that they work, and it draws no blueprint.
  • The cut made in V-4 (Chapter Six, Section 6). Status: this book's cut. Dependencies: Action Theory Paper 0, §9.3, "what is handed over here is the side of it that belongs to the critique of judgment". Limits: the object of the original item belongs wholly to the critique of judgment; the original does not say what its Second Critique address refers to.
  • Where the historical layer of persons and groups goes (Chapter Six, Section 7). Status: a linkage this book proposes. Dependencies: the original question in Paper 6, §7, item 3. Limits: the original paper did not take on this part of the work.
  • The candid dominator's cost (Chapter Three, Section 4; Chapter Six, Section 3). Status: a structural reading made on the model of Power Theory's back-binding. Dependencies: the doctrine of back-binding in Power Theory P1 and P6; row E of the table in Chapter Two matches this book's cost to the ρ layer by kind, not entry by entry. Limits: it holds only when he locks the other in; which item falls within his own law is said case by case; it cannot say that he is wrong.

Sixteen entries, each with its columns. One sentence of the First Critique holds for this book as well: "a register that could not register itself would fail on its first page."[^c4-fc-self]

Laying out the ledger has made clear only where each entry stands. By what a law binds the one who made it is the next chapter's business.
