Chapter Five · On Validity

1 · The Question: By What Does a Person's Own Choice Bind?

Action Theory left this question at this book's door: "A cannot-not as a person's own choice, with its standing, its boundaries and its relation to universality, is the business of the Second Critique; the cannot-nots set out in these nine papers, and their standing, will be what the Second Critique examines." The first sentence of the same paragraph states its own measure: "This series writes how one acts on a cannot-not, and does not prove whether it ought to be so".[^c5-a0-92]

At the same point Kant sought no deduction for the moral law; he said that the consciousness of it is a fact of reason (Introduction, Section 3). This book posits no fact here. What it puts in place are three things, each of a different kind:

  • a postulate, openly undertaken — a human being is an end in itself — which governs standing;
  • an in-domain zero point — an act that takes another's will as its object has conceded that he can say no — which governs concession;
  • a choice — making non dubito a premise of one's own law — which governs adoption.

The first case — the first full hearing that the First Critique handed over to this book — has to set each of the three in its place. The answer to the opening question lies in that case.

2 · The First Case

The matter of the case. The case the First Critique left reads, in its own words: "even if the structural judgment stands, the validity from 'cannot be taken in' to 'ought to acknowledge' must still be obtained separately by the practical critique — the first full hearing of that critique, and its case file, lie here."[^c5-fc-case5] First, where it is lodged. The case sits at the 16DD station, and what it examines is an argument of Methodology 00: in the structure where two independent subjects meet, B cannot take A in, and therefore "must recognize" A. The step from 14DD to 15DD — from "I am an end" to "the other too is an end" — the First Critique graded only as "finite exit, neighboring branch on record," and opened no case on it.[^c5-fc-15]

In taking up the case, this book reshaped it into two questions. If the other's being an end is a fact, can it be a reason for a person to enter 15DD? If it is a postulate, who undertakes it? The reason for the reshaping: this book does not discuss the lateral ought, so if "ought to acknowledge" is addressed to everyone, it is not examined here; whatever answer remains can lie only in a person's own law. The original question must be answered too; it comes later in this section.

The file. In this framework, "the other is an end" is said in several ways.

  • As a fact. Value Theory: "this other's being an end is a fact prior to my recognition, already so, not a thing my recognition made an end."[^c5-v14a]
  • As a postulate. Paper 4's Postulate One, "A human being is an end in itself," openly undertaken and refusable; Paper 6 registers non dubito as a postulate; the First Critique calls it "replaceable," "a normative self-interpretation, not an empirical definition of the species."[^c5-post]
  • As a definition. The Methodology Overview defines 15DD by "Not only I am an end."
  • As a choice. Action Theory: "A cannot-not as a person's own choice".
  • As not a willing. Action Theory: "for the layer that cannot be cancelled while one stands on the premise, the antecedent is a recognition, not a willing."[^c5-a0-32]
  • As a structural cannot-not. Methodology 00: "The 'must' in 'must recognize' is structural 'cannot not,' not ethical-imperative 'should'"; it turns "humanity as end-itself" into "a necessary corollary of asymmetric mutual causation structure at the 16DD position," and adds a qualification of its own: "accepting this defense path requires accepting Methodology 0's sole axiom (Negativa) as premise."[^c5-m00]
  • As a direction. To Forget the Self: "not a moral command but the direction that naturally emerges after completing observation and illumination".[^c5-fs]

On Kant's side there are two accounts as well. The Groundwork notes that the step from me to others is put forward as a postulate, its grounds deferred to the final section; the second Critique calls the consciousness of the moral law a fact of reason and seeks no deduction for it from any prior data of reason (Introduction, Section 3). Each has its own object and its own office; they are not to be summed up as two names for one proposition.

The verdict: Concession is unavoidable; adoption can be refused. It comes in four beats.

One: standing. Standing comes first. It is registered as a postulate, and its domicile is Paper 4's Postulate One (Chapter Four, postulate 1). It is derived from no capacity: "Within an already specified domain of objects, no appraisal of capacity or of recognition issues a certificate of standing as an end"; and the guard rail says that "impaired capacity, coerced change, or the absence of others' recognition does not constitute a ground for revoking standing as an end."[^c5-p4-stat] That the other can say no and can call out are visible capacities; they are not the way in to standing. Value Theory wrote this down itself: "one may not judge whether the other is an end by observing that the other has its own end-structure."[^c5-v14b] Value Theory's word "fact" is recorded as its self-description; this book does not rest standing on that word, nor rewrite it on Value Theory's behalf.

Two: concession. An act that takes another's will as its object concedes, in the act, that he can say no; within acts of this kind, the concession cannot be denied in a performatively consistent way. This is the in-domain zero point, entered in the register through the front door in Chapter Four. What is conceded is a capacity, not a standing. The evidence lies in the denier's own books: read how he guards against people. Chapter Three's ladder of precautions is cited here, not retold.

Three: diagnosis. A man who denies that others can say no, yet acts upon their will, displays a thing in his books and holds a person to be guarded against. That is false claiming, and his own precautions are the witness (Chapter Three). The man who concedes that others can say no and simply does not treat them as ends is not in this beat: he keeps one set of books.

Four: adoption and law. Adoption is making non dubito a premise of one's own law. This step is a choice, and it can be refused; the First Critique registers it as a "finite exit," with the neighboring branch inhabited. Only once a person has adopted is there an inside-out cannot-not in his law. The first theorem of Moral Law Paper 1 states exactly this: "Cannot-not recognize the specific other as End, irreducible to means";[^c5-ml1] the "recognize" in the quotation speaks of treating the other as an end and falls on the side of adoption; this cannot-not stands only in the law of the one who lays it down. Value Theory says that this theorem and its own "remainder and response" are "of one source, of one reference";[^c5-v14c] this book places the "cannot-not" half of the theorem on the side of adoption, while the standing half stays with the postulate. Rights Theory reaches the same theorem from the symmetric side and limits itself in one sentence: the theorem "is confirmation, not the ground of this paper's proposition, and it holds only for subjects running at the 15DD position".[^c5-r2-2]

Value Theory left the modality of this step blank: "Whether this remainder-content has the modality of worldly necessity, operational necessity, settling in this universe, or suspension, this paper does not adjudicate".[^c5-v13] This book reads it this way: the concession half is, within its domain, a performative necessity; the adoption half is a choice. That is this book's reading; it does not settle Value Theory's account for it.

The two questions of the reshaped case.

If the other's being an end is a fact, can it be a reason for a person to enter 15DD? It can be one reason within his own judgment; it cannot adopt for him. To infer "ought to adopt" directly from a fact is to carry one field's verdict into another field and issue it there as usual — the eighth type of the First Critique's On Illusion, the false claim of cross-field validity. And besides, the first person cannot be written by another: however true the fact, it cannot write his law (Chapter Four, derived region). What makes 15DD a line in his law is adoption — though a passing 15DD operation can occur without adoption: To Forget the Self says that the direction "naturally emerges," and Moral Law P7 records the "15DD moment."

If it is a postulate, who undertakes it? The standing row: Paper 4 undertakes it openly within its domain of objects. The posture row: Paper 6 undertakes it at two addresses, the framework and the subject. Brought down to a single person, the one who undertakes it is whoever has adopted it, in the name of "we." The First Critique said: "undertake an existing philosophy, and by the undertaking it becomes one's own".[^c5-fc-own] The critique does not undertake it: a critique only examines and records.

The original question. Read Methodology 00's argument in its own words: "'Must' here is structural 'cannot not,'" and its object is the end in itself — "B must recognize A as a unit of existence as end-itself"; it takes care to add that this is "not a matter of B's capacity." This book takes in only the first half: A is un-enclosable, B cannot take him in — the two positions do not substitute for each other — recorded as Methodology 00's structural judgment. If B's act takes A's will as its object, this book reads concession there separately, from the in-domain zero point; that condition on the act is not supplied by the sentence about un-enclosability. The second half, from un-enclosability to treating the other as an end in itself, Methodology 00 calls a necessary corollary; this book does not take it in as necessary, and reads it as adoption. As for the passage from "cannot be taken in" to "ought to acknowledge": if the "ought" is addressed to everyone, it is the lateral ought, which this book does not discuss; if it runs inside out, it is the cannot-not that follows adoption. At neither end is there an inference that can carry validity from "cannot be taken in" to "ought to acknowledge." The First Critique's "obtained separately" has its answer in this book: validity is obtained by whoever has adopted, within his own law; a critique cannot obtain it for anyone. Methodology 00 calls "humanity as end-itself" a "necessary corollary"; Paper 4 says that Postulate One "is not derived from the operation of negation." The two differ; both are recorded as written. Methodology 00 also says that every defense has a starting point and that "starting points can be rejected," "Negativa is the sole axiom" included; this book follows the First Critique's reading, on which Negativa is the one thing the act of denying it also enacts, and so cannot be refused (Introduction, Section 4). Methodology 00's road does not break at its starting point. It breaks at the step from un-enclosability to the end in itself.

Paper 4 itself says that from standing not varying with any test to "I must not demand that you prove yourself before I treat you as an end" "there is a further step about how persons are to be treated, and this paper does not build it" (Chapter Four, postulate 1). This book does not build that step by inference either: the step across is adoption.

Paper 6's sentence, "This paper does not argue that people ought to hold non dubito,"[^c5-p6v] is put to the first cut at once. If "people ought to hold non dubito" is addressed to everyone, it is lateral, and not discussed; read inside out, for whoever has adopted non dubito, non dubito is a cannot-not in his law. Paper 6 hands this step to the practical critique; this book records that as Paper 6's self-description and enters it among the incoming items of Appendix II; it is not counted as a debt of this book.

By what it binds. The question this chapter opened with can now be answered: a law that a person has laid down for himself — by what does it bind him?

First, what it does not rest on. Not on a fact: from "the other is an end" it does not follow that he ought to adopt. Not on a force applied from outside: no one stands outside his law to enforce it, and this book does not either. Not on a fact of reason: at this point Kant set up a starting point that seeks no deduction and establishes itself; this book sets up none.

It rests on where one stands. To adopt is to have taken up a position: not doubting that others are ends. For one who stands there, certain things cannot be cancelled — not because they cannot be done, but because, once they are done, the two statements, that he stands here and that he cancels this, cannot sit together in one person. Action Theory puts it exactly: "What it states is that two statements are incompatible, not a psychological inability to bring oneself to act."[^c5-why-a041] So the binding of this law does not push at a person from outside; it lies between two statements of his own. As long as he still stands here — still actually holds the premise, not merely says so — those things he cannot cancel. A single obstruction, or a single failure to manage it, does not by itself show that he has withdrawn the premise (Action Theory, D5). Where the binding stops is then plain: where he stands. Where he has left in a given matter, in that matter this line is no longer there for him (Chapter Four, postulate 5, second row); where he still stands, there is no exception.

Is this too light — if a person can cease to stand there whenever he likes, is the law not at his pleasure? That adoption can be refused is not discounted at all. But adoption does not start from empty ground. Before adoption, concession is already there: every time he acts upon another's will, the act has already dealt with the other as someone who can say no. What concession gives adoption is only a starting point, not a reason; no zero point can take the step from concession to adoption for anyone (Introduction, Section 4). Not adopting is an inhabited branch. A person can concede that the other can say no and still not treat him as an end — he keeps one set of books, and where his law stands and what it pays are entered in his own books. Only the one who denies in words that the other can say no, while his acts go on dealing with that very capacity, keeps two sets of books. So whether it is too light must be answered in two parts. For one who stands, the binding is not light at all; it runs as far as where he stands, without exception — he can leave in a given matter, but the leaving goes on the books: that matter is entered in his own books and cannot be treated as though he had never stood. For one who does not stand, this book never had a single ought. The law does not force anyone to adopt; it lets him see what adopting and not adopting each leave in his own books.

Time works the same way. The law binds the person who stands here today; it does not sign for the person of tomorrow — tomorrow he may revise. But revising is not dropping (Introduction, Section 1). Revising keeps the books: what the old law was and why it changed, he says plainly himself. Dropping is quietly setting it down when it suits, with no account kept. The three marks of autonomy given in Chapter Three — able to stop, and yet unceasing; the channel open when interrogated; revising the law with the books kept — say exactly this: in this book's branch, a law binds the one who laid it down until he revises it with the books kept. Keeping the books means being able to say it plainly oneself, not reporting to anyone. Three things are entered apart: leaving in one matter; revising the whole law; still holding the law and not managing it this time. A commitment already made to others is not written off by one act of leaving.

This answer has its cost, and that too is stated up front: it does not bind those who do not stand here. This book has no ought for them. Of those among them who act upon others' will while denying that others can say no, what it can say is that they keep two sets of books. Of the one who concedes that others can say no and simply does not treat them as ends, what it can say is where he stands; if he locks someone in place, Power Theory can read the back-binding he pays. Nor can it stop one who stands from leaving in a given matter; in that matter the law is no longer there for him, and all that can be entered is his own account. That is the cost of being able to leave, and it is a separate entry from the cost of not binding those who do not stand here. Who bears what lies beyond the overlap, Chapter Six says.

Action Theory's two sentences. First, "the antecedent is a recognition, not a willing": in this book's division, the "recognition" in that phrase falls on the side of adoption. What it speaks of is the binding that holds once non dubito has been made a premise of one's own law; it does not imply, in reverse, that every passing 15DD operation has first passed through adoption. Adoption makes non dubito a premise, and the antecedent is not a desired end, so this is not a hypothetical imperative; when an act bears on another's will there is concession as well, handed over by the act, and that is not a willing either. Action Theory wrote it a second time in the same place: "the antecedent of the shared layer is a recognition, not a desire".[^c5-a0-32b] Second, "A cannot-not as a person's own choice": this speaks of the adoption beat. Both sentences hold. The law of this branch in Chapter Four, being interrogated must not stop, is in purpose form; Action Theory's cannot-not is in adoption form. The two kinds of conditional are entered apart and do not cancel each other.

Not the eighth type. Standing is derived neither from a structural fact nor from a capacity; its domicile is a postulate. Concession comes from the form of the act, not from a fact about the other. Adoption comes from a choice. Nowhere in the verdict is a law of "ought to adopt" derived from "the other is an end."

Reach. The verdict of the first case states its reach in three rows, and the three are not one set. The standing row goes by the domain of objects already undertaken — complete subjects, subjects-to-be, persons whose capacity to exercise is reduced. The adoption row goes by those who have the corresponding operation of undertaking. The in-domain diagnosis goes by acts. Rights Theory: "Physiological or cognitive damage can lower exercise-bandwidth without lowering station-thickness."[^c5-r2-1]

By subject-to-be this book means what Value Theory means: a bearer able to develop true subjecthood, whose gate is still forming. Value Theory: "A subject-to-be enters the domain of bearers on the equal standing of already being an end, its negation-gate's function still in genesis. Here in genesis … says the gate's function is still forming, not that the standing as an end is still forming".[^c5-v3] An infant is the commonest example. Paper 4's domain of objects is "individual human beings now living"; in §7.4 it writes the infant as a "critical case." Paper 4 also uses the word "subject-to-be," in another sense: a kind of AI, where "negativity has been confirmed (the scientific gate has been passed), but the status of recognition is uncertain (the ethical gate is suspended)".[^c5-p4-quasi] One name, two offices; recorded as written.

For a subject-to-be, the ladder of precautions must be read at a different place. While he cannot yet say no, the denier's precautions fall on a third person: whom to keep in the dark, whom to keep him apart from, how to forestall others' treating him as an end. What these precautions register first is the third person's position to respond; they do not stand as proof that the infant himself can say no at this moment. The infant's standing is kept by the postulate and needs no proof of capacity found for it.

Paper 4 judges that AI on present classical hardware is not a subject; recorded as written. Paper 4: "Corollary one: negativity is impossible on purely classical hardware. Deterministic systems have no source of ontic randomness and cannot produce a macroscopic remainder. All current AI systems — regardless of their complexity — are not subjects." It calls this kind quasi-subjects: "A quasi-subject is a simulation of the emergent layer, not a realization of subjecthood."[^c5-p4-ai] Paper 4 also keeps "true-subject AI" open as a possible case; recorded as written. Value Theory does not write about AI. Whether such systems fall among what Value Theory calls subjects-to-be is a step this book does not take on the two papers' behalf.

On non-human systems and animals the two papers read differently; recorded as written, not adjudicated. Value Theory places animals in "The fourth case, no gate, as with an animal. It does not bear end-value"; Paper 4 says "Horses and dogs very likely have negativity," judging capacity and relation only, not standing, and states that "If one determination of capacity could bring a system into the domain, that would be capacity conferring standing — precisely the step 2.4 forbids."[^c5-animal] Where the boundary of bearers lies, Chapter Six records as an empty cell.

The boundary of departure is not within this reach. Meaning Theory: "Whether the position has already exited, this paper draws no conclusion in either direction: it does not judge the exit of the position from departure, and it does not infer from an unproved exit that the position continues."[^c5-y30] This book does not judge it either; it draws only three lines not to be crossed. Departure is not fixed by the loss of capacity; it is not fixed by others ceasing to give their approval; it is not fixed by declaration — whether the declaration comes from others or from the person himself. The first two have Paper 4's guard rail upstream. The third has two upstream sources: at the layer of standing, Paper 4 "undertakes non-revocation" within its domain of objects; at the layer of position, Meaning Theory states that "Whoever asserts a final exit bears the burden of supplying the corresponding ground." Value Theory's "Declaration produces none" has the same form and serves only as corroboration. The three lines say that these things are no grounds for revoking standing or ruling departure; they do not stand in for judging whether a person has in fact exited.[^c5-ban3]

The First Critique sent one sentence to be settled here: "a subject who halts this operation no longer realizes its being-an-end through this operation — that is all; this cancels neither its normative standing as an end, nor licenses anyone to treat it merely as a means (the strict argument for this layer belongs to the practical critique)."[^c5-fc-stop] It is argued only as far as it can be argued. The first half: halting an operation does not even amount to a loss of capacity, and under the guard rail still less does it revoke standing — within Paper 4's domain of objects this is the postulate's normative consequence. The second half, "nor licenses anyone": for whoever has adopted, it is a cannot-not in his own law. For one who has not adopted, this book has no lateral ought to say to him. If he denies that the other can say no while acting upon the other's will, what can be diagnosed is his false claim. If he does not deny it, and simply does not treat the other as an end, this book cannot argue this half through to him — it can say where he stands, and, if he locks someone in place, the entry Power Theory reads; it cannot say he is wrong. Strictness reaches only as far as the postulate and the zero point warrant, and no further.

Individual and group. Of adoption in group form, this book writes of one kind only: a person inheriting an adoption a group already holds. The latter half of the First Critique's sentence runs: "a branch can be inhabited without its inhabitant being its builder; and the bearer may be a person, or a community judging jointly by public procedure."[^c5-fc-own] The community as bearer this book does not take up; no address yet (Chapter Six, Section 7).

That a human being is an end can also be seen as a group's choice: those who hold others to be ends will, over history, win more people over to them. This book treats that claim in two halves. The first half, as written above, is taken only as a person's inheriting an adoption the group already holds; and half of its converse holds too: to make the denial that a person can say no into an institution likewise takes a group — the third rung of the ladder of precautions, fugitive-slave laws and passes, is the institutional trace of that group. This does not say that a single person cannot deny; it says that making the denial an institution takes many people maintaining it together. The second half is a historical claim, and it must state its source of warrant (a hypothesis) and its observational proxies; this book examines only its form, under Chapter Three's false claim of convergence, and makes no historical assessment. From outside, all that can be read runs in the direction of ruling-out. The First Critique's words are kept: "we neither promise nor predict the final unification of philosophies".[^c5-fc-unify]

Two items settled with the first case. The First Critique sent two items attached to the first case. They are settled here.

First, the two ways of speaking of 15DD. The First Critique's Appendix IV records that To Forget the Self speaks of 15DD's modality as a direction, and that this and Methodology 00's structural wording are "anti-ought in form," while "the exact relation of the two registers joins the practical critique's first case file."[^c5-fc-b9] Read in the original texts, they and Action Theory's cannot-not are three different sayings, each at its own address:

  • Methodology 00's "cannot not" speaks of the structure in which two subjects meet, and its station is 16DD; the fact that the other cannot be taken in, on which it rests, is recorded as a structural judgment, and connects to concession only through a further act that takes the other's will as its object; and its object, the end in itself, this book reads as adoption;
  • the "direction" of To Forget the Self speaks of how 15DD is reached — it goes on to say "what Kant's Third Critique calls 'purposiveness without purpose'" — and this side belongs to the critique of judgment; here only its destination is recorded;
  • Action Theory's position-conditioned cannot-not falls on the side of adoption.

None of the three says ought, yet they are not one thing.

Second, the normative validity of cultivation. The First Critique says that the sentence "to chisel is to cultivate" has "its normative validity traveling with Case Five to the practical critique."[^c5-fc-b8] The definition of cultivation is in To Forget the Self: "creating conditions for the other's DD sequence to operate without colonization"; it also writes: "Nurturance includes both a negative obligation (I do not colonize you) and a positive obligation (when a third party colonizes you, I do not stay silent). Standing by when the other's remainder is being suppressed is not 15DD … When non dubito stops running, it is no longer 15DD."[^c5-fs-44] The first cut comes first. On its face this is a duty toward others; but the force holding it up is the last sentence, "When non dubito stops running, it is no longer 15DD" — a constitutive conditional, running inside out. This book reads it accordingly: the duty faces inward, and its content concerns others; others cannot use it to make claims on me. Rights Theory states exactly this shape: "The constraint faces inward, and inward the law already includes the other." Action Theory writes it more finely: "inwardly this is a cannot-not"; "Two things still cannot be cancelled: not legislating for him; not closing his ways to refuse, revise, exit and change course"; and "cultivating others does not come at the price of cancelling one's own development."[^c5-nurt] The sentence holding it up is a constitutive conditional, and it carries the flaw the Introduction diagnosed: fail to respond once, and one is ruled out of 15DD. Action Theory's D35 has already lowered it — "That one did not give this time does not by itself show whether the direction was cancelled, the judgment was wrong, or one judged and did not act" — and this book reads it as D35 does. So the normative validity of cultivation is entered in two halves. The half that is the sentence form is the same as in the first case: after adoption, it is an inside-out cannot-not. The half that is positive response: if a person's own law has undertaken it, how "I do not stay silent" is carried out is his to judge, and no rule settles it for him; adopting the default of non dubito alone is not enough to obtain the full validity of this half — this book does not obtain it for free from a thin premise, and records it as still awaiting justification. One more entry: the First Critique states that the "chisel" in "to chisel is to cultivate" is cognitive work, "not the chiseling-down that the theory of power condemns."

3 · The Chain of Oughts

The "oughts" spoken in this framework's series are examined series by series. Each series is written in six columns: the original sentence; modality; source of warrant; address sent to; propagation; disposition. The full series-by-series volume is Appendix I.

The modality column takes the first cut: lateral, or inside-out. The lateral this book does not discuss; it is entered as beyond the range this framework discusses. The inside-out is read as cannot-not, and then sorted against the Introduction's table.

This cut cannot be made by grammatical person. Power Theory wrote that concealed power "lets you reach the conclusion I should go this way on your own" — a first-person "should," with the demand bent in from outside.[^c5-p2-6] The Moral Law shows the reverse: its moral court lists statements like "A should compensate more" as inputs that exceed the boundary, yet takes in the perpetrator's own "I should compensate Z."[^c5-ml4] So the first cut looks at where the demand comes from, not at whether the sentence says "I" or "you."

Series The load-bearing sentence Modality (first cut) Address sent to (self-description, as written) Disposition

Reason Cannot Not"To exit is to be degraded"Inside-out, ontological form, refuses the hypothetical"Clause IV (16DD cooperative imperative) still lacks complete unfolding; this is the next important paper position in SAE ethics."Revision queue (the cost of exit)

A few cells in the table need a further word.[^c5-chain]

In the Rights Theory series, one sentence must be read apart from Action Theory. On Accountability writes: "That exit does not release a duty means that the act of exiting cannot loose it, because by the termination lemma one cannot-not legislate only for oneself, and there is no exiting into not being the subject of that law."[^c5-racc] Action Theory says instead that one can leave in a given matter. The two sentences do not speak of the same thing. On Accountability says that, by A16's termination lemma, a subject cannot exit into not being the subject of that law; this layer governs the structure of self-bearing, not every particular content within the law — as the same text says, exit's inefficacy "read too widely goes wrong in the same way." It hangs on A16's stipulation; and the Introduction, Section 4, has shown that denying one has a self-given law is performatively consistent. Action Theory speaks of whether one stands in the position of treating people as ends, and that layer can be left matter by matter.

Value Theory. Value Theory has four items that hand questions of the "ought or ought not" kind elsewhere:

  1. P3 V38: "How a guardian ought to act, and what obligations it bears, this paper does not judge. That is the object of the practical critique."
  2. P3 Section VIII: "Whether a colonization that holds ought to be dissolved, who has standing to conduct an audit, what obligations a guardian network bears at a subject-to-be … They are the objects of the practical critique."
  3. P4 Section VII: five items, beginning with whether "the gaining of accessibility ought to be brought about": "Most belong to the critique of practice"; and those among them that involve one person judging another's case and claiming assent are "a matter for the critique of judgment."
  4. P5 Section Five: "Whether any of this ought to be so belongs to the second critique."[^c5-v4b]

These four are Value Theory's own thinking, recorded as written. Such incoming items do not automatically become this book's debts; this book does not settle them for Value Theory by the three routes of Value Theory Paper 0, Section Seven — discharge, public reassignment, public withdrawal. What falls within this book's main question, this book examines for its premises, connections and validity; examining whether it holds is not issuing to the reader the command it speaks of — this book issues no ought. Only the act of handing over is examined, in three questions.

First, what modality of question is handed over. Each item takes the first cut. To ask "how a guardian ought to act," to ask whether a colonization that holds ought to be dissolved, to ask whether something ought to be brought about, discouraged or recommended — if these are addressed to others, they are all lateral, and not discussed. The guardian network and who may conduct an audit belong to the group and institutional layer, and are entered as no address yet (Chapter Six, Section 7). The inside-out side, by this book's general reading of the inside-out ought (Rules of the House, Thirteen), falls within the own law of whoever has adopted — that is a reading, not receiving the item on Value Theory's behalf.

Second, whether the receiving address takes the item. "The practical critique" and "the second critique" are this book. This book examines only the handing over; it does not receive the items on Value Theory's behalf.

Third, whether, after handing over, Value Theory still draws on normative force on its own account. It does not, and it holds to this throughout: "this series draws on no normative force at the object level and cannot say what a person ought to do"; and the introductions to P4, P5 and P6 each state that normative questions are not drawn upon at any point.[^c5-v0-35]

Value Theory handed off "ought or ought not" four times, and only two rows of Action Theory caught any of it. Here one sees most clearly how, in a system that issues no ought, its questions of ought are pushed downstream all the way. The row-by-row comparison and the reconciliation of Value Theory Paper 0's forward assignments — "forward assignment" being Value Theory Paper 0's term for work one paper hands ahead to a later one — are all in Appendix II.[^c5-v29]

Meaning Theory. Meaning Theory hands off "ought" in two versions. One address: "ought to the Moral Law"; "Whatever involves ought, to the Moral Law." Three addresses: the introductions of P1 through P4 all write "wherever ought arises, it is routed to the Moral Law, Rights Theory, and Action Theory." Both versions occur within the single proposition P3 Y25: first it says "Which action, if any, is to be taken remains a matter for the Moral Law, Rights Theory, and Action Theory," and later "its ought half belongs to the Moral Law."[^c5-ym] Both versions are recorded side by side; neither is chosen.

Y25 has one more sentence: "Meaning Theory draws the conceptual boundary; the Moral Law issues the ought." Taken literally, this clashes with the Moral Law's own words. Moral Law Paper 0 says that "SAE moral law articulates no 'ought' for any subject"; the "ought" it declines is the kind "requiring the subject to obey some external rule or internal virtue" — it declines the external and the internal alike — and what it states is that "the structure cannot-not be such." Moral Law Paper 1 says as well: "Not 'ought.' 'Ought' is normative—morality externally requires the subject to do something."[^c5-ml0] This book reads each side by its own words: what Meaning Theory hands to the Moral Law is received there as cannot-not. The inside-out ought is cannot-not. That is this book's reading; it changes no word of Meaning Theory or of the Moral Law.

Action Theory. The abstract of Action Theory's Prequel writes: "Every ought routed here from upstream is repaid, without exception, in cannot-not and in what is open either way." Section 3 says it again and points to where the reason lies: "The part routed to this series is repaid, without exception, in cannot-not and in what is open either way; the series does not write ought. Why it writes this way, Paper 1, §4 says."[^c5-apq] Paper 1, §4 does not write even "I ought": "ought, as a way of speaking, is itself a command, issued to a person from outside; even when the one issuing the command is his own reason, the sentence still has the shape of a command".[^c5-a1-4] This goes further than this book: this book says only that the inside-out ought is cannot-not, while Action Theory does not use the word at all. The two point the same way.

The twenty-nine rows settled one by one in Section 5 of the Prequel are recorded as Action Theory's self-description: "The table below is the receiving side's own statement, not an acceptance made on upstream's behalf".[^c5-apq5] What came to this book's door: V-4 (reassigned to the Second and Third Critiques, undivided) and the second half of V-5; and Paper 0, §9.2, handed over "A cannot-not as a person's own choice" as well. All three are entered in Appendix II.

4 · Settlement

Two columns. What the First Critique sent to this book is settled here; what the framework's series sent is only recorded here, not settled.

What the First Critique sent: settled. The First Critique sent nine items. For each, where it went and why:

Item As sent Where it went How it is settled
OneThe practical cannot-not as a whole; the strict argument for the cost to one who stops; parting autonomy from obsessionIntroduction, Section 2, First; Chapter Two; this chapter, Section 2; Chapter Three, Section 6 (B)Delivered. The cost to one who stops: Chapter Two reads the cost, this chapter's Section 2 argues the standing; the three marks of autonomy are in Chapter Three
Two"Whether mutual recognition and mutual examination can grow into common legislation"Chapter SixDelivered at the individual layer; legislation by a community this book does not take up — handed over publicly, no address yet (Chapter Six, Section 7)
Three"whether one ought to advance"Chapter Two, Section 4, stopping and not stoppingDelivered: not advancing is a branch; whether to advance is a matter within a person's own law — a conclusion about the boundary, not a justification of every kind of advance

SevenCase FiveThis chapter, Section 2Delivered, with the reshaping stated; the second half of Methodology 00 not taken in
EightThe normative validity of cultivationThis chapter, Section 2The sentence form delivered, with the first case; the positive content still awaiting justification
NineThe two ways of speaking of 15DDThis chapter, Section 2Delivered; the three at separate addresses

The original dispatching sentences are recorded together in Appendix II.[^c5-fc9] The few things the First Critique sent to the critique of judgment — by what right judgment "may demand others' assent," "that is another critique's address" — this book does not take in.

What the series sent: recorded, not settled. This book reads from outside. What the examined texts themselves wrote of handing what to whom is recorded as written; whether it was handed rightly and received well is for those who read both sides to check. Meaning Theory had this measure early: "the settlement here is the receiving side's own statement, not an acceptance made on upstream's behalf; acceptance is made by whoever reads both papers."[^c5-m0-7] The incoming items are entered one by one in Appendix II, the Register of Incoming Items — Value Theory, Meaning Theory, Paper 6, Action Theory — each with its original sentence, the address it was sent to, the chapter of this book where it is examined, and what the examination found.

The three ledgers. The First Critique set up three ledgers: the corpus revision queue, the reading registry, the annex ledger.[^c5-fc-ledger] This book keeps them on, numbering in continuation of the First Critique (Colophon); it does not write back into the First Critique. The revision queue records only revision candidates that this book's examination turned up; whether to revise is up to each series, and this book does not press.

Revision queue, second batch (candidates, by series):

  1. One's Own Law: §3.1, "This is not a choice, not nobility — it is a mode of being"; §3.2, "you have no option of 'not opposing'"; §3.3, "unconditional"; the closing, "everyone should be their own philosopher-king".
  2. Reason Cannot Not: III.1, "To exit is to be degraded" and "reduced to a tool"; the drive vocabulary of Clauses II and III; Clause III, "the only legitimate outlet".
  3. Claims of derivation from the axiom: Moral Law Paper 1, §2.1, "All content in this series is deduced from this single axiom"; Rights Theory Paper 0's three versions of "all follow from it"; the War Treatise, "It is SAE's axiom itself — subjectivity cannot be eliminated, cultivation is the form in which subjectivity grows, cultivation cannot not continue" (§1, rendered from the Chinese page); Economics P6, "core axiom".
  4. Sentences that count people by level: Moral Law P0, "the 14DD-to-15DD ratio rises"; P6, "12DD constitutes the majority" (used alone at P6 §0.1, without the same paper's qualification); Economics P3, "no longer a cognitively available operation". Remedy: supply the index of operation within the sentence; do not delete the sentence.
  5. Final verdicts and naming: Moral Law P3, §5.3 and §5.4, the two places that read motive and warrant convergence; Moral Law P7, after the filtering, positively naming historical figures' "15DD moment".
  6. Unsourced validity: the Conflict Treatise, §§11 and 29, "an effect further upstream".
  7. Colonization defined "in a single sentence": Action Theory Paper 2, §2; Paper 6, §1.
  8. Older sentences of the kind found in Paper 2, "without integrity, the subject becomes a tool" — already self-registered by Paper 4's second edition; this book only places them alongside.
  9. To Forget the Self, §4.4, the closing sentences, "Standing by when the other's remainder is being suppressed is not 15DD … When non dubito stops running, it is no longer 15DD": one failure to respond rules one out of 15DD; Action Theory D35 has already lowered it.

Reading registry (from entry nine):

  • Entry nine: the First Critique's Introduction, "precisely the inner drive of SAE": the drive read as a condition (Introduction, Section 2, First).
  • Entry ten: the First Critique's On Genesis §3, "ethics — … the one-way acknowledgment," and Appendix I, the 15DD station, "Ethics — … one-way acknowledgment": read as non dubito (the Methodology Overview, V3.1).
  • Entry eleven: Value Theory Paper 0, Section Seven, One: the forward assignments it settles are confined to the four in the fifth slot; the four recorded as written.
  • Entry twelve: the word 承认 inside quotations (on the English pages, "recognition" or "acknowledge"): in the passages of Action Theory, Value Theory V14 and P6, and the Methodology Overview V3.1 §1.7 that speak of standing as an end or the posture of non dubito, it falls on the side of adoption; where an act takes another's will as its object, there is concession besides.
  • Entry thirteen: Meaning Theory's "the Moral Law issues the ought": received by the Moral Law as cannot-not.
  • Entry fourteen: Methodology 00's "structural 'cannot not'": the fact that the other cannot be taken in, on which it rests, is recorded as a structural judgment; to connect with concession it needs, besides, an act that takes the other's will as its object; its object, the end in itself, this book reads as adoption and does not take in as necessary.
  • Entry fifteen: On Accountability's "exit does not release a duty" and Action Theory's "can leave": the first governs the structure of self-bearing under A16's termination lemma, the second whether one stands in the position; read apart.
  • Entry sixteen: Action Theory P6, "the demand is that high": read as the strength of a definition, not a yardstick for measuring people (Action Theory P7 points the same way: "Writing it as a standard would bring back an ought pressed down from outside").

Annex ledger: Appendix III, the Formal Package of Practical Configurations (item-by-item verification of the configurations in Chapter Four, Section 3; the formal account of cannot-be-cancelled and can-leave, with its four outstanding items), not yet cleared; until it is, the independence claimed in Chapter Four stays at the level of candidates.

The first case is decided: Concession is unavoidable; adoption can be refused. Those who have adopted each have a law of their own; what happens when two laws that both treat people as ends meet is the business of the next chapter.

Chapter Six · On Overlap

1 · The Question: Cannot-Not and Universality

The question Action Theory handed over has one last part: "its relation to universality".[^c6-a0-92]

Kant makes universal legislation the form of autonomy, and the kingdom of ends the union of rational beings under common laws. At this point the First Critique withdrew one thing and kept another. Of the withdrawal it says: "withdrawing that test's exclusive validity is a redefinition of autonomy, not a mere loosening of content"; what it kept is the form: "Its universality is formal only: every subject cannot but keep the law it has itself laid down (that is the form of autonomy); the content of the law differs from subject to subject."[^c6-fc-univ]

The framework uses the word "universal" in more than one sense. Recorded as written:

  • One's Own Law says of Kant that "after legislating, he universalized it … Autonomy reverted to heteronomy";
  • Reason Cannot Not keeps "universalizability", and reads it as "the self-consistency that reason cannot not maintain when operating as reason";
  • Rights Theory says that Kant's kingdom of ends runs "on the fact that each member's self-legislation is already universal";
  • Action Theory "does not check maxims by a test of universalization".[^c6-univ]

Each of these points at something of its own, and this book does not unify them on their behalf. The question here is a single one: once the exclusive validity of universal law has been withdrawn, what is left between laws? What is left is overlap. Whether overlap alone is enough — whether it comes to no more than each to his own — Section 3 answers head-on.

2 · The Structure of Overlap

Secured by definition. When two people are both operating at 15DD, neither doubts that the other is an end. This coincidence is given by the definition of 15DD; it is analytically true and carries no new information (Chapter Four, derived region).

Formal only. The First Critique says: "the convergence is formal only. The subject-shape at the end — 'self-legislating end in itself' — is form; the content of the law does not converge: Laozi also arrives here in form — he self-legislates, and can stand in mutual recognition with other ends — yet his law is not to construct." Moral Law P0 says the same thing: "SAE consensus is posture convergence, not proposition convergence."[^c6-form]

Intermittent. Overlap holds only at the moments, and in the domains, where two people are both operating at 15DD. Levels describe operations, not persons. Moral Law P0 writes that "the same person, at different moments, can operate at 15DD, 14DD, or 12DD"; and Value Theory, when it speaks of resonance, also fixes the domain first: V77 is headed "For Two Bearers Each Able to Run Outward Recognition at the Fifteenth Row", and its Chinese heading adds "in that relational domain".[^c6-inter]

Not presupposed, only made out. Overlap cannot be presupposed of any particular person in advance. It can only be made out in an encounter, by each person, from the first person. One's Own Law: "Two 15DD subjects may each derive the same law, but this is not consensus, not sharing — it is each arriving independently. You cannot use your arrival to confirm another's, nor use another's to prop up your own." And: "'happening to be similar' is not the same as 'jointly held.'" The Conflict Treatise writes this out again and states the consequence: "between the two laws there is no position one could occupy to say that this one is truer than that."[^c6-arrive] Value Theory keeps the same measure on resonance: "The two may also say together that resonance occurred here, and that sentence may hold and may equally be a misjudgement. Neither party, nor any joint procedure, can raise it into a final verdict."[^c6-v77]

Not a premise of inference. To use overlap as a premise — you and I both treat people as ends, so you too ought to do such-and-such — is Chapter Three's false claim of the lateral. The First Critique has a name of its own for this shape, the false claim of cross-branch validity: "a law, without common objects, shared field, and cross-branch warrant, carries itself as universally valid."[^c6-fc-cross]

One shared name still has to be told apart. The First Critique's On Genesis also speaks of overlap. It says that "overlap has three sources", one of them "the shared premise of the acknowledgment layer — common to those who have walked to acknowledgment".[^c6-fc-ovl] There the overlap lies between roads, between philosophies; here it lies between laws, and the definition of 15DD secures it. The name is the same; the scope and the warrant differ.

3 · Why This Is Not Each to His Own

Withdraw the exclusive validity of universal law, and all that remains between laws is overlap. That sounds very like relativism: each has his own law, no one can say anyone is wrong, so anything goes. The objection has to be met head-on. If it held, this book's whole procedure would be nothing more than bookkeeping for a world in which anything goes.

Begin by putting the objection at its strongest. You allow no law to bind anyone who refuses it. You say that cost lies only on each person's own books and cannot be compared across persons. From outside, you cannot even rule that someone is operating at 15DD. Then a man whose law is to dominate others and a man who does not doubt that others are ends are, for you, simply two branches, with exactly the same entitlement to be registered.

This book answers in four layers.

First: overlap is not luck. While operating at 15DD, a person does not doubt that others are ends; when two people both operate so, the coincidence is given by definition and owes nothing to chance. However widely the contents of laws differ, one thing does not vary: there, no one requires another to prove first that he is an end. That one thing is not empty. The items Chapter Four recorded as ones that cannot be cancelled — not requiring others to produce their credentials first, not withdrawing the default because of what someone has done — cannot, inside the overlap, be cancelled for anyone. This is secured by definition and holds only at moments when two people are both at 15DD; it does not guarantee how many such moments there will be. The inference to be opposed here runs: contents do not converge, so nothing is held in common. Contents do not converge; and the one thing held in common in form is precisely the one that people have use for in dealing with one another.

Second: plural is not equal. An equal entitlement to registration does not make every branch equally good. The First Critique puts it exactly: "Plural is not equal: the standing to be registered is equal; validity is settled on each road's own account; cross-branch validity must still be earned."[^c6-plural] Every law pays its own cost, and the cost is not waived because others have laws too. A man whose law is domination still pays, on his own books, for every cut he makes. This book does not say "ought" to him, and his books are not cleared on that account. To refuse to count the branches equal is not yet to have a criterion of better and worse across branches; this layer answers "anything goes", not "who is better".

Third: there are readings from outside. Others cannot see a person's law. They can see whose road he has locked: sealing off another's ways to refuse, revise and exit, or, once interrogated, shutting the door on review of himself (Section 6). The first, once verified, can ground a ruling-out; the second yields a direction report. Both can be withdrawn, and neither is an absorbing state, but both are real readings. Where anything goes, there are no such readings. Here there are.

Fourth: domination that denies the capacity to say no is not a stable place to live. Domination in practice comes in two kinds, and this book does not say as much about the one as about the other. One kind denies that others can say no, while its acts never for a moment stop guarding against the one who can: fugitive slave laws, passes, the demand that bystanders join in not treating him as an end (Chapter Three, Section 4). This kind is intelligible, and inhabited, but no one lives in it stably: whoever lives there keeps two sets of books, one listing a thing, the other a person to be guarded against. The stamp that falls on this kind crosses branches (Section 5), because it rests not on this book's law but on his own acts. The other kind is far more candid: it concedes that you can say no, and simply does not treat you as an end. The stamp cannot reach it; it is a performatively consistent, inhabited branch. What this book can say of this kind is what the second and third layers say. It can say where the branch stands. If the branch locks in someone who can say no, Power Theory reads the entry it pays: it is bound first by that same lock — Power Theory calls this back-binding — and the ρ layer cannot be pressed to zero (Chapter Two, Section 4); this is a structural reading, and which item falls in the dominator's own law is said matter by matter. And the book can say what traces the branch leaves outside. This book stands opposite the branch in our name, on the ground of the non dubito we have adopted (Chapter Four, region of laws of this branch); that is our own undertaking, and it does not pose as a refutation of it.

Taken together, the four layers mark where this book parts from relativism. What this book refuses is not the existence of different laws but any claim's exemption, on that account, from examination by fact, by reasoning and by its own premises. It does not say "ought" to the refuser; nor does it say that anything goes. It can say where each law stands, what it pays and what traces it leaves outside. Of the one who dominates by denying that others can say no, it can say that his own books do not balance. Of the candid dominator, it can say where he stands and what entry Power Theory reads when he locks someone in, and it can say on what ground it stands opposite him. On who is wrong, this book gives way one step to him; on what may be exempt from examination, not one step. It cannot say he is wrong: this book has no criterion of better and worse across branches, and the fact that we stand opposite him does not supply one. Still less does anything it can say give it the entitlement to legislate for him.

Not being able to say he is wrong, and not legislating for him, is the cost this book pays, and it is not hidden. The refuser is not bound by this book's law. The harm he does to others cannot be stopped within the overlap; it has to be stopped by something beyond it, and the next section says what. Kant pays a different cost. His law binds the refuser, and the cost is that there must be a law able to bind every rational being, and a starting point laid down for it that asks for no deduction and establishes itself. Two costs, each on its own books.

4 · Beyond the Overlap

One boundary. Each person's own law governs that person, inside the overlap and outside it alike; what the overlap governs is the one thing two people hold in common. The part the overlap cannot reach and the refuser's own law will not stop — the harm he does to others — is borne by external constructs.

This book has found no sentence in the corpus that says this. It is an architectural claim this book assembles from several places, and so it must carry its grounds, its opponent and its cost. The grounds are four:

  • One's Own Law: "Law is external construct. The codification of operating rules within a civilization at a given DD level. It does not claim to be morality; it simply says: these are the rules, and violating them has consequences."
  • Law series P1: "15DD meets 14DD: the 15DD understands law but does not need law to constrain itself. It observes law not because it is constrained but because the other needs law. For a 15DD, law is a floor, not a ceiling."
  • The Conflict Treatise: "Law saying 'you may not treat her as a means' is the externally enforced version of the ontological imperative: it marks off a forbidden zone, and violation has consequences. It is the substitute and the scaffolding for when the 'we' is not present; it is not the 'we' itself." It also draws the line of what is ceded: "What is ceded is the procedural power of adjudication over disputes about action, not the constitutive power over one's own law."
  • Rights Theory P2 separates two modes of handling: verification, at 15DD and without a center; and the court, which obtains enforceability. The switch from verification to the court is made "not because the court is higher than verification".[^c6-outside]

The abstract of the Conflict Treatise restates a sentence of Law series P1: "at 15DD a subject already has its own law and external law is no longer a necessity". Law series P1's own words are: "At 15DD, external law is no longer necessary."[^c6-law] The sentence says that a person at 15DD needs no external law to bind himself; it does not say directly who takes charge of what lies beyond the overlap. Here it therefore serves as corroboration only, not as a ground.

Opponent. Outside the framework: Kant. A universal law binds at once those who accept it and those who refuse it. Moral Law P0 states the difference between the two sides most plainly: "universal law demands obedience from all rational beings—SAE consensus demands obedience from no subject"; "the binding source of universal law is reason—the binding source of SAE consensus is cannot-not … and cannot-not is binding only on subjects already operating at 15DD."[^c6-ml05] Inside the framework, the Moral Law's moral court is easily read as an opponent, but it does not oppose this boundary. Moral Law P0 says that "the moral court does not adjudicate guilt; the legislative subject has already judged itself", and takes as its own task of articulation "how a community independent of external authority is structurally naturally possible";[^c6-court] P4 sets its own limit: "Paper 4 articulates only the dispute-handling architecture for the pure 15DD community." The moral court operates among people at 15DD and does not reach the refuser. What it blocks is a different reading: that even inside the overlap, external constructs are needed.

At one point the two sides in fact run the same way. Action Theory summarizes Kant's point: "a rational being, even if he keeps the maxim himself, cannot count on others keeping it likewise", and yet the law "remains in full force"; Action Theory itself says that external conditions change how a person can act and "do not reset the direction and principles of his action".[^c6-kant-rk] A person's law does not take effect on condition that others keep it too. The disagreement lies elsewhere, over the refuser: Kant's law still binds the one who refuses it; the law here does not.

Cost. Constraint beyond the overlap is no longer a matter internal to any one law; institutions must set it up separately. There are disputes inside the overlap too: two people who do not doubt each other will still dispute over resources and procedure. A procedure such as the moral court handles them inside the overlap, and it is not an external construct; external constructs, for their part, can also serve an overlap already there. Nor does the absence of overlap mean that everything must be institutionalized: refusal, non-cooperation, leaving — many matters end there. To hand something to a construct is not to have proved that the construct is legitimate or effective, still less to guarantee that it will not turn round and dominate people. This book does not develop a theory of institutions; it only states where that work goes: the Theory of Institutions and the Theory of Government, and perhaps another critique in the future. There are two reasons. This book examines by what a person's law binds the one who made it, and an institution is not a person's law. And Action Theory has already handed "the institutional side of guarding", and the question "who calls power to account", over to the Theory of Government.[^c6-gov] Where this book does make use of institutions — the fugitive slave laws and passes on the ladder of precautions, for example — it treats them only as traces and evidence, and builds no theory of institutions on them.

When two laws collide. Two already-formed laws that require incompatible actions in the same matter are what the whole Conflict Treatise deals with: "Two independently formed laws, each its bearer's own and each already formed, require actions that cannot be jointly realized in the same situation, and three things hold at once: they cannot certify each other's arrival, neither may legislate for the other, and no single party's withdrawal can complete the settlement."[^c6-conflict] How that treatise proceeds, this book does not redo.

5 · Cross-Branch Validity, and the Procedure Between Two People

How cross-branch validity is obtained. The First Critique left this question: "a law that claims cross-branch validity must separately establish common objects, a shared field, and cross-branch warrant — whether it has the standing to claim so, and how that standing is obtained, is the practical critique's docket". It also states: "a verdict without cross-branch warrant does not take cross-branch effect automatically".[^c6-fc-b4] This book's answer: a law is entitled to make the claim; the validity it claims has to be earned. The First Critique said so long ago: "ambition is no disease; exempted ambition is."[^c6-fc-amb]

The first example ready to hand is the stamp of performative inconsistency, stamped on the kind of position that denies others can say no. It takes the other's own acts as its evidence, crosses branches by way of the in-domain zero point, and does not require the other to accept this book's law first. This fits exactly the footing the First Critique found for cross-branch mutual examination: "the shared zero point, the common field, and auditing the other by the other's own ledger — examination crosses the line".[^c6-fc-foot] The zero point is shared within its domain; an act aimed at another's will takes place in a common field from the start; and what is audited is the other's own ledger. What crosses must be stated exactly. It is a diagnosis, not a law, and a warranted diagnosis at that: one and the same person, over one and the same stretch of time, toward one and the same person, draws on that capacity in his acts while denying it in the same sense — and the proof must still be delivered case by case. The stamp says that his acts and his denial do not match; it does not say what he ought to do. Once across the line, it brings no measures with it.

The procedure between two people. What can two laws have in common? On the side of law there are sentences ready to hand. The Conflict Treatise: "Consensus does not automatically generate a third first-person legislator." As for the rule the two then hold in common: "that rule's binding force must pass separately through each party's own law, or come from a procedure both have acknowledged. It is not a third law above the two."[^c6-c24] Action Theory goes on: "This is a limited shared procedure: what it governs is how this one matter is ordered, and its binding force comes from both of them having accepted this way of doing things beforehand; it does not thereby become a position that legislates their own law for the two of them". It also sets down two propositions: "it is doing something together, not a shared law", and "institutions can let people find each other, but cannot produce a 'we' on behalf of both".[^c6-a8] Rights Theory's verification has the same shape: "It does not judge who wins or loses; it verifies whether the parties still recognize one another as ends from the 15DD position."[^c6-r2-4]

On the side of value there are two sentences of the same form; they serve as corroboration only. Value Theory heads V71 "A Joint Decision Does Not Generate a Third Position of Value Adjudication", and of joint audit it says: "What comes out is two first-person statements each party stands behind, together with a record of relational conditions checked in common."[^c6-v71] Value Theory itself says: "Why each of them is bound is settled separately by consent, by promise, by the moral law, by rights and by institutions"; and the Conflict Treatise states that the value side's limits, on the side of law, "are not re-established here and are not borrowed as premises of derivation".[^c6-c1] So they only share a form with the law side, and are not grounds here.

Can "mutual recognition and mutual examination" grow into common legislation? The First Critique asks: "Whether mutual recognition and mutual examination can grow into common legislation, rather than stopping at recognition, is left to the practical critique."[^c6-fc-b2] This book answers only at the level of the individual. What two people agree on is a shared procedure, a thing done together, not a shared law; no third legislating position arises from it. The two may each consent to the same arrangement without a third first person arising from that. Action Theory draws the line of the individual half clearly: "Guarding comes back to the individual: not handing over one's judgment under 'we decided together'".[^c6-a9] Beyond the level of the individual — whether a community can act as a legislator — this book does not take the question up; it hands it over publicly, with no address yet (Section 7). The second half of the First Critique's sentence, "the bearer may be a person, or a community judging jointly by public procedure", is taken up there.

6 · Ruling-Out and Reputation

Ruling-out. From outside, one can rule that a person is not operating at 15DD at this moment; one cannot rule that he is. A ruling-out needs something solid: some necessary condition of this operation, together with its not being met, must have been independently verified — that he has sealed off the other's ways to say no, to correct, to exit, for instance. The necessary conditions this book uses for ruling out are drawn from the definition of 15DD and the content of adoption: not requiring the other to prove first that he is an end; not withdrawing the default because of conduct; not sealing off the other's ways to say no, to correct, to exit (Action Theory D35). The requirement of a necessary condition is this book's own procedure (Chapter Four, self-registration); the places below only keep the same measure and are not its source. Seeing nothing more than general traces of risk yields a direction report, not a ruling-out. Both can be withdrawn; neither is an absorbing state. The framework keeps the same measure elsewhere:

  • Value Theory: "Audit reads only the opening and closing of positions; it does not judge what the bearer really avows"; "What can be said is the direction of change … What cannot be said is where it has got to"; "Being unable to judge is itself a legitimate conclusion."
  • Action Theory: "These are clues, not certification"; "Those not found are ends all the same."
  • The Conflict Treatise: "no test is given anywhere in this treatise for determining which layer the other is running on."[^c6-neg]

The Conflict Treatise sorts what can be read from outside into three tiers: "There are cues; there is no closure-guaranteeing final criterion; and there are traces visible from outside." None of the three may pass for another: "cues are not criteria, and traces are not verdicts." It gives one trace as the clearest: "Declaring a stopping point while closing every channel of review is a strong risk signal that the stopping point may only be stalling. It is enough to lower substantially the public credibility of the declaration; it is not enough to complete the internal classification for the subject."[^c6-three] Traces of this kind yield a direction report. A ruling-out needs one thing more: that some necessary condition, together with its not being met, has been independently verified.

Reputation: only the individual half is kept. Once a person, by his own law, has ruled another out, what can he do? The corpus's sentences on the individual side:

  • Non-cooperation and exit. Action Theory: "After that, wherever he could avoid working with this colleague, he did not work with him, and wherever he could avoid dealing with him, he did not deal with him." D41: "Invisibility and exit are not a judgment that the other is unworthy: no longer lending one's strength, no longer keeping in touch, coexist with recognizing that he is a person." Moral Law P6 puts it more bluntly: "Exit = pure physical communication-link silencing (physical cut-off) — not ontological judgment".
  • Opposition. One's Own Law: "But you can oppose. Opposition is your own remainder unfolding, your own 'cannot not,' not legislation upon them."
  • Discernment lies with the individual. Moral Law P3: "The 15DD legislative subject has primary discernment, and the community's observation is secondary"; "The community's discernment is the aggregate of individual discernments."[^c6-rep]

Each of these has an edge that whoever stands here cannot himself cancel: not sealing off the other's way to say no (Action Theory D35). Action Theory's phrase "not add a cost to it" says the same thing — "if the new hire says no, he truly accepts the no, and does not give him any less afterward because of it"[^c6-a6] — and the "cost" there is, by this book's division, a consequence (Chapter Two, Section 5). To exit is to stop lending one's own strength. To add consequences is to set a penalty on his no, and what it seals off is exactly that way to say no.

Value Theory's granting and withdrawing lies on the other side: a community "may confer recognition and may withdraw it", and "What is removed is social position and support, not this bearer's own value content."[^c6-v22] That is a phenomenon of the community. Reputation as a phenomenon of the community belongs to Moral Law P3 and P4 themselves (Section 7).

A few of the Moral Law's sentences on reputation go beyond the measure kept here, and this book records how it reads them. It says that reputation's "recognition state" is "the on/off character of the recognition structure as it displays in the field", and speaks of "observing how A treats B to assess A's DD level"; yet P0 of the same series says: "The community does not evaluate the individual".[^c6-ml-rep] This book reads by that sentence of P0 and by the discipline of Chapter Three: reputation is an aggregate of individual readings; what it reads is a direction, not a switch; levels record operations, not persons. The Moral Law itself also keeps this way open: "when the Self returns to 15DD operation the community can re-recognize".

One incoming item, cut in two. Value Theory handed "those that involve one person judging another's case and claiming assent" to the critique of judgment; the Action Theory Prequel reassigned the item to "The Second and Third Critiques"; and Paper 0, §9.3, adds: "what is handed over here is the side of it that belongs to the critique of judgment."[^c6-v4] The object of V-4 belongs entirely to the critique of judgment; the original does not say what the Prequel's added address, the Second Critique, refers to. This book reads it as the side of acting on one's own judgment — and the cut is this book's:

  • acting on one's own judgment of another — non-cooperation, exit, opposition, keeping one's distance — is the practical half, and it belongs to this section;
  • requiring the other to assent to that judgment is the half that belongs to judgment, and it goes to the critique of judgment.

At the level of the individual, the second half too has an edge ready to hand. Action Theory: "Consensus rests on each of the two people's own acknowledgment. So neither side can declare agreement in the other's place."[^c6-a8-3]

7 · Blank Cells and Reassignments

Institutions. Publicly reassigned to the Theory of Institutions and the Theory of Government; for the reasons, see Section 4.

Dispatches with no address, gathered in. In several places the earlier chapters mark work as handed on to what comes later. Here each one is given an address. The address is the one the text under examination itself points to; where it points to none, the entry reads no address yet.

Source What is handed over Address
Chapter Five, Section 2, the individual and the groupThe community as bearerNo address yet
Chapter Five, Section 4, second item; this chapter, Section 5Community legislation: whether "mutual recognition and mutual examination can grow into common legislation"No address yet
Chapter Five, Section 3, the Rights Theory branchThe two modes of handling the dispute over whether a constraint "ought to be enforced"Rights Theory P2, F9 (the court and verification)
Chapter Five, Section 3, the Value Theory branchThe network of guardianship; who is entitled to auditNo address yet
This chapter, Section 6Reputation as a phenomenon of the communityMoral Law P3, P4
Appendix I, the Rights Theory P5 rowThe dispute over whether a constraint "ought to be enforced"Rights Theory P2, F9
Appendix I, the War Treatise rowThe group-level "should"No address yet

The historical layer of persons and groups. This book has no cell for it, and proposes linking it to the question opened by the third item of Paper 6, §7: "Third, the temporal dimension of institutions. The five propositions are structural … and do not address the evolutionary dynamics of institutions—how do institutions evolve over time?"[^c6-p6-7] That item, as written, asks how institutions evolve. To hang the historical layer of persons and groups on it as well is this book's proposal; the original paper has not taken up that part of the work. This book keeps one thing only: the warrant of a historical claim must be stated, and Chapter Three's case of the false claim of convergence examines it in form.

The boundary of bearers. Left as a blank cell; no line is drawn. Only three things have been written down: the range of the first case and its three prohibitions (Chapter Five, Section 2); Value Theory's reserve — "This belongs to the bearer's boundary-conditions — whether the standing to bear ceases with it — and this paper does not adjudicate it, only marks it as held in reserve, matching Rights Theory P2's reserve at the same place"; and Meaning Theory's symmetry — "The subject's present occurring directly refutes the claim that its position has already finally exited; no one else can perform that self-attestation in its stead. Nor may a criterion of departure simply stand in for a judgment of final exit. This paper draws no conclusion either way about the correspondence, and leaves both sides open symmetrically, neither sealing the question nor opening it on anyone's behalf".[^c6-border]

Non-human systems belong in this cell too. Paper 4: "on what its standing as an end would rest is not adjudicated here. This does not close that question; it keeps a single capacity test from closing it in advance." Value Theory gives only the infant as its example of the subject-to-be, and does not write about AI. Beyond what Paper 4's Corollary One has already determined, this book does not rule either.

The critique of judgment. For the following, this book records only where they go. The First Critique asks of the power of judgment "by what right it may demand others' assent", and says: "that is another critique's address". The First Critique's Appendix IV records two borrowings — "the corpus borrows the third Critique's purposiveness formula, this book its doctrine of judgment" — and "when the critique of judgment convenes, the two borrowings are heard as one docket". Then there is the item Value Theory P4, Section VII, hands to the critique of judgment; the half that belongs to judgment, cut off in the previous section; and the side of "purposiveness without purpose" that Chapter Five mentions.[^c6-judg]

8 · The Close

Between laws, overlap without unification; rule out, never rule in; state the cost, give no command.

Every entry this book lays down stands under interrogation as well. Its law does not take effect on the reader by being read. The reader reads it by his own law. A clause that fits takes effect in his law only once he himself has undertaken it; resemblance or overlap does not undertake it for him. Where the book does not reach him, he goes another way by his own law, and this book registers that branch, with its conditions and its cost. Moral Law P0 has a sentence close to this: "The author articulates their own posture, allowing the reader to see; the reader as independent legislative subject articulates their own version; substitution is welcomed."[^c6-ml02]

Appendix I · The Chain of Oughts, Series by Series

The appendix goes series by series, one table to each. The six columns mean:

  • Original sentence: verbatim, cited to paper and section.
  • Modality: the first cut comes first — lateral, or inside-out? The lateral this book does not discuss; the inside-out is read as cannot-not and then graded on the table in Introduction, Section 2, Third.
  • Source of warrant: postulate; definition; derived from a postulate; claimed as derived; or unrecorded.
  • Sent to: the series' own statement of where the matter goes, recorded as written; where one series says it in several versions, all are recorded side by side and none is chosen.
  • Propagation: whether a verdict of the First Critique reaches the sentence. For a paper earlier than the First Critique, whether it reaches is judged by the general form of those verdicts; a later paper is outside the propagation batch. Earlier and later go by the date of first publication recorded on the site; where a paper was revised afterwards, which version the quoted sentence stands in is checked separately. Words in quotation marks in this column are the First Critique's verdicts.
  • Disposition: recorded as written; reading registry (numbered as in Chapter Five, Section 4); revision queue (second batch, numbered as in Chapter Five, Section 4); or incoming item (Appendix II).
